The Dust Reckoning
A Novel
---
Chapter 1: The Third Burial

The day had come up clear, and for that she would always mark it the worse. It was the fourteenth of April, a Sunday, and the light lay over the farm like water in a pan, white and still and without a single cloud to warn them. June Vogel stood at the kitchen door in the middle of the morning and read the sky the way she read everything, the way she read her ledger, column by column, and the sky said nothing. The wind was out of the southwest, warm and dry, and it had been blowing out of the southwest for as long as any of them could remember, which was the trouble with that country. A thing that never stops, you stop noticing. The wind had been blowing for ten years and she could not have said when it began to be a wind that had an appetite in it.

At noon they sat down to dinner. Walter at the head of the table with the window at his back, June at the far end with the stove and the door, Cora on the side by the window, Sammy across from her. Four plates. The fifth chair was pushed in flush against the table, its back to the wall, the way it had been pushed in on the morning of the day Ezra died, and no one had moved it since. It was not a rule anyone had spoken. It was a rule the house kept by itself, the way it kept the crack in the ceiling over the stove and the place on the porch where the boards had sprung. Ezra's chair held its space like a body would have held it. They set no plate. They did not look at it. The chair sat as full as if there were a meal in front of it, and the meal was the silence that came down over the table whenever one of them started to say his name.

Cora ate fast, the way she did everything. She was seventeen and all of it was aimed at the door. Her hands moved even when she was still; even now, with the fork and the salt and the bread in reach, she was talking about the county agent's talk at the feed store, about the boy who had gone off with the harvest trucks in April, about the price of anything, and she talked prices the way other girls talked songs, fast and bright, as if the numbers were a tune she was learning to carry. June let her talk. June was good at letting people talk.

Sammy ate quiet. Ten years old, and he ate the way a man eats when he is hungry and thinking about other things. He had been eight when Ezra died, and there was a carefulness in him now, a way of setting his spoon down flat, a way of not asking for things, that was old in a boy and would not be unlearned. He had his father's hands, wide across the knuckles. June watched them come and go over the plate, and she did not say anything, because there was nothing to say about a thing that was only true.

Walter ate and watched the window. He was forty-six and the land had put a crease in him the way it put a crease in everything, in the fence line and the house and the road. He had been a tall man once. Now he was a tall man carrying something. He had been carrying it a long time, and it had begun to round his shoulders, and he never set it down, not even at the table, not even on a Sunday.

The chicken was thin. There were four potatoes. The water in the glasses was warm and tasted of the cistern, of iron and of the dry. June had drawn it and no one mentioned what it was made of, made of a country that had not rained, made of dust, made of the year 1935, the third bad year running, and no one at that table needed to be told what was in the glass.

It was a little after noon, the sun white overhead, when the light changed.

June noticed it first because she was facing the door. The white light out the kitchen door went yellow, went the color of a bruise going bad, the color of a lamp flame seen through smoke, and then it went out of the room altogether, and the kitchen went dark the way a kitchen goes dark at dusk, only it was noon, only there was no dusk, only the air had begun to hum, a low steady hum like a wire strung too tight across the whole sky.

Walter put his fork down. "It's the storm," he said. He said it the way a man says the name of something he has been waiting on a long time, with relief and with dread in the same word.

They moved. That was the thing about a farm family. They moved as one animal with four bodies, no one giving the word because the word had already been given. Cora was at the windows with the quilts. Sammy went for the kerosene and the lamps. June had the dishpan full of water from the cistern before the first gust took the side of the house, and she tore the old sheets into strips, she kept a stack of them by the cistern for this, the way other houses kept a stack of kindling, and they went around the house, all four of them, jamming the wet cloths into the cracks of the windows and the frames of the doors, the wet cloths at the door, the wet cloths over the sills, while the light went on dying outside and the hum went on rising into something that had weight in it.

The wind hit. It hit the house like a thing with shoulders, and the house shuddered, and the roof took it, and the walls held, the walls had been holding for fifteen years and they held, and then the dark came.

It was not the dark of night. Night has edges; you can feel the edges of it, the way you can feel the walls and the windows. This was a dark that had volume, a dark that pressed on the glass, a dark that breathed. The lamps were lit but the room stayed grey with it, and the air began to fill. You could see it, the way dust comes off a dirt road in the wake of a truck, only this dust was coming through the walls, through the wet cloths, through cracks that were not there. The dust came into the house like a dog that had always lived there, settled into the corners as if it owned them, lay down in the seams of the floorboards and the folds of the curtains and the grain of the table, and was not going anywhere.

Sammy had his face in his arms. June could see his shoulders. She went and stood by him and put her hand on his head, and he did not lift his face, and she did not ask him to. The lamp flame guttered. On the table the four glasses had gone from water to something else; the grit had found them.

Walter stood at the window, looking out into nothing. She could see his hands on the sill, and they were white at the knuckles, and when he spoke his voice was not loud. The storm was loud enough for all of them, and no one raised a voice against it.

"This is the third time," Walter said. "This is the third time this place has been buried since the rains stopped, and I am calling it what it is. It is a grave. We are living on the lid of a grave, June, and I am tired of setting a table on it."

The words lay on the table between them like a cold dish. Nobody took them up. Cora's eyes moved to her mother's face, watching to see what she would do with them, and June saw that too, saw the whole of the room waiting on her, and she did what she always did with a word she would not take up: she put her hands to work. She went to the window nearest her and pressed the wet cloth hard into the frame, and the cloth had gone brown with grit, and she turned it and pressed it again, and the gesture said all she meant it to say. The storm was the storm. There would be a settling with it, and there would be a settling with the words, but it would not be tonight and it would not be in the dark.

She did not answer him. She was counting. The arithmetic was the thing she could hold onto when the dark pressed in, the arithmetic that lived in the ledger on the shelf by the stove, the column for weather, the column for what was planted, the column for what came up. Nineteen thirty: the rains stopped. Nineteen thirty-one: the creek went dry and the stock went thin. Nineteen thirty-two: the grasshoppers, and after the grasshoppers the wind. Nineteen thirty-three: the first burial, the storm that took the top of the garden and the neighbor's barn and laid a smooth grey tongue of dust over the north quarter fence line like a tide going out and forgetting to come back. Nineteen thirty-four: the second, the month after they put Ezra in the ground, the wind had come again and laid its dust over the fresh sod, and she had stood at the grave with the wind taking her skirts and had not been able to tell, for a long moment, where the new grass ended and the dust began. And now this. The third. She counted them the way she counted seed corn, one by one, in her head, against the window, and the count came out to three, and three was a number with a shape to it, and she held the shape in her chest like a stone.

Cora was at the door. She had her hand on the latch, and June saw her, saw the set of her shoulders, the way she leaned into the wind as if she wanted to lean through it. Cora Vogel, who would argue with a fence post, who had never once in her life stayed where she was put. The storm was the biggest thing that had ever come to this country, and Cora was trying to see it. She was trying to get out into it.

"Get away from the door, Cora." June's voice was flat. It was the voice she used for the chickens and the children and the land, the same flat voice, and it was the voice that held.

Cora did not move. "I want to see it."

"It's nothing to see."

"It's everything. It's the whole sky."

"It's the whole sky gone wrong, and you will not be in it."

Cora turned. Even in the grey lamplight June could see her face, could see the wanting in it, and she understood, with the part of her that understood things before they happened, that her daughter was hungry in a way no amount of food would touch. That the storm that was burying their land was the storm Cora wanted to walk into. That this country was dying, and Cora was seventeen, and seventeen was not made for dying countries.

"Come help me wet the cloths," June said. "The east windows are letting it through."

And Cora came. She came because June asked her to work, which was the only language between them that never failed, and she took the wet cloth from her mother's hand and went to the east window, and she pushed the cloth into the crack with a violence that was almost the wind's own violence, and for a while there was peace in the room, of a kind, the peace of four people doing the same thing in the same dark.

The storm lasted. June never knew for how long. An hour. Two. Three. She knew it by the sound and by the dark, and she knew it by Cora, who had gone quiet at last, who sat now on the floor by the stove with her back to the wall and her arms around her knees, not speaking, not reading, not arguing, just listening with the whole of her to the great machinery of the sky. June watched her and thought, without saying it, that this was the first thing in a year her daughter had looked at without wanting to change it.

The dust came in even so. No cloth was thick enough. It moved through the room like a slow tide and lay down on everything with the patience of water, on the back of the stove, on the ledgers, on the canning jars on the high shelf, where it coated the glass like fine grey moss. Sammy coughed once, and June's whole body went tight, and he coughed again, a dry hooking cough, and she crossed the room and put her hand flat against his back and held it there, and under her palm she could feel the small shudder of his lungs, and she stood that way a long time, not breathing, until the coughing stopped and his chest went quiet under her hand, and only then did she let the breath out of her. Time in the dark room was measured by the lamp flame and by the grit accumulating on the cloths and by the sound, which went from a roar to a scream to a low endless howl like something grieving, and which finally, without anyone noticing the exact moment, began to thin. The howl loosened. The dark at the windows went from black to brown to a pale sick yellow, the color of the bottom of a jar that had held something old. The wind let go of the house the way a hand lets go of a wrist, slowly, finger by finger.

No one moved. They stood in the grey-yellow light, four of them, scattered across the room where the storm had left them, and the dust in the air hung fine and soft and was still coming down, settling on the table, on the lamp chimney, on the faces of the glasses, on Ezra's chair, which held a film of it like a shroud.

Walter went to the door and opened it, and the light that came in was brown and thick, and the world that stood beyond the door was not the world that had stood there at noon.

It was flat. It was smooth. The fence line that had run along the north quarter at noon was gone, the fence line, the gate, the corner post, all of it, under a grey smooth tongue of dust that lay over the pasture like water over a drowned field, like a sheet pulled over something. The garden was gone. The chicken house stood to its eaves in it. The road, where the road had been there was no road, only the level grey, only the country remade, only the land lying flat and still and finished, the way land lies in a grave that has been dug and filled and stamped down.

The wind had died. The dust was still falling, fine as flour, out of a sky the color of a dirty nickel.

Behind her she heard Walter's feet on the porch boards. She heard him say something, low, that the wind had taken. She did not ask what.

They stood at the door, the four of them, and looked at the third burial. The land lay smooth and grey and level as far as the eye could reach in the brown light, and above it the dust went on falling, fine as flour, out of a sky the color of a dirty nickel, and it fell on the roof and the fence posts and on Ezra's chair and on the four of them standing in the door of the house like the last four things standing in the world.

Chapter 2: June's Ledger

The dust was still coming down when she went for the ledger. It came down out of a sky the color of a dirty nickel, fine as flour, and it did not so much fall as hang, and the air for the rest of that day was a grey drift you walked through. June put on her coat over her housedress and went out to the well and drew water, and the bucket came up heavy with silt, and she set it by the door to settle and went back in.

The ledger lived on the shelf by the stove, between the oil lamp and the stack of clean jars, wrapped in a piece of oilcloth the color of a flour sack, and she lifted it down the way she lifted down a dish, careful, and she carried it to the table, and she did not open it for a long moment. The table was the same table. The dust was on it. She had wiped it once already, and it was on it again, a grey film that came through the wet cloth the way it had come through the walls. She wiped it a third time, and a third time it gathered, and she left it, because there was no finishing it, only holding it off, and she put the ledger down in the middle of the clearing she had made and unwrapped the oilcloth and opened it.

The book was a composition book, the kind the school sold for a nickel in September, black cover, marble paper gone soft at the corners. It had been new in 1919, the year after the wedding, the year she and Walter had taken up this quarter section on the flood plain of the Cimarron with a team and a plow and a tent and not much else, and she had bought it at the feed store in Boise City on a day so hot the road had been white with it, and she had written on the first page, in the careful round hand she had learned from the district school the year the district school had a teacher who stayed a whole winter:

June Vogel, her book. May 1919. The Lord planted a garden, and he gave it rain.

She had not meant it as scripture. It was how she thought, how she had always thought, in the cadence of the plain-church prose she had heard since she could remember, the sentences that stood up straight and told you what was true and expected nothing back. The ledger was her real religion, and she knew it, and she did not repent of it. The preacher spoke of a God who kept count; the ledger was how she kept count back. Every seed bought, every seed saved, every seed planted, every seed lost, and beside them a column for the weather, and it was the weather column that was the long column now, the column that ran on and on with the same word and its little variations, and the word was dry.

She turned the pages slowly. 1919: beans, corn, potatoes, the first garden in the good dark soil of the flood plain, and beside it the word wet, the word rain, the words "the river came up and watered the bottom." The Cimarron had been a river then. It had run brown and lively through the spring, and the bottom land had drunk it, and the corn had come in so tall you could walk into it and be lost, and she had written it down the way a woman writes down the births of her children, careful, grateful, in ink that did not fade.

1920. 1921. The wheat had done well, and the garden, and the stock. The page for 1922 held a pressed stalk of something, gone pale, and the word rain. 1923, the word rain, and she had written it now without looking up at it, she knew them all, she could have recited the weather column from memory the way a preacher recites the genealogies, all those years of rain, all those years of water, and then the column had begun to change.

1930. She turned to it slowly. The word was still rain, the last good rain she would write down for a long time, and under it she had written the tallies in her careful hand. And after 1930 the column had narrowed and begun to repeat, and she had watched it the way you watch a well going down, week by week, one more bucket, and the word she wrote had become the same word, and she had watched her own hand write it.

1931. Dry. The creek went dry in June. The corn came up thin and stopped.

1932. Dry. The grasshoppers came on the wind from the south and ate the garden in two days, and the wind came after them and took what they left, and she had written that, too, in the ledger, the plain words, and there had been no sermon in them, only the count.

1933. Dry. The first burial. The storm had come out of the north in the spring and buried the fence line on the north quarter under a grey tongue of dust, and they had dug it out, and the wind had come again and buried it again, and she had written, in the weather column, dry, and in the margin, once, the words she did not usually set down: the land forgets what grew here.

1934. Dry. And in the spring of 1934, Ezra. The fever, the dust in his lungs, the doctor from Boise City who had come in his Ford and stood at the bed and said the words a doctor says. She had buried her son in the ground of the north quarter, in the row where the new trees would have been, and she had written it in the ledger. She had written it the way she wrote everything, in the column for lives, because the ledger had a column for lives, had always had one, you could not grow a family and not keep the count, and the entry was three words and the date. She had not written more. She had not written the fever or the doctor or the sound of the wind in the nights after, because some things the count itself carried, and a column that ran Ezra, b. 1926, d. 1934, spring was a column that did not need her to explain it.

She sat with the ledger open at that page a long time. The dust came down through the air of the room and settled on the paper, and she did not wipe it away. It lay there on the page for Ezra, on the thin place in the paper where her hand had paused, and it seemed to her, sitting in the grey room, that the dust and the page had a kind of truce, the dust covering what the page remembered, the page remembering under the dust, and that between them they held the whole of the thing, the way a grave holds what it holds and lets the grass tell it.

Dust on the page, and the page remembered rain. She turned a page and ran her finger along the margin where, in 1931, she had begun to keep a second tally, a list of what the weather had taken, and the list was not long, but it was entire. The garden. The wheat. The south well. The fence line. The corn. The creek. The barn roof in the first storm. She read the list the way she would have read a list of what a flood had carried, and she thought how a flood at least gave the water back to the river, and the dust gave nothing back at all. The dust was the country forgetting, and the ledger existed because the dust forgot, because somebody had to remember, because if nobody kept the count then the count was lost, and a lost count was a lost life, and she would not lose the count.

She found the saved seed corn. It was in a jar on the high shelf, the jar she had wrapped and set at the back against the wall, and she lifted it down and set it on the table beside the ledger, and she uncorked it, and she poured the corn out onto the table in a pile, and it was not a big pile. It was two seasons of saving, and it was not a big pile. She had kept it the way her mother had kept it, the way her grandmother had kept it before her, the best ears, the fullest kernels, dried and shelled and held against the year when the land would want seed again, and this was the whole of it, and she looked at the pile, at the gold of it against the grey film of the table, and she began, with the old patience, to count it.

She counted by the dozen, into a jar lid, the way her mother had counted, the way you count seed corn when you have decided the arithmetic yourself. The count came out. She did not write it down, because it was burned into her the way the weather column was burned into her.

The seed corn was only one line of the ledger's account, and she knew it. She went through the house in her mind the way she had gone through the well and the garden, taking the count of the other jars on the high shelf, the beans and the squash and the saved tomato seed in the twist of paper, the onion sets in the cellar in the box of dry sand, the potatoes the wind had shriveled, and she set the count out in columns in her head, and the columns said what they said. What the columns said was that the seed was small but it was whole. Three years of dust had not eaten the seed. The land was dead, or dying, or gone grey, but the seed in the jars was alive, and there was a kind of arithmetic in that, a statement about what the weather could take and could not take, and she set the thought by, the way she set the best ears by, to hold against the hard years.

The chickens had made it through. That was more than the barn cats could say. She had gone out in the brown light before supper and found the hens packed into the far corner of the coop with dust over their backs like a grey flock, and the old rooster, who had seen every storm of the decade, standing at the door of the coop with his head cocked at the grey sky as if to ask it what it thought it was doing. She had tossed them cracked corn and they had come, which meant they were alive, which meant eggs, which meant something, however small, that the weather had not taken. She counted them into the ledger too, in the column for lives, under the weather, because the ledger kept the lives of the stock as surely as it kept the lives of the family, and a woman who kept chickens kept their names and their years and the spring and fall of them. She turned the ledger to the last written page, the page for the fall of 1934, where the corn had failed and the wind had come and the list of what the weather had taken had taken its last line, and she dipped the pen, the one pen, the one she kept wrapped in a cloth at the back of the drawer, and she held it over the page.

The dust settled on the inkwell. She had read the almanac like scripture all her life, and the almanac said what it said, and the sky said what it said, and she had stood in the door of the house on the afternoon of the third burial and looked at a country that had been remade in grey, and she had come in and drawn the water and wiped the table and lifted the ledger down, and now the pen was in her hand, and there was nothing to write but what was true.

She wrote.

April 14, 1935. The third burial. The storm came at noon. We are still here.

She wrote it in her careful hand, the round hand of the girl who had bought the book in the white heat of 1919 and believed the column for rain would run forever, and she read it back, and the words sat on the page the way a stone sits, and she closed the ledger and wrapped it in the oilcloth and set it back on the shelf between the lamp and the clean jars.

Then she went to the window. The dust was still coming down. The world was grey to the fence line, and beyond the fence line grey to the horizon, and the horizon, where the sky met the country, was only a lighter grey, the way a nickel is lighter where the sun has worn it. Somewhere out in that grey were the Hallorans' place, and the county road, and Boise City, and the country, and the whole of the country that had become a grey country, and she stood and looked at it, and she did not pray, because she had never been a woman who knew how to pray the prayers other women prayed. She kept count. That was her prayer. And the count said the rain had not come, and the count said the land had been buried three times, and the count said the seed corn was small, and the count said her son was dead and her daughter was hungry for a country that was dying, and the count said they were still here.

We are still here, she thought, standing at the window with the ledger wrapped behind her on the shelf. And she turned the word over, the way you turn a seed over in your hand, testing it for what it would do in the ground. Still here was a statement of fact. It was not yet a promise. But it was something to plant.

The dust came down all that night, and the next morning the sun rose white through it, and the world had the look of a room where someone had swept everything into the corners and pulled a sheet over the middle. June Vogel took the ledger down from the shelf and laid it open on the table, and she began, in the small hours of the morning, to count what was left in the house, in the barn, in the cellar, in the ground, the whole arithmetic of ruin, written out in her careful hand, because if the dust forgot, the ledger would remember, and remembering was the beginning of planting.

Chapter 3: The Windbreak

The county agent's pamphlet reached the feed store in Boise City in April, the same month as the third burial, and it passed from hand to hand the way a letter passes in a country where the mail is uncertain. It was a thin thing, folded in thirds, with the seal of the federal government at the top and the words Prairie States Forestry Project printed in a plain type, and it told of a plan to plant a windbreak across the whole of the plains, a hundred million trees, a living fence against the dust, and every man who read it out loud in the feed store read the number twice, because a hundred million was a number with no country behind it, and then he folded it and put it in his pocket and did not know what to do with it, because a windbreak was something you planted in good years, and there were no good years.

June heard about it from Abel Halloran, who came by on a Sunday afternoon with the pamphlet creased and the creases dirty from his pocket, and he stood in the yard and read her the best of it, the part about the rows of cottonwood and willow running north to south against the wind, and he left the pamphlet with her, and she read it after supper by lamplight, the whole of it, slowly, the way she read the almanac, and she read the words shelterbelt and the words living fence and the words the wind will break, and she set the pamphlet on the shelf beside the oil lamp, and she did not say anything about it, and that was how everyone knew what she meant to do.

Walter said it plainly enough, the way he said everything, on a cold morning with the wind raw off the quarter.

"Trees. We're going to set out trees."

"I'm going to set out trees," June said. "You and Cora are going to help me, or you're going to stand in the road and watch. One of the two."

"Against what, June? Against the sky? The wind that buried us three times is not going to be stopped by a row of whips that won't be tall as a man in my lifetime."

"Not your lifetime," she said. "Sammy's. And mine, if the rain ever comes. Trees live longer than the men who set them, Walter. That's the whole point of a tree."

He looked at her a long moment, and she saw something move behind his eyes, something she could not name, and he did not argue again. That night he came in with the shovel and the bar and set them by the door, and he said, "If we're doing it, we do it right. Double row. They say a single row won't hold."

She had not said double row. She had not said anything about the count. She looked at him standing there with the tools, and she saw that he had been reading the pamphlet after all, reading it the way a man reads when he is past hoping and has to be careful what he lets in, and she said, "Double row," and he nodded, and that was the most they had agreed on in a year.

The morning she went into the feed store to buy the seedlings, the pamphlet was lying on the counter under the tin of chewing tobacco, and the men had been at it. Mr. Ellis, who ran the store and kept the weather the way other men kept a ledger, held up one of the federal trees, a cottonwood whip no thicker than a pencil, and the men laughed, not cruel, the way men laugh at a thing that is a long way off and not coming back. "A hundred million of these," Mr. Ellis said, "and the wind'll be out of a job." The men laughed again. June did not laugh. She stood at the counter with her egg money folded in her handkerchief, and she looked at the box of seedlings Mr. Ellis had set out against the wall, the federal seedlings in their wet sacking, and she counted out her money onto the counter, and she bought three dozen cottonwoods and three dozen willows and three dozen Russian olives, and she bought them the way a woman buys seed corn, carefully, against the hard years.

The men went quiet. She did not look at them. She loaded the wheelbarrow and walked out, and the wind came up the street and took her skirt, and behind her she could hear them take up again, softer, and she did not care. A man at the feed store could laugh at a tree and go home and forget it. A woman who had watched her land buried three times could not.

The windbreak went in on the north quarter, along the fence line that the third burial had taken, the line where the country's wind came off the flat and hit the house first. The county agent had drawn it that way in the pamphlet: north to south, double row, cottonwood and willow and Russian olive, the willow in the inside row where the water was, the Russian olive in the outside row where it was dry, the cottonwood tall in the middle, and June had read it and set the rows in her mind, and she had gone out before dawn with a ball of twine and two stakes and she had laid the line out herself, straight as a string, and when the sun came up she was standing at the north end of the rows with the twine in her hand, and the country lay grey and flat before her, and she was not afraid of it.

Sammy dug the holes. He dug them the way he did everything, careful, with a seriousness that was old in his shoulders, and he measured them with the handle of the shovel the way the pamphlet said, and he would not let Cora dig them faster, because a hole that was too shallow was a tree that was dead, and he knew what dead was. Cora hauled the water. That was the job June gave her, and it was the job that mattered, because the trees were set in the ground of a country that had not rained, and a tree in that country was not planted so much as watered, and Cora carried the bucket from the cistern down the length of the rows, one bucket per tree, slow and heavy, and the cistern went down a little with every trip, and June watched the water go out of the cistern the way she watched everything, in the columns of her head, and she did the arithmetic and she held to it, because the arithmetic said the cistern would just hold, if the rain came, if the rain remembered its own country, and the arithmetic had been wrong for five years, and she held to it anyway, because there was nothing else to hold to.

She planted the double row with her own hands, every third hole hers, and she did not count aloud, and she did not say what the count was. Sammy asked her once, squatting by the willow at the end of the row, "How many trees, Ma?" and she said, "One more than we need," and he looked at the row, at the whips standing thin and grey and barely alive against the grey country, and he counted them with his eyes the way children count, and he said, "There's one that's shorter. That last one. It's a runt."

"That's the extra one," June said.

"What's it for?"

"It's for Ezra."

She said it plain, and she said it without looking at him, and she said it the way she said everything, as a fact, as an entry in a ledger, and Sammy did not ask again. He stood up and he went to the short tree and he took the dipper from the bucket and he gave it its water first, before the big ones, and he did that every day of that spring without being asked, and June watched him from the corner of her eye and said nothing, because there was nothing to say about a thing that was true.

The extra tree. She had not planned it. She had bought the seedlings from the county agent's box at the feed store, three dozen cottonwoods, three dozen willows, three dozen Russian olive, and she had counted them out into the wheelbarrow, and she had stood at the box a long moment, and she had taken one more, a willow, thin and notched at the crown, the runt of its lot, and she had paid for it with the last of her egg money and she had walked home with it wrapped in a wet rag and she had not explained, because there was no explaining, because a woman who has buried a child does not have to explain the extra tree.

They worked the rows into May. The wind came and the dust came, and the whips stood in the grey ground and took it, and she watered them from the cistern and the cistern went down and the whips did not die, which was something, which was the first something the north quarter had given them in three years. She kept the ledger column for it. She wrote, in the back page, under the pamphlet's own words:

April 1935. Set out the windbreak, double row, north quarter. Cottonwood, willow, Russian olive. Watered from the cistern. One extra tree, for Ezra.

She did not write more, because the ledger did not need more. The count was the count. But she read the entry over at night, by the lamp, after the house was quiet, and the words sat on the page and she looked at them, at the four names of the trees, cottonwood and willow and Russian olive and Ezra, and it seemed to her that the row and the family were the same thing, that she was planting the family, that every whip in the grey ground was one of them standing in the wind, that the row stood for the family and the family stood for the row, and that a windbreak was only what a family was when it held together. She had always known this, she thought. She had not had the words for it, and now the pamphlet had given her the words, living fence, and she kept the word in her the way she kept the seed corn, to hold against the hard years.

On the last day of planting, with the sun low and the wind down, she walked the rows alone. The whips stood to her waist, thin, grey, barely there, and the light went long across the country, and she walked the double row north to south and south to north, and she came to the extra tree, the willow for Ezra, and she stood over it, and the wind came up off the flat, and the willow bent and stood, bent and stood, and she looked at it for a long time, and the wind was the same wind, and the tree was a small thing against it, and she understood, standing there in the long light, that that was the whole of it. The wind would come, and the tree would bend, and she was going to water it anyway.

That night she asked Cora for the bucket at the door, and Cora brought it, and June knelt in the dark by the rows and loosened the soil at the foot of the extra tree with her fingers, and she poured the water slow into the little basin she had made, and the ground drank it, and she sat back on her heels, and the dark was the grey dark of the dust year, and the row stood invisible around her, and she talked, low, to the tree.

"I won't tell you he was a fine boy, because you'll know it soon enough. He was eight. He was quicker than the rest of us, and he never once minded that the dust came in under the door. He liked the cows better than he liked people, and he never met a gate he didn't open."

The wind came and went. She set her hand on the little willow.

"He died in the spring," she said. "The fever in the lungs. The doctor said it was the dust, but the dust was in everything that spring, and it was in him too, and I have asked the Lord a thousand times why He took the quick one and left me the careful ones, and the Lord has not answered, and I have stopped asking. I count, instead. That's the thing I know how to do. I count what's left, and I plant, and I hold, and I am telling you, tree, because you will stand here long after I am counted, that I mean to see this family stand the way these rows stand. One for each of us, and one more than we need."

She sat with her hand on the willow a long time, and the wind came off the flat, and the willow bent and stood, and she got up and walked back to the house, and she did not look back, because a woman who has set her hand to a thing does not count it twice.

The light went out of the country by degrees, the way it went out of everything that spring, grey to greyer, and behind her the double row stood in the grey ground like a line of small soldiers, and one of them was short, and the wind came off the flat, and the short one bent and stood.

Chapter 4: Walter Goes West

He told them on a Sunday night, at the table, after the dishes were done and the lamp was lit and there was nothing left to put off. He said it the way he said everything that mattered, flat, with his eyes on the table, the way a man sets down a load he has been carrying a long time and does not look at it.

"The harvest run starts in Kansas the first of June. Dutch's trucks are coming through Boise City the last week of May, and they'll take men. Wheat's short this year, but a short harvest is still a harvest, and a man can follow it west — Kansas wheat, then the corn, then California, the fruit and the cotton. Money in California. Real money, for a man with two hands."

Cora looked up. Her eyes went sharp and bright, the way they did when a road opened in front of her. June saw it and felt the cold run through her, and she said nothing.

"California," Sammy said. "That's where the oranges grow. The postcards."

"That's right," Walter said. "Oranges. You ever seen an orange, Sammy?"

"No, Pa."

"You will. I'll send you one in the mail. A whole one, in a box, and you can smell the rind before you open it."

Sammy grinned. He was ten and the idea of an orange in a box was the idea of a wonder, and the grin lit his whole face, and June watched it and felt the cold run through her again, because the grin was on Ezra's face too, the quick grin of the quick one, and Walter had not seen Ezra grin in two years, and Sammy had been carrying the grin for both of them.

The quarrel came later, after Sammy was in bed and Cora was out at the henhouse and the kitchen was down to the two of them, the lamp low, the dust film grey on everything. Walter stood at the window with his hands in his pockets, and June stood at the stove with her hands folded, and they did not look at each other, and they had had this argument every way there was to have it, and still it came around again, the way the wind came around.

"I'm not going to argue with you," he said. "I'm telling you the truth of the matter. The farm is done, June. It's been done for three years and you know it. The seed you put in the ground is money you're burying. The trees you planted are prayer flags, and I'll be gone before they're tall enough to break a dust storm, and so will you. There's nothing here but the grave and the dust."

"The grave is my son," she said. "You will not call him nothing."

"I'm not calling him nothing. I'm calling the farm nothing, and I'm calling the rest of our lives nothing if we stay here and let the wind keep burying us. I wanted to sell out and go together — all of us, the whole family, the way the Hallorans are going to do before the year's out, the way every man on this road is going to do. You're the one who wouldn't go. You're the one who said no."

"And I'll say no again," she said, low, and now she looked at him, and her face was the quiet steel he knew. "I am not leaving him out there with no one to mind the grave."

"There's a fence around the grave, June. The wind can't take a grave with a fence around it."

"The fence line went under the dust in April. The third time. I stood at the door and I watched it go under, and I stood there knowing he was under it, and I dug it out with my hands, Walter. I know what a fence can do. A fence is not a person. A person is a person, and the only person who minds that grave is me."

He turned from the window. In the low light his face was drawn, the face of a man who had been carrying the load a long time and had come to the end of the arguing, and there was something in it that was not anger, that had not been anger for a long time, and when he spoke his voice was different, lower.

"I'm not going to ask you to go. I've stopped asking. I know the ground you stand on, June, and I know why you stand on it, and I would stand on it with you if I thought standing would feed the boy. It won't. It'll bury him like it buried the fence line. So I'm going to go out and find the money and send it home, and when the dust is done burying this country — and it will be done, one day, it always is — I'll come back and we'll stand on the ground together. But I will not set another table for the wind."

She did not answer. She stood at the stove, and the lamp burned, and the dust film greyed, and she heard him, and she kept the count in her the way she kept everything, and the count said he was going, and the count said she could not stop him, and the count said he was right about the money and wrong about the grave, and the two true things sat in her chest like two stones.

He went out after that, down the lane, and she watched him from the window go to the barn and stand a long time at the open door, and she knew without seeing his face that he was looking at the tools, at the plow and the harness and the seed bags he would not be using, and she turned from the window and she remembered, the way a woman remembers in the small hours, the other leaving, the one before the farm was a grave, the spring they had taken up this quarter section, the two of them with the team and the tent and everything they owned in the wagon, and the way the land had looked to them then, dark and good and theirs, the flood plain of the Cimarron with the river running, and Walter had stopped the wagon on the rise and looked out over it and said, "This is where we'll stand." She had been twenty-six. She had believed him. She had kept the belief the way she kept the seed, and the seed had gone down to a jar, and the belief had gone down with it, and it was not gone, she thought, standing at the window, it was only planted deeper, the way a tree goes down in dry ground to find the water.

She went to the dresser in the bedroom and took down the old cigar box where she kept the egg money, the nickels and dimes and the rare paper, and she counted it out on the bed, slow, the way she counted everything, and it came to a little over five dollars, and she folded the five-dollar bill in quarters and she put the loose change back in the box and she sat on the bed with the bill in her hand a long time, and she did not say anything, because there was nothing to say about a thing that was only true.

The week that followed was the longest kind of week, the kind that has no weather in it. Walter worked the north quarter with the new shovel, running the rows the wind had half buried, and June saw him out there in the grey afternoons and did not ask what he was digging, because a man who is going away digs with a different weight, and she understood that he was settling the country in his mind, row by row, so that he would have it to carry. Cora went quiet. That was the strange thing. Cora, who talked at the world the way a river talks to its banks, went quiet and watchful, and June found her more than once standing at the road's edge at dusk, looking west the way a person looks at a road she has not been allowed to take, and June said nothing, and kept the knowledge, and held it.

He left on a Thursday. The day came up clear, a good day, a day with a washed look to it that the dust year only gave you once in a while, and the harvest trucks were coming through Boise City that afternoon, and Walter stood in the yard in his good shirt with his bundle and his bedroll, and the family stood with him. Cora had her arms crossed and her jaw set, and she was looking at the road, and June could see in the set of her daughter's shoulders the exact thing she had seen in the set of her husband's, the aim out and away, and she held her peace. Sammy stood close to his father, close enough to take his hand if he was the kind of boy who took hands, which he was not anymore, and he stood with his arms at his sides and asked the question he had been holding since Sunday.

"When are you coming back, Pa?"

"When the dust is done," Walter said. "When the harvests pay. When I've got a stake to come home with. You mind your mother and keep the trees watered, and I'll send you an orange before the snow."

"Will you write?"

"Every week. You watch the mailbox."

They shook hands, the way men did, father and son, and it was more than a handshake, and they both knew it. Walter turned to Cora, and she uncrossed her arms, and she said, "You write to Ma. Don't you go forgetting the way home."

"I won't forget," he said. "A man forgets a lot of things, but he doesn't forget his own country."

He looked at June. They stood apart, the two of them, the way they had stood apart for two years, and she was holding something in her hand, folded small, and she came to him and put it in his hand, and he opened it, and it was a bill, a five-dollar bill, folded in quarters, and there was the dust of the house on it, and he looked at it, and he looked at her, and she said, "That's the egg money. Saved. You'll need it before the first pay."

"June —"

"Take it. It's not for you, it's for the family. A man on the road with an empty pocket is a man at the mercy of the road. You'll send it back when you're paid. I'm keeping the count."

He held the bill a moment. She saw his throat work. He was not a man who cried, and he did not cry then, but something moved in his face, and he folded the bill into his pocket and he picked up his bundle and he went down the lane, and the truck came up the road out of the dust, a flatbed truck with the planks of its sides high and the names of other men painted out, and Dutch, the boss, reached down a hand, and Walter took it and swung up, and the truck did not stop, did not idle, just swallowed him up the way a river swallows a stone, and the truck went on down the road toward Boise City and the pavement and the whole of the country beyond, and the dust of the road rose up behind it and hung in the clear air, and the family stood in the yard and watched it go until it was small and then gone, and the dust settled, and the road was empty.

Cora was the first to move. She turned without a word and went into the house, and June heard the kitchen door close, and she stood a long time looking at the empty road. The sun was white. The road went straight and level to the horizon, and there was nothing on it, and the country lay grey and flat, and the wind came up off the quarter, and she stood in the yard with her hands hanging, and she said it quietly, to the road, to the empty place where the truck had been, to no one.

"The land keeps a reckoning."

She did not know where the words came from. They came up out of her the way the wind came up off the flat, and she stood with them, and they had the weight of a truth she had been holding for a long time and had not had words for. The land kept a reckoning. It kept the count of what was buried in it and what was taken from it and what was owed to it, and the truck going west did not escape the count, and the man in the truck did not escape it, and the country did not escape it, and the account would be settled, one way or another, in rain or in dust or in the return of a man who had gone out to find the money and would have to come back to find himself.

She turned and went in. The kitchen was empty. The lamp was out. The dishes stood in the pan, and the dust film lay grey on everything, and she went to the shelf and took down the ledger and unwrapped it, and she opened it to the page, and she dipped the pen, and she wrote, in her careful hand:

May 1935. Walter has gone west with the harvest trucks. We are still here.

She read it back. We are still here. She had written it in April, and now she wrote it again, and it was not the same entry. In April it had been a statement. Now it was a promise she was making to the ledger, to the grave, to the road, and the pen in her hand made it, and she closed the book and wrapped it in the oilcloth and set it back on the shelf, and she went to the window.

The sun had gone down and the road had gone grey with the dusk, and the wind was coming up off the quarter, and the little trees on the north quarter were bending to it, bending and standing, and she stood at the window and watched them a long time, and the wind went on, and the trees stood, and the road was empty, and the account, whatever it would cost, had been opened.

Chapter 5: Cora's Reckoning

She told her mother on a Tuesday, in the kitchen, over the dishpan, because it was the only way she knew how to say a hard thing, with her hands busy and her eyes on the water. The week after Walter left had been the quietest week of her life. The house had gone down around them to two people and a boy, and the silence had come up like a well, and Cora had stood at the edge of it and looked down and decided.

"Ma. I'm going west too."

June's hands kept moving in the dishwater. She did not turn. She had learned a long time ago not to turn, and Cora knew it, and the knowing was part of the thing she was saying.

"Pa's on the trucks. The harvests. California. He said money, and I believe him, because the money isn't here. It isn't coming here. I can stand in this yard for the rest of my life and watch the wind bury the fence line, or I can go out and find where the money is and send it back, and send it back, and send it back, until there's enough of it to buy this place out of the dust. You taught me the arithmetic, Ma. You taught me to count. I've counted this place, and the count says there's nothing here but what we carry out."

June turned then. She turned and looked at her daughter, and Cora looked back, and the water dripped off her hands onto the floor, and neither of them looked away.

"You're seventeen."

"I know how old I am."

"Seventeen," June said, "and a girl, and the road west is full of girls who went west at seventeen. You've heard the same stories I have. You've stood in the feed store. You know what happens to a girl alone on the road."

"I know what happens to a girl alone in a yard with a dead country," Cora said. "I can see that from here. It happens slow, and nobody tells a story about it, and it happens right in front of me."

"Slow is not the same as dead," June said. "Slow is what keeps a house standing. Slow is what keeps a seed in the ground long enough to come up. You've watched me do slow for seventeen years, and I've watched you refuse to, and I have loved that about you, Cora. I have loved that about you and feared it in equal measure, and if I could buy you a year of slowness I would, but I can't, because the country's done the buying and it bought the year from us."

The words lay between them. June's face was still. Cora watched her mother's face the way she had watched it all her life, looking for the seam, and she found it, the small working in the jaw that meant her mother was counting, was turning something over in the columns of her head, and Cora waited, because she had learned that much.

"The harvest trucks are gone," June said at last. "They won't come through again till the cotton. How do you mean to get there?"

"The Greyhound runs out of Boise City on Tuesdays. The bus station's the feed store counter, but the bus runs, I've seen the schedule. I've got the fare — I saved the egg money you pay me. I've got it counted."

"You've been counting on this."

"Yes, Ma. I've been counting on this since the third burial. Since before that. Since the first one. I watched this country die, and I decided I would not die with it. You can call it running. I call it arithmetic."

June turned back to the dishpan. She washed a plate, slow, and set it in the rack, and washed another, and Cora stood at the table with her hands wet and did not move, and the water went on dripping, and the dust film greyed the windows, and when June spoke again her voice was the flat voice, the voice for the chickens and the land, and it held.

"I won't hold you," she said. "I could argue with you the way I argued with your father, and it would go the same way, and you'd go the way he went, and we'd part on the words instead of the blessing. I won't do it. If the road is what you've counted on, you go, and you go with my hand on you, not my back."

Cora felt the world move under her, the way the world moves when a door you've been pushing gives. She had not expected the blessing. She had armed herself for the fight, the way you arm yourself for a thing you expect, and the blessing came and found her unarmed, and something in her chest gave, and she had to look away.

"I'll send money," she said. "Every pay. You'll see the money order in the mail before the month's out. I'll write."

"You'll write," June said. "And you'll plant these where you land."

She went to the shelf by the stove and took down a twist of paper, and she untwisted it, and Cora saw seeds, small dark seeds, and June held them out in her palm and Cora looked at them, and they were ordinary, they were the seeds of a country, and her mother said, "Radish seed. It comes up fast, and it comes up anywhere, and it will grow in ground that won't grow corn. You take these, and when you find where you're standing, you put them in the ground, so there's a piece of home in the ground there."

"Ma —"

"And there's this." June went to the bedroom and came back with a folded seed packet, a paper packet, the kind the feed store sold for a nickel, and Cora saw the picture on the front, a tomato, round and red and impossible, and June put it in her hand and closed Cora's fingers over it. "These are for later," she said. "The radish is for now, so you eat. This one is for the day you find the place where you're going to stand, so there's a piece of home that takes root. You keep it in your coat, next to your chest, and you don't plant it till you're where you mean to stay. You'll know when. It's the kind of knowing you can't be told."

Cora looked at the packet in her hand. It was a small thing. It was the whole of her mother, the whole of the farm, the whole of the country, folded into paper, and she put it inside her coat, next to her chest, the way her mother had said, and she said, "I'll keep it. I'll plant it when I'm home. I'll come back and plant it in this ground."

"You won't be coming back to plant it here," June said. "You'll be planting it where you stand. That's the point of it."

"It's a seed packet," Cora said. "It's not a letter. It doesn't need an address to find home."

"It's a letter," June said. "It's the only letter that never gets lost in the mail. The mail goes astray in this country — you've seen the post office hold a month of letters at once — but a seed planted is a letter delivered. The ground delivers it. Every time you eat what grows from it, you're reading it."

Cora did not answer. She held the packet in her hand and she looked at the impossible red tomato on the front, and she understood, without having words for it, that she had just been handed the family's real post, the thing that would carry her mother's name across the miles the way a letter carried a name, and she folded the packet and put it inside her coat, next to her chest.

They did not hug. They were not a family that hugged, not since Ezra, not since the grief had gone down into them and come up as work. But June took her daughter's face in her hands, the way she had done when Cora was small and had fallen, and she held it a moment, and she looked at her, and she said, "You're the quick one now. You've been carrying his quickness. You go west and be quick, and don't you be ashamed of it. Quick is not a sin."

Cora left on the Tuesday bus. She left before the light, with a cardboard suitcase tied with rope and a cloth bag of bread and the jar of canned tomatoes her mother pressed on her at the door, and the seed packet in her coat, and she walked the four miles to Boise City in the grey dawn with the dust working up under her shoes, and she bought her ticket at the feed store counter from Mr. Ellis, who looked at her over his glasses and said, "Going to California, are you?" and she said, "Yes," and he said, "There's two hundred of you gone through this door this year, girl. Mind you come out the other end of it whole," and he sold her the ticket and did not make a joke of it.

The bus came down the street out of the dust at a quarter past seven, a Greyhound, all chrome and windows, and the sun came up white behind it, and Cora stood with her suitcase at the feed store steps and looked at it, and it was the biggest thing she had ever seen move, and the driver swung the door open and looked down at her, and she climbed up into the smell of exhaust and oil and other people's lives, and she took a seat by the window, and the bus pulled out, and Boise City went by the window, the courthouse and the feed store and the post office with the WPA mural the men were still painting on the wall above the door, and then the town was gone, and the road was the road, and the country unrolled grey and flat and endless on both sides.

She sat with her face to the window and her hand on the seed packet under her coat, and she thought of her mother standing at the kitchen door watching the bus go, and she did not look back, because looking back was a habit she had decided not to keep. The land went past, grey and level, the same land she had lived on all her life, and it was not until the bus crossed the state line that she realized she had been holding her breath, and she let it out, and the land on the other side of the line was the same land, grey and flat, but it was not the same, because on this side of the line it was not home, it was the road, and the road was going somewhere.

There were three other people on the bus. A woman in a black dress with a pasteboard box on her knees, going to a son who had sent for her; a man in a suit that had been good in 1929, asleep with his mouth open against the window; a boy not much older than Sammy with a dirty bandage on his hand and nothing else with him. None of them talked. The road did the talking, and the tires did the talking, and the dust did the talking, and Cora watched the three of them and thought how a bus is a church of people who have left something behind, and she counted herself in the congregation, and she did not know if that was comfort or warning, and she rode with it.

The seed packet was warm against her chest. She had counted everything, she told herself. The fare, the food, the days between now and the first pay, and the count had come out to just barely enough, the way her mother's counts always came out to just barely enough. But there was one thing she had not counted on, and she sat with it in the moving bus, the one thing her mother's arithmetic had never had a column for, and it was this: that the country on the other side of the state line was enormous, that the horizon was a rumor the road never caught up to, that she was seventeen years old and alone on the road, and that for the first time in her life there was nothing she had to do tomorrow, no rows to water, no chickens to feed, no count to keep, and the feeling that came up in her at that, sitting in the chrome and the dust of the Greyhound with her hand on her mother's seed packet, was so large it had no name.

She pressed her hand flat against her chest, over the packet, and she held the feeling, and she let the road unroll, and she said, very low, to the window, "Plant a seed and the land will answer." It was her mother's saying, and she said it as a question and as a promise, and the window gave her back her own face, and the face was young and going west, and beyond it the land went grey and flat and endless, and the road went on.

Chapter 6: The Harvest Trucks

The Greyhound let her off in a town she had never heard of, a crossroad on the Texas side with a gas station and a café and a row of parked flatbed trucks, and the man who ran the gas station pointed her at them. "Harvest trucks," he said. "Dutch's run. They'll carry you west if you can pay in work. Going to Kansas for the wheat, then out to the coast, then down to the valley for the cotton. You want California, you ride with Dutch. Nobody else is going."

She found Dutch sitting on the running board of the lead truck with a coffee can between his knees, and he was a man shaped like his name, wide and blunt, with the creased neck of a man who has driven every road in the country, and he looked her over the way he had looked over a hundred girls on the same running board, and he said, "You're a girl."

"I know what I am."

"Harvest run's no place for a girl. It's a long way, and it's rough, and I've got eleven men on these trucks and a certain amount of God-fearing sin in the back of them. You sure you're on the right road?"

"I'm on the road I mean to be on," she said. "I can ride in front with you, or I can ride with the men, or I can walk behind and catch you at the next town. One of the three. I've got a jar of tomatoes that says I'm not a problem."

He looked at the jar in her hand, and something moved in the creased face, and he laughed, a short laugh, the laugh of a man who has seen everything and is glad to have found one new thing.

"Get in," he said. "Ride up front. You can read the maps and keep the schedule, and you can keep the peace back there with your tongue, which looks like it's built for it. And we'll see about the tomatoes."

She climbed up into the cab of the lead truck, and it smelled of tobacco and oil and a man's long life on the road, and she set her suitcase at her feet and the jar of tomatoes in her lap, and the truck started, and behind them the other trucks started, one by one, a whole line of them, flatbeds with high plank sides and canvas over the top and the names of other towns painted out, and they pulled out of the crossroad town onto the pavement, and the pavement was the road, the big road, and Cora watched it unroll and felt the whole of her country let go of her the way a hand lets go.

The procession ran west. She counted the trucks from the cab window: seven of them, five loaded with men and gear and the high tent canvas of the harvest camp, two loaded with what looked like household goods, furniture and a stove and a mattress roped to the top, families riding on top of their whole lives, and Cora watched them the way you watch a country go by, and she began to learn the arithmetic of the road, which was not the arithmetic of the farm.

The road had its own prices. A sandwich at the café where they stopped was a dime, and a cup of coffee was a nickel, and a canteen of water that a man would not have thought to buy on the farm cost five cents at a pump that had gone to the trouble of hauling the water in, and Cora learned to read the prices the way her mother read the weather, and she learned which men on the trucks had money and which had nothing, and she learned the look of a man who had nothing, the set of his shoulders at a café door, the way he stood to the side and watched the ones with dimes go in.

The men on the trucks were a country of their own. She learned their names the way she learned the prices, by sitting and listening. There was a man called Preacher, who had not preached in years and carried a Bible in his bedroll the way other men carried tools; there was a man called Shorty, who was not short, and would not say why; there were brothers from Arkansas who did not speak to each other and worked as one; there was a boy not much older than she was who had been on the road since he was fourteen and could roll a cigarette with one hand while the truck bounced, and who watched the horizon the way a sailor watches water. They did not bother her. That was Dutch's doing, and she knew it, and she was grateful in the quiet way of a person who has learned that gratitude is a thing to keep in your pocket. A couple of the men made the joke that a girl in a harvest cab was a bad omen for the wheat, and Dutch said, "She reads the schedule. You can't. Get back in the truck," and the joke went away and did not come back.

Dutch taught her the country as they drove. He would point out the window and say, "That's where the wheat turns. You watch the color, you'll know before the county agent does." He knew the road the way her mother knew the rows, and it occurred to Cora, riding beside him in the hot cab, that a road boss and a farm woman were the same animal with different ground, both of them keeping the count of the weather and the work, both of them reading the signs, both of them holding the family of men and women together by the sheer refusal to let it come apart. She told him so, once, and he laughed his short laugh.

"You're a farmer's get, all right," he said. "You see a column where other folks see a road."

Dutch was the boss of the run, and he was boss the way a river is boss of its own bed. He did not give orders so much as he stated facts, and the facts had a way of happening. He knew every town on the road, every gas station that would water a truck for the price of the tank, every county where the sheriff was a friend and every county where the sheriff was a law, and he talked to Cora the way a man talks to a map, not because he cared for her but because she could read, and reading was a thing worth a place in the cab.

"You read the schedule," he said. "You keep the count of the miles and the money. That's the job. A run like this lives and dies on the count. The man who doesn't count is the man who gets stuck in the mud in a county with no water and no gas and a sheriff who's been waiting on a harvest truck since March. You count, and you'll never be stuck."

"Yes, sir," she said. "I was raised to count."

He looked at her a long moment, and he nodded, and he said, "Your people are farmers."

"How'd you know?"

"I can tell a farmer's get by the way they count. A farmer counts everything, because the weather's free and it charges what it likes. Count this: we cross the state line into Kansas in two days, we work the wheat through the first of July, and then we go west to the coast and down to the valley, and the valley will take every pair of hands that can reach a branch. A girl who can count and a girl who can read will eat in the valley. That's the arithmetic."

The country went by. It went by in a way she had never seen, because she had never seen country from a moving truck, and she learned that a moving country is a different thing from a standing country. A standing country is a map; a moving country is a river, and it has a taste and a smell and a motion, and the motion never stopped. The land went grey and flat and went on being grey and flat until she understood that flatness was not a condition of the ground but a condition of the world, that the horizon was a rumor the road never caught, and that she had lived her whole life inside a rumor and had thought it was a wall.

The trucks crossed the Texas Panhandle and the men in the back sang. They sang the songs of the road, the old ones and the ones no one would remember, and Cora sat in the cab and listened to the singing come through the little window behind her head, and she learned the songs by heart without trying, the way you learn a thing that is going to matter. Some of the songs were about the road and some were about the country and some were about a girl in a town behind them, and they sang them all the same way, with the same hunger in the voices, and Cora listened and understood that the hunger was the road's real cargo, that the trucks were carrying hunger west, that every man in the back of the trucks was a man who had left something standing and gone to find the money.

They stopped for the night at a crossroads camp, and the men made a fire out of fence posts and mesquite, and there was coffee and the smell of it on the night air, and Cora sat apart with the women, because there were two other women on the run, wives, and they took her in the way women take a girl in, asking nothing and telling everything, and they showed her how to sleep in the open with your coat rolled under your head and your feet to the fire, and the sky above the camp was enormous, and Cora lay looking at it, at the whole of the night sky that the dust year had been hiding behind its grey, and it was like a roof being lifted off her whole life.

The two wives were sisters, the wives of the brothers who did not speak, and they had ridden the harvest run together every year since 1930, and they talked to Cora the way an old farmer talks to a new fence post, explaining the ground. They told her which towns on the road had clean water and which had alkali, and which harvests paid by the bushel and which by the hour, and how to read a pay line, and how to hold a canvas over yourself in a wind that would take the skin off, and how to keep a pot of beans going on the same fire through three shifts of men. They told her the names of the towns they had left behind them and the names of the children buried along the way, in the two years the dust had taken children, and they told her, last, the thing they said every girl needed to hear on the road, which was this: "You're a person before you're a hand, and don't you let a foreman make you forget it."

She rode on in the cab with that, with the names of the children and the words about being a person, and she turned them over the way she turned everything, and she kept them in the pocket with her gratitude, to spend when she needed them.

She was not afraid. That was the strange thing. She had expected to be afraid, had packed her fear along with her coat, and she lay in the open at the crossroads camp under the enormous sky and she was not afraid. The road had its prices and she had read them, and she could read, and she could count, and she had a jar of tomatoes and a seed packet against her chest and the whole of the west in front of her, and the fire burned, and the men sang low, and the night went on, and she slept, and in the morning the trucks started, and the wheels took the road, and the road took her west, and the country unrolled grey and flat and endless, and Cora Vogel, who had never been more than four miles from the farm in her life, rode in the cab of the lead truck with her hand on the schedule and her mother's seed packet warm against her heart, and the land went by like a river.

She counted the miles. It was the one thing she could do that made the road lie down and behave. Seven trucks, thirty-one men, two women, one girl. A hundred and twelve miles to the first Kansas town, then a hundred and forty, then the wheat. She wrote the numbers on the back of the schedule in the small cramped hand her mother had taught her, and she watched the color of the wheat turn the way Dutch said it would turn, and the land went by, and the road went on, and in the cab of the lead truck the schedule grew a second column, her own, and the column said what the columns always said: that the miles were being eaten, that the money was being spent, that the road was being crossed, and that somewhere ahead of her, past the wheat and the river and the mountains and the valley, the road ended in ground that would grow a tomato, and she meant to plant her mother's packet in it and stand there and let the counting come out even at last.

Chapter 7: Highway 66

The harvest trucks turned off the pavement at the Kansas line, and the wheat was waiting, and Cora rode the season the way a hand rides a season, in the field at dawn and in the camp at dark, and the wheat went down and the money went up, a little, and the little was real, and she sent a penny postcard home in the middle of July, the first word her mother had had since the truck swallowed her in Boise City. She bought it at a gas station for a penny, and she wrote on it in her small cramped hand, in the space the size of a hand, We are in Kansas. The wheat is high. I am fine. Do not worry. — Cora, and she mailed it, and she watched it drop into the box the way you watch a root go down, and she went back to the field.

The trucks moved west when the Kansas wheat was down. Dutch said it the way he said everything, as a fact: "We go west now. There's a corn harvest in the river country, and then the valley, and the valley will take all of us." The trucks crossed out of Kansas onto the road that was called Route 66, the big road, the road that ran from the middle of the country to the coast, and Cora sat up front in the cab and watched the road go from a road to a river.

It was a river of people. She had thought the seven trucks were a procession; they were a single log in the flood. The road was full of them. Trucks and cars and buses and wagons, and on top of the trucks the tents and the mattresses and the furniture, and on top of the cars the family belongings roped down, and in the open trucks the people, whole families of them, riding west with their faces turned toward a country they had heard about, and the road ran with them, a river of the displaced, and Cora sat in the cab and looked at the river and understood that she was in it, that she had been in it since Boise City, that the whole of her country was in it, that the dust had done this, that a wind that buried the land had uprooted the people the way it uprooted the fence lines, and they were rolling west on the big road like tumbleweeds, only they were people, and they were singing.

The road had stations, and Cora learned them the way a child learns the stations of a story. The gas stations with the pumps and the glass towers of oil, the tourist cabins in rows painted the color of the desert, the roadside camps where a family could pull off for the night for a quarter, the cafés with the jukeboxes, and the jukeboxes were the wonder of the road.

At one of the camps she met Lula. She was an old woman, sixty if she was a day, with a face like a plowed field and eyes that had seen the road since 1929, and she sat at a fire at the edge of the camp with a pot of beans that had been going since some other state, and she took one look at Cora standing at the edge of the light with her suitcase and her jar and she said, "Set down, girl. You're the newest thing on this road, and that makes you the oldest thing too, because it makes you the same as the rest of us only with less dust on you." Cora set down. Lula talked, and Cora listened, and she learned the road from a woman who had crossed it every year since the bottom fell out, who had buried a husband in one state and a daughter's hopes in another and had come out the other side of both with a pot of beans and a way of speaking that was as straight as a fence line. "The road's a school," Lula said. "It charges tuition and it don't give no refunds, but it teaches you what you're made of. You'll find out, by and by, whether you're made of enough."

Cora looked at the old woman's face and at the beans and at the fire, and she said, "And what am I made of?"

"Same as all of us," Lula said. "Wanting. The road runs on wanting. You want something bad enough, you'll get there, and you'll find out what you wanted once you've got it."

She rode on with that, with Lula's words about wanting, and she turned them over the way she turned everything, and she kept them in the pocket with the other things she had learned, to spend when she needed them.

The jukeboxes were the wonder of the road, and the first time she stood before one she had never seen anything like it. The feed store in Boise City had a radio, but a jukebox was a different thing, a box of glass and light that played a song for a nickel, and the first time she stood in a café on Route 66 and watched a man put a nickel in and the box light up and the music come out, she stood very still and watched it the way she had watched the storm through the door of the kitchen, with the whole of her wanting it.

"It's a Wurlitzer," the café woman said. "A nickel a song. You can pick any record in the rack."

"Any one?"

"Any one at all."

Cora stood at the rack a long time, reading the names of songs she had never heard of, and the names were like names of towns, promising something, and she chose one with a name that sounded like a road, and she put her nickel in, the nickel she had saved against her own hunger, and the box lit up and the music came out, and she stood in the middle of the café on Route 66 with the music going through her, the music she had never heard before, and she understood, with the part of her that had been counting all her life, that this was the song the world played, that the world had a song and this was it, and that she was going to have to learn it, note by note, the way she had learned the prices.

The road had its music and it had its preachers. Every crossroads had a tent, it seemed, a tent of white canvas gone grey with the dust, with a cross on the pole or a hand-painted sign, and the trucks would pull off and the men would go and stand at the back and listen, and Cora learned that the tent meetings were as much a part of the road as the gas stations, that a people being moved needed a word to move by, and the preachers gave it.

They came to Brother Pemberton's tent at a crossroads in the Texas Panhandle, where the road forked and the dust lay deep in the ditches, and the tent was bigger than the others, and the preacher was a big man with a voice that carried over the noise of the road, and Cora stood at the back with the crowd from the trucks and listened, and she did not know why she stopped, except that the man's voice had the cadence of her mother's speech in it, the plain-church cadence, the sentences that stood up straight and expected nothing back, and it found her where she stood.
