Brother Pemberton preached the road that night. He preached the people on it and the country they had left and the country they were going to, and he preached the dust, and his voice went low and then high and then low again, and the crowd swayed with it, and then he preached the word.

"You have been moved," he called out. "You have been moved off the land your fathers broke. You have been moved by wind and by want and by the failure of the rain, and you are standing here tonight on the road to a country you have not seen, and I am telling you there is a reckoning coming. There is a reckoning for the land that was broken and the rain that did not come and the children that were taken. The Lord does not forget the count, brothers and sisters. The Lord keeps a book, and every name is in it, and the reckoning is coming, and it will be a sweet reckoning for the faithful."

The word went into Cora like a splinter. She stood at the back of the tent with the crowd swaying around her and the word in her, and she turned it over in the dark of her own mind, and it was not a preacher's word, not to her. It was her mother's word. It was the word that lived in the ledger, in the columns, in the count of what was planted and what was lost. It was what her mother did at the kitchen table every fall, and here was the preacher saying the same word, and the word meant something else to him, meant the Lord and the book and the judgment, and Cora stood in the tent with the two meanings of the word and felt the splinter of it go in and stay.

She did not pray. She had never been a praying girl. But she stood at the back of the tent until the meeting was over and the crowd broke up and the preachers' song died away into the dust of the road, and she walked back to the trucks with the word still in her, and she thought of her mother's ledger, and she thought of the columns, and she thought, that is the word. That is the whole of it. There is a reckoning coming, and it is not the Lord's, it is the land's, and the land keeps a book, and my mother keeps a book, and the book is the same book.

She climbed into the cab and sat in the dark with her hand on the seed packet, and the road went by, and the trucks went west, and somewhere behind her the tent stood empty in the dust with the word still hanging in it, and ahead of her the road went on, and she carried the word the way she carried the seed packet, in her coat, next to her chest, to plant where she landed.

The road taught her other things that season. It taught her the look of the camps along the roadside, the families under the canvas, the women cooking at fires made of anything that would burn, the children with the grey dust on their faces like a second skin, and it taught her the look of the men standing at the gas stations with their thumbs out, men who had missed the trucks and would take anything with wheels, and it taught her the look of the towns that did not want the road, the towns with signs at the edge telling the travelers to keep moving, and the look of the towns that did, the towns with the cafes and the camps and the welcome written in a different hand.

It taught her the chorus of the road. The families that passed the trucks going west, wave after wave of them, whole households in open trucks with the dishes stacked in a crate and the children wedged in among the furniture, and the families that passed going east again, fewer, and the look on the eastbound faces was a look she learned to read before she learned any other, the look of a country that had not been what the postcards promised. It taught her the men without women, driving alone or riding the trucks with nothing but a bedroll and the names of towns they had heard paid well, and it taught her the women without men, which were fewer and went more quietly, and it taught her that the road had a grammar, that a person's place in the stream of it told you what they had lost, and that the whole of the stream was made of loss with hope riding on top of it, the way a river carries a leaf.

It taught her, mile by mile, what the country was made of, and she rode the road and she learned, and the road went by like a river, and the river carried her west, and every mile the word from the tent meeting went with her, a splinter in her coat, and her mother's seed packet warm against her chest. When they crossed into New Mexico the land began to change, and the grey of the Panhandle gave way to a red the color of rust, and the flatness gave way to a country that stood up, mesas and buttes rising out of the dust like the bones of something, and Cora watched it the way she had watched the storm, with the whole of her, and she understood that she was seeing, for the first time in her life, a country that was not her own, that the world was not the flat grey she had been born into, that it was enormous and various and full of countries within countries, and that she had left one country and was riding through others on her way to the one she had been promised, and the feeling in her chest was so large it had no name, and she rode with it, through the red country, into the west.

Chapter 8: The First Camp

The trucks stopped for good at the Texas–New Mexico line in the last week of July, at a camp that had no name and needed none, a scatter of canvas and board at the edge of a town that was itself a scatter, and Dutch climbed down from the cab and looked at the sky and said, "This is where the run splits. The wheat is behind us and the valley is ahead, and the valley won't take the whole of us at once, so we set here and work the local harvest till the cotton opens. This is the camp. Learn the rules."

The rules. Cora learned them the way she learned everything, by watching, and the first thing she learned was that a camp was a town with the walls torn down. Every family's canvas was its house, and the ground between the tents was the street, and the fire was the hearth, and the rules were written in nobody's hand and enforced by everybody's eyes. There was the rule of the water: the camp had one well and one pump, and the water was rationed by a system of turn and count that her mother would have admired, and a girl who took more than her share learned it from the set of the women's backs. There was the rule of the fire: the fire was public, and a pot that went on it was a pot that fed anybody who was hungry, and a person who fed without paying in fuel or work found the fire cold at her side the next night. There was the rule of the flies, which were the real governors of the camp, more absolute than any county law, and the rule of the stink, which came off the ditch where the camp's waste went, and the two together taught a girl more about the arithmetic of the world than any schoolroom.

She counted the tents the first day, the way she counted everything, to make the place lie down in her mind: thirty-one of them, and the trucks at the edge of them like a fence, and the water pump at the center of the row like a flag, and the fire ring, and the ditch, and the highway beyond, and the country. Thirty-one families holding what was left of thirty-one farms, and the farms themselves gone grey behind them, and the whole of it spread on the ground at the line between two states as if the country had set it there to be counted. She learned the camp's other arithmetic too, the one the one-armed man would teach her in numbers: that a camp of thirty-one tents fed itself on the wages of the men who could find work, and that work was a season, and that a season was measured in days, and that a day without work was a day of the fire going short, and that the camp's whole machinery ran on the count of what was coming in against what was going out. It was her mother's ledger, she saw, writ large and set down in canvas and dust, and the arithmetic was the same arithmetic, and the answer, so far, was the same answer, and she held the knowledge the way she held everything, to spend when she needed it.

The camp had a schoolmaster, though no one called him that. He was a one-armed man who had lost the arm in France in the war, and he sat by the fire of an evening with a pencil and a board and taught the children their letters and their numbers, and the adults their numbers especially, because a man who could not reckon was a man who could be cheated, and the camp had seen cheating. Cora sat at the edge of his class the second night, close enough to hear, and the one-armed man looked at her and said, "You're a new one. Can you reckon?"

"Tolerable," she said.

"Show me. A man buys forty pounds of beans at a cent and a half a pound. The scale weighs light. What does he pay?"

"Sixty cents," she said. "And if the scale weighs light, he pays more than the beans are worth, and he can't see it, because he can't read the scale."

The one-armed man looked at her a long moment, and he nodded, and he said, "You'll do. Set down."

The camp taught her the price of things, and the price of things was the arithmetic of her new country. She had learned the prices of the road, the nickel and the dime, but the camp taught her the prices of a woman, and it taught her slow, and it taught her without kindness, the way the road taught everything. She learned what a canteen cost, and a wash cost, and a bed in a house if she was fool enough to want one, and she learned what a woman alone was priced at, and the price was set by the men who did the pricing, and she learned to carry herself so that the pricing was not asked to her face, and she learned the look of the girls who had not learned it, and the look of the women who had not learned it in time, and she held the knowledge the way she held the seed packet, in her coat, next to her chest.

Lula found her on the third night, sitting at the edge of the firelight with her suitcase and her jar, and the old woman set down beside her without asking, the way she had set down at the roadside camp, and she looked at Cora for a long moment, and she said, "You been sleeping rough."

"Under the truck. It's dry under the truck."

"It's dry under the truck," Lula said, "and it's dark under the truck, and it's alone under the truck, and a girl alone in a camp at night is a girl the camp will price, and you said you know what a woman alone is priced at. Set your blanket in my tent. I got room, and I got a tongue, and between the two of us we'll keep the pricing honest."

Cora looked at the old woman. The firelight moved in the plowed-field face, and the eyes that had seen the road since 1929 looked back, and Cora said, "I can pay. I've got a jar of tomatoes —"

"You'll eat with me and I'll eat with you and we'll call the count even," Lula said. "The road's a school, and I'm the teacher's pet, and you're the new girl, and you're going to learn what I learned, which is that a woman alone on the road has two choices, and the first one is to be somebody's, and the second one is to be everybody's friend. I been everybody's friend for six years, and I got a pot of beans and a tent and a tongue, and I ain't never been anybody's, and I mean to keep it that way, and so do you."

That night Cora slept in Lula's tent, on a blanket that smelled of woodsmoke and road, with the old woman's snoring a low constant against the canvas, and she lay awake a long time listening to the sounds of the camp, the fire, the low talk, the far music from the highway café, the sound of the flies settling, the sound of the wind moving over the canvas, and she understood that she had crossed something, that she had come from the farm to the road and from the road to the camp, and that the camp was not a stopping place but a country of its own, with its own laws and its own arithmetic, and that she was going to have to learn the country or be priced in it.

She learned it. She learned it the way her mother had learned the farm, by working it, by getting her hands into it. She took the work the camp offered, the washing, the mending, the carrying, the cooking at the public fire, and she learned the camp the way you learn a field, row by row, and the camp learned her, and by the end of the first week she was not the new girl, she was the girl who could reckon, and the camp's women took her in the way Lula had taken her in, the way women take a girl in, asking nothing and telling everything, and they showed her the seams of the camp, the places where the canvas was thin and the places where the world came through.

The camp at night was the camp she learned best. By day the camp was work and flies and the flat white heat, and the tents stood grey under the sun and the children played in the dust and the women worked the fire and the men were gone to the fields or the road, and it had the look of a town asleep. But at night the camp woke. The fires went up and the talk went round and the music came across the highway from the café, the jukebox, the nickel song, and the young people gathered at the edge of the firelight and the older ones sat close and the whole of the camp became a single listening, and Cora sat in it and learned what the camp was, which was a people holding, a people who had been moved and had gathered here on the line between the country they had left and the country they were going to, and who were holding, night after night, by the only thing they had to hold by, which was each other.

Lula said it to her one night, at the fire, with the beans going and the music thin across the highway.

"The road takes a lot, girl. It takes the land and the house and the children, if the dust gets them, and it takes the men, if the road gets them, and it takes the hope, if you let it. But it don't take the camp. The camp is what's left when the road has taken everything else, and the camp is yours as long as you're in it, and when you go, you take it with you, because the camp is not a place, it's the way we hold. You remember that when you get to the valley. There'll be a camp there too, and it'll be the same camp, and you'll know it by the smell of the beans and the sound of the flies and the way the women hold the children."

Cora lay awake that night in the tent, with the old woman's snoring and the canvas moving in the wind, and she thought of her mother's house, of the kitchen and the ledger and the windbreak rows going down the north quarter, and she understood that her mother had been holding the same way, on the farm, alone, that the farm and the camp were the same thing, that a family holding was a family holding whether the walls were canvas or sod, and that the holding was what the road could not take. She put her hand on the seed packet against her chest, and she made a small vow, in the dark of the camp on the Texas–New Mexico line, that she would carry the holding with her, the way she carried the seed, that she would be the camp for the people she found, the way Lula had been the camp for her.

The night sky over the camp was the first thing she came to love in it. The dust year had hidden the sky from her for as long as she could remember, a grey ceiling over the whole of her childhood, and the camp had a sky the color of dark glass, and the stars came down in it in their thousands, and she lay on her back at the edge of the firelight, out of the way of the camp's work, and she looked up at them, and she thought how her mother's ledger had no column for the stars, and how the sky was the one count no one could keep, and how the stars went on being there, night after night, whether the rain came or not, and the thought was a comfort to her, and she did not know why, and she took it to sleep with her.

She learned the camp's songs, too, by sitting at the fire and listening. The songs were not the songs of the trucks, which were the songs of the road, hungry and quick; the songs of the camp were older, slower, songs about home and water and the rain that used to come, songs the women sang low while the beans went, and Cora learned them the way she had learned the truck songs, by heart, without meaning to, and she found that she carried them the way she carried the seed, that a song learned on the road was a seed that went down in you and came up somewhere else.

In the morning the heat came up white out of the dust, and the flies came with it, and the camp woke to work, and Cora woke in Lula's tent with the blanket smelling of woodsmoke and road, and she went to the pump for her water and stood in her turn and took her share, and she went to the fire and fed it, and the camp took her in for another day, and the road waited at the edge of the camp, going west, and the valley waited beyond it, and she was not afraid.

Chapter 9: Sammy's Gun

She found it on a Tuesday in August, when the heat lay on the farm like a hand and the dust days had begun again, the dry blows that came off the quarter and greyed the kitchen even with the cloths in the cracks. She was changing the sheets on Sammy's bed, the ones he was supposed to change himself, and her hand went under the mattress the way a mother's hand goes under a mattress, looking for nothing, and it found the weight.

She pulled it out and stood with it in the two grey sheets and the heat, and she knew it without looking at it twice. Walter's shotgun. The double-barrel he had brought to the farm in 1919 and used on the rabbits and the prairie chickens in the good years and on nothing at all in the years after, the gun that lived on the nails over the door of the bedroom where Walter used to keep it, and she had seen the empty place on the nails weeks ago and had not counted it, had put it down to Walter having taken it, Walter being gone, the house being what the house was now, a house missing its man. The gun lay in her hands, and it was oiled, it was clean, it had been cared for, and under it in the mattress valley, wrapped in a rag, were three shells.

She stood a long time with the gun in her hands and the shells in the rag and the heat coming through the window, and she did not need to be told what the gun was for. A boy of ten with his father's gun and three shells had a plan. The plan was a rabbit for the pot, or the plan was following Walter, and the one was as dangerous as the other, because a boy who went out the lane with a gun was a boy who could come to harm, and a boy who went out the lane with a gun to follow his father was a boy who would be gone, and June stood in the heat with the gun and the shells and the two plans, and her heart went down in her the way the well had gone down in the dry years, and she held it.

She did not confront him. That was not the way. She put the gun back under the mattress exactly as she had found it, the shells in the rag, the oiled weight of it in the exact place, and she went to the kitchen and she set the kettle on and she waited, and the heat came through the window and lay on the room, and she stood at the stove and kept the count in her the way she kept everything. The count of the days since Walter went: ninety-three. The count of the letters: none. The count of the ways a boy of ten could come to harm in the country without him: she had made the list, and she did not let herself finish it, because a list of that kind had no end. She set the kettle on and she waited, and at noon Sammy came in from the quarter where he had been watering the windbreak, his face grey with dust and the dipper in his hand, and she said, "Sit down and eat, and then we'll talk."

He ate. He ate the way he always ate, careful, and she watched his hands on the plate, his father's hands, wide across the knuckles, and she waited until the plate was clean and then she said, "Sammy. I found the gun."

He did not look up. He set his spoon down flat, the way he did everything, and he sat very still, and she saw the carefulness come up in him like a wall, and he said, "Yes, Ma."

"Whose plan was it?"

"Mine."

"The rabbit or the road?"

He did not answer for a long moment. The dust film greyed the window and the heat lay on the house and the two of them sat at the table across the cleared plates, and she waited the way she waited for the cistern to settle, and at last he said, low, "The rabbit, Ma. We're short of meat."

"And the road?"

He did not answer. He did not need to. The answer sat between them the way Ezra's chair sat between them, full and empty at once, and June understood, with the part of her that understood things before they were said, that the boy had been holding the plan the way she held the ledger, that he had been going to the quarter to water the trees and standing at the end of the lane looking west, that he had been pricing the shells in his head the way she priced the seed corn, that he had been ten years old and alone on the farm with a woman who had been gone into her grief, and that the gun and the three shells had been his arithmetic, his way of making the count come out.

She reached across the table and took his hand. It was a small hand, still, in spite of the man's span across the knuckles, and it lay in hers the way a bird lies in a hand, and she said, "I'm going to take the shells out of the gun, and I'm going to empty them into the dust where no one can find them, and I'm going to keep the gun oiled, because a gun that isn't oiled is a gun that isn't worth anything, and when you're older, when you're a man, it'll be yours, and you'll know what to do with it, and you won't have to make it up in the dark."

"Ma —"

"I'm not angry, Sammy. I'm not angry at the gun and I'm not angry at you. A boy who can make a plan is a boy who can be a man, and I wouldn't take that from you for anything. But the plan was a boy's plan, and the boy's plan would have taken you where you didn't mean to go, and I am not going to lose you to the road the way the road took your father. Do you hear me?"

"Yes, Ma."

"The road doesn't take your father," she said. "Your father went. There's a difference, and it's the difference between a thing that happens to you and a thing you choose, and I need you to know it, because you're going to have choices, and I need you to make them with your eyes open."

He looked at her then. He looked at her the way he had not looked at her since Ezra died, full on, with his whole face, and she saw the question in him before he asked it, and she saw him decide to ask it, and he asked it.

"When's Dad coming back, Ma?"

The question stood in the room. It was the question that had been standing in the room since the truck swallowed Walter in the yard in May, and she had been holding it at the door, and now it was in the room and the room had gone very still with it, the way the room went still when someone said Ezra's name.

"I don't know," she said. She said it plain, and the plainness was the mercy, and she watched his face take it. "He went to find the money, and the money is real, and I believe he'll come back when he has it, or when he's done what he set out to do. But I can't tell you when, and I won't tell you a lie to make it easier, because a lie is a seed that comes up somewhere you didn't plant it."

"He said he'd write," Sammy said. "Every week. The mailbox's been empty."

"The mail goes astray in this country," she said. "You've seen it. The storms hold it, and the roads fill with it, and a letter can sit in a post office in Kansas while a man rides a truck into California. The mailbox being empty is not the same as the man being gone."

"Did he write to you? Before he left, did he say —"

"He said he'd come home when the dust was done," June said. "And I'm holding him to it, and I'm holding you to it, and I'm holding myself to it, and holding is the work of this farm, Sammy. It's the whole of the work. We hold the trees and the seed and the house and each other, and we don't let go, and we don't let the road make the count for us."

That evening she took the gun out from under the mattress and she walked out into the yard with it, and Sammy stood at the door and watched her, and she broke the gun and took out the three shells and held them in her palm a moment, the brass and the red of them, the whole of a boy's plan, and she opened her hand and let them fall into the dust, and they lay there a moment in the grey, and she ground them under her heel into the dust where no one could find them, and she went back in and she oiled the gun, the way she had promised, with the oil rag from the drawer, working the metal and the wood until it shone, and she put it back on the nails over the bedroom door where Walter used to keep it, and she stood a long time looking at it, and the house was quiet, and the heat lay on it, and the gun hung on the nails in the empty place.

Sammy did not ask about the shells. He watched her work the oil rag up and down the barrels the way he had watched her work the ledger, the same slow ceremony, and when she hung the gun on the nails he stood a long moment looking at the place where it had been, and she saw him understand, without being told, that the empty place under the mattress had been filled with something else, that the oiled gun on the nails over the door was a different thing from the hidden gun, that she had taken his plan and returned it to him as a promise, and that the promise had cost her something, and she saw him carry the understanding out to the yard with him and stand at the edge of the dark looking west, and she let him stand, because a boy who has given up a plan needs the dark to put the plan down in, and she had learned that much from watching the well.

Sammy came to the door of the bedroom, and he looked at the gun on the nails, and he looked at her, and he said, "Ma."

"Yes."

"When I'm a man, and the gun's mine —"

"It'll be yours," she said. "And you'll know what to do with it, and you'll make the plan with your eyes open."

He nodded, and he went back to his room, and she heard him get into bed, and she stood in the dark of the kitchen with the gun oiled on the nails and the shells ground into the dust, and she went to the shelf and took down the ledger and opened it and wrote, in her careful hand, the entry for the day:

August 1935. Found the gun under Sammy's bed. Three shells, emptied into the dust. The gun oiled and put up. He asked when his father is coming back. I told him I don't know. We are holding.

She closed the ledger and wrapped it in the oilcloth and put it back on the shelf, and she stood at the window in the dark, and the heat lay on the farm, and the windbreak stood grey on the north quarter, and the road was empty and going west, and she stood and she held, and the holding was the whole of the work.

In the morning the mail brought a penny postcard. It was from Cora, postmarked from a town in New Mexico, and it said, in her daughter's small cramped hand, We are in New Mexico. I am fine. Do not worry. — Cora, and June read it three times and put it in the ledger, in the back, where the letters would live, and she stood at the door with the postcard in her hand and the heat coming up white out of the yard, and the road was empty, and the farm was still, and she held the postcard, and it was not much, it was a penny's worth of paper with four sentences on it, and it was the whole of her daughter's country, sent home, and she put it in her apron pocket, and she went out to water the windbreak, and the trees stood, and she held.

She told Sammy at supper. "Cora's in New Mexico. She's fine. She sent her address on the back, care of the general delivery, and I'm going to write her tonight and tell her the postcard came, and I'm going to tell her about the windbreak and about the chickens and about the weather, and I'm going to ask her if she's eating, because a mother can read a postcard and know what's not on it."

"Will she write back?" Sammy said.

"She will," June said. "The mail goes both ways, or it's not mail. You watch the box, and you'll see."

He watched the box. She saw him go down the lane that evening in the long light and stand at the road's edge with his hands in his pockets, looking west the way the boy with the hidden gun had looked west, and she stood at the kitchen door and watched him, and the windbreak went down the north quarter in the long light, and the trees stood, and the road was empty, and she held the gun on the nails and the shells in the dust and the postcard in the ledger and the boy at the end of the lane, and the holding was the whole of the work.

Chapter 10: The Fruit Camp

The valley took them in the last week of July, and Cora understood, the moment the trucks came over the pass and the country opened out below them, why the road had been full of people going west. She had ridden through a grey country and a red country and a country of high dry rocks, and she had thought she knew what land was, and then the trucks came over the pass into the San Joaquin Valley and the land turned green, and she sat forward in the cab and put her hand on the glass and did not speak.

It was not the green of her memory. It was a green with no dust in it, a green that ran to the mountains on both sides, orchards in rows that went on past counting, the trees low and dark and heavy, and beyond the orchards the fields, and beyond the fields the mountains blue in the heat, and the whole of it watered, the whole of it alive, the whole of it a country that had not been buried, and Cora sat in the cab with the green going past the window and she could not find the words for it, and Dutch looked at her and said, "There's your California. Ain't it something."

"It's a different world," she said.

"It's the same world with water in it," Dutch said. "You'll learn the difference."

The camp was not the camp on the Texas line. It was an orchard camp, the tents pitched in rows between the peach trees, the canvas gone grey with the road and the new sun, and the fruit heavy on the branches overhead, and the air so thick with it you could taste it, the sweetness and the dust of the road and the heat, and Cora stood in the middle of the camp with her suitcase and her jar and her seed packet and looked at the peaches above her head, whole trees of them, red and gold and low enough to touch, and she had to stop herself from reaching, because a girl who picks the crop she is there to pick is a girl who learns the difference between what hangs and what's yours.

The camp had its own rules, and the first of them was the piece rate. The foreman explained it to the new hands the first morning, standing at the edge of the orchard with a tally book and a pencil, a man called Earl, with the sun gone to leather in his neck and a way of looking at the hands like a man looking at a row of boxes. "You pick by the box," he said. "You fill a box, you get a ticket, the ticket's worth so much, the weighmaster at the scale writes it down. You work fast, you eat. You work slow, you don't. It's simple as that."

Cora stood at the back of the crowd and listened, and she did the arithmetic the way she did everything, the way the one-armed man had taught her at the fire on the Texas line, and the arithmetic said the rate was low, and she kept the knowledge and did not say it, because a new hand who tells the foreman the rate is low is a hand who learns what the scale weighs.

She picked with Rosa Delgado. Rosa was sixteen, from a family that had come out of the same country Cora had come out of, though Rosa's family had left a year earlier and Rosa's father had found work in the packing sheds and Rosa's mother kept the tent and Rosa picked to keep the family's count coming out even, and she took Cora in the way Lula had taken her in, the way women take a girl in, asking nothing and telling everything. Rosa taught her the picking. She taught her how to fill a box without bruising the fruit, how to read the blush that meant a peach was ready and the look that meant it would hold on the tree another day, how to work a ladder fast without wasting a step, how to carry a full box down the row without spilling, how to tie the ticket into her kerchief so the sweat didn't take the pencil, and she taught her the arithmetic that the foreman did not put in the tally book, the real arithmetic, the one the camp ran on.

"Watch the scale," Rosa said. They were at the edge of the orchard, squatting in the shade of the peach rows at the noon break, eating the bread Rosa's mother had sent, and Rosa spoke low, the way the camp spoke when it spoke of the scale. "The weighmaster, he weighs the box with the fruit. He takes the tare off for the box, but he takes it off light, and the fruit is what the grower pays for, so a light tare is a heavy cheat. And the box, when it's full, the fruit settles on the way to the scale, and a settled box weighs short, and the short is yours, not his. You learn the scale, Cora, or the scale learns you."

"How do you beat it?"

"You can't beat it. You learn it. You learn what a full box weighs in your arms, and you pack it a little over, so when it settles it comes out right, and you learn the weighmaster's thumb, and you set your box down on the scale yourself, and you watch the needle, and if the needle says short, you say, 'Tip it out, weigh it again,' and you say it loud, so the other hands hear, because a cheat in the light is a cheat that costs him, and a cheat in the dark is a cheat that costs you."

Cora looked at the girl. Rosa was sixteen and she talked like a woman who had been doing arithmetic against a stacked scale for a year, and Cora said, "You learned all this in a year?"

"I learned it in a season," Rosa said. "The season teaches you. The fruit teaches you, and the scale teaches you, and the camp teaches you, and the grower teaches you whether you want to learn or not. You're going to learn it, Cora. You've got the look."

"What look?"

"The look that says you're going to count it all the way down before you believe it. My papa has the same look. My mama says it's the look of a person who's been cheated once and won't be again."

Cora thought of her mother's ledger, of the weather column, of the count that had been coming out short for five years, and she said, "I was raised to it."

The heat was the thing. She had known heat, had lived in it all her life, the white heat of the plains, but the valley heat was a different animal, a wet heat that came up off the irrigated ground and lay on you like a hand, and the first week Cora thought she would die of it, and she did not die of it, she learned it, the way she had learned the dust, and she learned to drink at the water barrel the camp kept at the row end, and she learned to work in the early cool and the late cool and to hold the middle of the day in the shade, and she learned the rhythms of the camp, the dawn rising and the noon rest and the evening fire, and she learned the sound of the camp at night, the same sound she had learned on the Texas line, the low talk and the far music and the wind moving in the peach leaves overhead, and she slept under the fruit and dreamed of her mother's ledger, of the columns going green.

The company store was the camp's second lesson, and it taught her the arithmetic Rosa had not named. The store stood at the edge of the camp, a board building with the grower's name painted over the door and a ledger of its own behind the counter, and it was the only store within walking distance, and it sold the flour and the lard and the coffee and the boots and the cloth that the camp could not carry from home, and it took the tickets the scale paid, and it kept the accounts, and the accounts were kept in the store's favor, and Cora learned that lesson the way she had learned the scale, by watching a man set down a month's picking and carry away a sack of flour and a can of coal oil and stand at the door with the arithmetic of it in his hands and no change in his pocket. She learned the store the way she was learning everything, and she kept the knowledge the way she kept the seed packet, against the day she would need it, and she wrote her mother nothing about it in the letters she composed at night and did not send, because the letters she sent were the letters that lied about the food, and a letter could not carry the weight of the store and stay a letter.

The camp fire taught her the rest. It was the same fire she had sat at on the Texas line, the same pot, the same women holding the same children, and she sat in it the second week and learned the camp's arithmetic the way she had learned the scale's, by listening, and what the fire taught her was the piece rate in its true shape. A box of peaches paid a few cents. A good hand could fill thirty boxes in a long day, and thirty boxes at a few cents was not the money the road had promised, and the difference went somewhere, and the somewhere was the grower's ledger, and the grower's ledger had a column for the fruit and a column for the water and a column for the land and a column for the hands, and the hands were the bottom column, the column that came out short, and Cora sat at the fire and listened to the men do the arithmetic aloud, the way her mother did the arithmetic aloud at the kitchen table, and she understood that the same book was being kept in both countries, that the ledger of the farm and the ledger of the valley were the same ledger, and that the bottom column was the same column, and that her mother had been right all along, that the count was the count no matter where you stood on it.

There was a man at the fire who did the arithmetic in a different way. She had seen him the first night, sitting a little apart with a pencil and a board, and she had thought he was the one-armed schoolmaster come west with the camp, but he was not. He was a younger man, thirty or so, with a face that had been handsome before something had crossed it, and there was a scar on his jaw, a pale seam of old skin, and he sat at the plank table the camp had set up between two trees and he taught, in the evenings, by lamplight, and Cora had watched from the edge and had not gone close, and then one night she had gone close.

He was teaching the grower's arithmetic. She stood at the edge of the lamplight and she heard him, and it was the arithmetic of the pay stub, the arithmetic of the deductions, and she heard him do what she had done in her head on the day Earl had explained the piece rate, only he did it on paper, and he did it in front of the camp, and the numbers went up on the board in his careful hand, and the camp watched him the way a congregation watches a preacher, and he went through it line by line, the wages and the tent rent and the water and the company store and the weighing-in fees, and when he was done the board told the truth of what a hand took home, and the truth was small, and the camp sat in the lamplight with the truth and did not speak.

He looked up when he was done, and his eyes found her where she stood at the edge of the light, the new girl, the one with the look, and he said, "You're the one they say can reckon. What did I leave out?"

She felt the whole of the fire turn to her, and she had not spoken to more than two people since the camp took her in, and she stood in the lamplight and she did the count, the way her mother had taught her, the way the one-armed man had taught her, the way the scale had taught her, and she said, "You left out the weighmaster's thumb. A light tare and a settled box, and a hand loses a box in five that the paper never shows. You can't put the thumb in the stub, because the thumb isn't a deduction, it's the difference between what the scale says and what the scale weighs."

The man looked at her a long moment. The scar on his jaw caught the lamplight. Something moved in his face that was not quite a smile, and he said, "Then the arithmetic has taught you what it taught me, which is that the paper is not the whole of the count, and the count you keep with your own hands is the one you can trust." He held out the pencil. "Set down. I'm Miguel Otero. The board's yours if you want it."

She set down. She had not meant to, but she set down, and the lamplight came up on the plank table, and the camp settled around the fire, and the peaches hung heavy overhead in the dark, and she sat at the board with the pencil in her hand and the whole of her mother's ledger behind her and the whole of the valley in front of her, and she learned, that night, the first thing of a year of things: that a man could do the grower's arithmetic and do it true, and that the truth was a thing you could set down on paper and hold up to the lamplight, and that a girl with a pencil and a board could be worth something the scale could not weigh.

Chapter 11: Miguel's Lesson

The plank table stood under the biggest peach tree, and the peaches were gone, but the tree still threw a square of shade at evening that the pickers used like a roof. Someone had nailed three sawhorses together with a door on top. Lula had hung an oil lamp from a branch by a length of wire, and the lamp swayed whenever the wind came off the rows, setting the shadow of the table walking.

Cora sat at the end of it with her pay stub. She had been paid that afternoon for the first time, and the stub was a small brown paper slip with numbers on it that did not add up to the money in her fist. She had added them four ways on her thumb and got four different answers, and none of them was what Earl the foreman had handed her.

The day behind her had been a long one, the sun up over the rows before five and the ladders warm by the time the dew burned off, her sack slung on her hip and the peaches coming off clean into her palm if she did it right, the twist and the lift, the way Rosa had shown her on the first morning. She had filled her sack three times and emptied it into the crate each time, and the foreman had walked the line and marked his book, and her hands were stained dark to the wrist and her back had its own arithmetic by evening, a column of aches she could read without numbers. It was honest work, the way the dirt and the sun and the fruit were honest, and the money in her fist had felt like a real thing, a beginning. Then she had unfolded the stub and the beginning had shrunk.

"You keep doing that," said Miguel, "you'll wear the paper out before the work does."

He sat across from her, his elbows on the door, a stub of pencil in his fingers. The scar on his jaw caught the lamp light, a pale seam running from his ear to his chin, and Cora had learned in two weeks not to stare at it and not to ask. Rosa said it came from a fight in a cotton field somewhere south, but Rosa also said a man who taught arithmetic at a plank table at night had to have a story, and stories cost more than they were worth.

"Show me," Cora said.

He took her stub and laid it flat between them. "This is the ticket. This is the law of the camp, and the law of the law is that you cannot read it by lamplight and you cannot keep it." He turned it over. "See the back. They pay you in tickets. The tickets you cash at the store."

Cora knew the tickets. The store took them like money and gave back less money's worth. She had watched a man buy a can of beans with a day's picking and come away with it in his pocket and nothing else. She had watched a woman pay her whole week across the counter in tickets and leave with a sack of flour and a tin of lard and her hands empty, and the clerk had not smiled and the woman had not met his eyes, and Cora had stood at the door of the store and felt the arithmetic settle on her like dust she could not brush off.

"Now." He flattened the stub with his knuckles. "Fifty-three cents a hundredweight. That's what you picked today, a hundredweight and a quarter, so the arithmetic should be yours." He wrote in the air with the pencil, not on the paper, drawing numbers the size of his hand that Cora could follow. "But here is the weigh-in. They weigh you going out to the rows and coming back, they weigh your sack empty and full, and they take the empty sack's weight off the full one, and that is fair. Then they take the dirt off." He paused. "You picked clean today?"

"I picked clean."

"The foreman's thumb says you picked dirty. That's the dirt they take, half a pound here, a pound there, whatever the thumb decides." He wrote the number in the air. "That comes off the top, before your cents. Then the rent for the tent."

Cora had not signed for the tent. Nobody had signed for the tent. The rent came out of her pay and she had never seen the paper.

"Then the water. Then the ice. Then the store bill for the week, because you cannot pay the store with nothing and the store will not wait." He wrote each number, patient as a teacher with a slow child, and Cora watched the row of them grow until they ate the fifty-three cents and started on the next day's.

"That's theft," she said.

"It's arithmetic," said Miguel. "The growers would call it the cost of doing business. The men call it the cut. Nobody calls it theft out loud, because out loud is how you end up in the blacklist file."

Cora looked at her mother's ledger the way you look at a thing you have finally understood. June had a book of seeds and weather, columns for what was bought and saved and planted and lost. Cora had run her finger down those columns a hundred times, watching the years bleed red, and she had always read them as her mother's stubbornness, her mother's faith that numbers could hold a farm together when nothing else would. Now she saw it plain. Her mother had been counting out the same arithmetic her whole life, the cut, the thumb, the rent, and she had never once called it theft, she had only written it down.

"You're like my mother," Cora said.

Miguel raised his eyebrows. The scar moved with them, the way a crease moves in a dry field. "I don't know your mother."

"She keeps a ledger. Seeds and weather and what's owed. She writes down every loss so it can't be lost twice." Cora turned the stub over in her fingers. "I thought it was religion. It's arithmetic."

"Those are the same thing," said Miguel, "in a country that doesn't want you to do either."

The lamp swung. Somewhere down the rows a harmonica started, the same tune the jukebox played at the highway café, and Cora knew the nickel song by now, the one the world seemed to play wherever she stopped. She had put a nickel in the machine herself in Amarillo and stood watching the record turn, and the song had gone into her and stayed, and now it followed her down the rows at night through somebody else's mouth.

A man across the table leaned in, a thin man with a bandage on his hand who had been listening, and pushed his own ticket across the door. "Here. Show her the water. Show her what the water costs."

Miguel took the ticket and held it up to the lamp. "This is your week," he said, half to the man, half to Cora. "Seven days. The water truck comes twice a day and sells out of the back at two cents a gallon, and the store sells you the same water at a nickel, and the tent doesn't have a pipe, so you buy what you can't live without." He wrote the number. "That's the water. That's not the well. The well belongs to the grower, and the grower doesn't sell the well, only the road to it."

The thin man nodded, not surprised, and took his ticket back and folded it into his shirt like a holy thing.

"Who taught you arithmetic?" Cora asked.

"A man in Las Vegas, New Mexico. He had a schoolroom and a blackboard and a row of desks and thirty children, and he taught them numbers first, because numbers are the only thing a poor man is allowed to own and keep." Miguel's eyes went somewhere past her, to the dark between the trees. "I taught numbers for nine years."

"You taught school?"

"I taught school till I taught too much." He said it flat, the way a man says a date, and then he was quiet, and Cora understood that the story had a door in it and the door was not open. Rosa had been right. Stories cost more than they were worth.

"I'm sorry," Cora said.

"Don't be. I'm a better man for it." He tapped the stub. "The point of the lesson is not the lesson. The point is that you counted it four ways and got four answers, and you will learn to count it one way, the honest way, and then you will know what they take and what they leave. A man who can read his own ticket is a man who cannot be cheated quiet. That's all I teach. That's all anybody should have to teach."

Cora looked down at the numbers. She had been afraid of him for a week, the way you're afraid of anything that watches you too long, and now she was afraid of something else entirely. She was afraid of how easily she could learn the thing he was offering, and how much it would cost her to carry it.

"I'm a fast learner," she said.

"I know." He smiled, and the scar shortened. "You've been counting my lessons in your head all week. I saw you at the scale, the day you caught Earl's thumb. You didn't say anything, but you counted."

Cora felt the heat come up her neck. She had not known anybody saw that.

"You did right not to say it," he said. "Talking means the blacklist. Counting is free. Counting is yours." He slid the pencil across the table toward her. "Keep this. Your hand should be busy at night, and a busy hand is a quiet heart."

She took the pencil. It was a yellow pencil, worn to the nub, the wood soft at one end from his teeth. She held it the way you hold a thing you mean to keep.

"Tomorrow," he said, "bring your ticket and we'll do it again. Numbers are like seeds. You plant them once, and they keep coming up."

The harmonica quit, and in the quiet the camp settled, the tents coughing, the wire of the lamp humming. Cora walked back to the tent she shared with Rosa and Lula, the pencil in her fist, the stub folded in her pocket. Rosa was already curled on her cot, breathing slow. Lula sat by the door, mending a sack by the last of the light.

"Learned anything?" Lula asked.

"He taught me my ticket," Cora said. "He said a man who can read his own ticket can't be cheated quiet."

Lula bit a thread. "He's right. But he didn't tell you the other half."

"What other half?"

"That you can read your ticket clean and still go hungry." Lula threaded the needle and held the sack up to the light. "Arithmetic don't put meat in the pot. It only tells you who took it." She looked at Cora over the sack. "You like him."

"I like the lesson."

"Same thing," said Lula, and Cora could not tell if she was teasing or warning, and she could not decide if it mattered.

In the dark, before sleep, Cora took out the seed packet from her coat. It was flat and soft, the paper creased where it had been folded and refolded. June's writing on the front: garden beans, 1934, saved. Cora did not open it. She had not opened it since the farm, and she did not know why she carried it, only that her mother had folded it into her hand the morning she left, the way you press a coin into a child's palm for a journey, and Cora had hidden it in her coat before the truck was out of the lane.

She put it back, and then she took the pencil Miguel had given her and held it, and she thought about the two of them together, the seed and the pencil, the thing from home and the thing from the road, and she fell asleep with her hand curled around one and the other under her pillow, and the camp wind moved the tent wall in and out like breathing.

In the morning there was a new entry to be learned. Earl weighed the sacks, and the foreman's thumb came down on the scale as it always did, and Cora stood at the line and counted, and she did not say a word. But she held the number in her head like a coin in her fist, and at evening she went to the plank table and laid her ticket down in front of Miguel, and the second lesson began.

It was late when the lamp burned out and the camp went dark, and Cora sat a moment in the dark with the pencil in her hand and the number safe in her head, and she felt the small fierce joy of it, the way you feel the first time you can name a thing that has been done to you. She did not know it yet, but she had begun to learn the arithmetic that would carry her home, the arithmetic of what was taken and what was kept, and the seed packet in her coat was already, without her knowing, the other half of the equation. The tent wall moved in the wind. The wind had a taste in it now, faint and green, the taste of the valley at night, and Cora lay down and closed her fist around the pencil and counted the day's numbers once more, slow, the honest way, and they added up at last.

Chapter 12: The Grower's Scale

The scale stood at the edge of the orchard under a canvas awning, a great iron thing on wheels, and the line of pickers moved past it all day like water past a rock. Each woman came up with her sack, each man with his crate, and the weighmaster set the weight with his hand on the bar and called it out, and the clerk wrote it in the book, and the picker moved on. It took less than a minute for each of them. It took the whole day for the line to move.

Cora had learned to watch the weighmaster's thumb. It was not a thing anybody taught her. It was a thing she saw, the way you see a stone in a furrow — the thumb rode the bar as the weight settled, and when it rode down it added a pound or two to nothing. She had watched it for three weeks and she had said nothing, because Lula had said talking means the blacklist, and Cora had seen a man's name in the blacklist file once, seen the way it was written there like a brand, and she did not want her name in any file.

But on a September morning when the heat came off the orchard in waves and the peaches were small and the picking was thin, the weighmaster's thumb came down harder than she had ever seen it, and he called out a weight that was not on the bar, and the woman in front of Cora — a girl not much older than Rosa, with a baby tied on her back — heard the number and looked at her sack and said, quiet, "That's not my weight."

The weighmaster didn't look at her. "Move on."

"That's not my weight," the girl said again, louder. "I picked a hundred and nine pounds this morning. You called a hundred and one."

"Move on or I'll call it ninety."

The girl stood there with the baby on her back and the sack at her feet, and the whole line went still, and Cora felt the words come up her throat like a thing she could not swallow. She had counted. She had counted every time, holding the numbers in her head the way Miguel had taught her, and this girl's sack was a hundred and nine pounds. Cora had watched it go on the scale, had watched the bar sit at the hundred-and-nine mark, had watched the thumb push it back to a hundred and one.

"That's not her weight," Cora said.

She said it before she meant to, the way you step on a nail before you see it. The line turned to look at her. The girl with the baby turned. The weighmaster's eyes came up from the bar and found her, and Cora saw them mark her face the way Earl's had marked her the day she caught his thumb, the way a man writes a name in a book he means to keep.

"Nobody asked you," said the weighmaster.

"The bar sat at a hundred and nine," Cora said. "I saw it. Her sack is a hundred and nine pounds, and you called a hundred and one, and your thumb is still on the bar."

The silence was the kind you can hold in your hand. Somewhere in the rows a man's voice called, and a horse stamped, and the canvas of the awning popped once in the heat. Then Earl the foreman was there, walking up the line with his clipboard, and the line opened for him like water.

"What's the trouble?" Earl asked. He did not look at Cora. He looked at the weighmaster, and something passed between them that did not need to be spoken.

"Girl says the scale's wrong," said the weighmaster.

Earl turned to Cora. He was a big man gone soft, his shirt damp at the chest, and his eyes were flat the way a cat's are flat when it has decided. "You the one been talking to that Mexican."

It was not a question. Cora's throat closed.

"Re-weigh it," she said. It came out smaller than she wanted, and she hated it.

Earl looked at the girl with the baby, and at the sack, and at the line of pickers standing in the heat, and he smiled the way a man smiles at a horse that has kicked. "Re-weigh it," he said. "Let the girl have her weigh." He nodded at the weighmaster, and the weighmaster put his hand on the sack and lifted it onto the scale, and this time his thumb stayed off the bar, and the bar sat at a hundred and nine.

"There," said Earl. "Satisfied? The girl gets her hundred and nine. Now everybody back to work, and let's not have any more trouble."

The line moved. The girl with the baby took her ticket and went, and she did not look back, and nobody looked at Cora, and Cora stood in the line with the heat coming up off the ground and understood that she had won nothing at all. She had cost the girl her morning, herself her place, and the weighmaster his face, and all three of those were debts that would be collected.

At noon the girl found her at the water barrel. The baby was asleep against her chest, a scrap of cloth over its head against the sun, and the girl stood a long moment without speaking, working her hands, and then she said, "I don't know your name."

"Cora."

"I'm Elsa." The girl's eyes were pale and dry, the eyes of a woman who had decided not to cry. "I didn't thank you. I couldn't. You understand."

"I understand."

"It's not that I'm not grateful. It's that gratitude's a thing I can't afford." She looked past Cora, at the rows. "I got a mark now too, same as you. We're the same kind of poor, you and me — the kind that can't keep our mouths shut when the arithmetic's wrong." She turned and walked away, and the baby's foot swung against her hip, and Cora watched her go and felt the small cold thing settle in her chest where her supper should have been.

She picked that afternoon with Rosa, the two of them two rows over and the foreman walking the line behind them, and Rosa said, low, without turning: "You shouldn't have said it."

"I know."

"They'll watch you now. Earl writes names in that book of his, and the book follows you down the valley. One mark and you're done at every orchard from here to Fresno."

Cora twisted a peach off the branch, the clean twist and lift that felt good in her hands, and put it in her sack. "It was her weight. She had a baby on her back. She can't afford to be cheated."

"The baby makes it worse," said Rosa. "The baby's why she can't fight back. You fought for her and now she owes you and can't pay, and that's the meanest arithmetic there is, because it turns a kindness into a debt."

Cora had not thought of it that way. She stood a moment with her hand on a branch, the green leaves moving in the heat, and she thought about her mother, who kept a ledger of every seed and every loss, who wrote down the weather like scripture, and she wondered if her mother had ever said the words out loud, and whether saying them had ever done her any good, or whether the ledger was just the place where the words went to die quiet.

That evening Lula found her at the plank table. Miguel was not there yet, and the lamp was not lit, and the camp was cooking supper in the last of the light, the smoke going up straight and thin, and the smell of beans and coffee came down the rows and Cora was not hungry and knew she ought to be, because hunger was a thing you could not afford to skip, any more than gratitude.

"Heard what you did," Lula said.

Cora did not look up. "Everybody's heard. The whole orchard heard."

"Earl's book heard." Lula sat down across from her, heavy, the way she sat when her knees hurt. "You got a mark in it now, girl. I seen him write it. The rest of us, we been carrying our marks for years, and we know how to carry them low where they don't show. You just carried yours up high where everybody could see, and that's the one thing you can't do."

"I know."

Lula reached across the table and took Cora's chin in her hand, the way you take a child's chin to make her look at you. "You're a good girl with a sharp eye and a sharper tongue, and both of them are going to get you hurt or get you out. This country don't pay for sharp eyes, girl. It pays for eyes that can look away." She let go. "Now you mind me. From here on you count your own weight and keep it in your head, and you let the rest of the world cheat itself quiet. That's the way we live, and it ain't shame, it's arithmetic."

"It's my mother's arithmetic," Cora said. "She writes everything down. She never lets a loss go unwritten."

"Then your mother's got the right idea and the wrong country," said Lula, "and I'd like to meet a woman like that, because she's a rarer thing than rain."

The lamp was lit, and Miguel came up the row with his pencil and his door and his quiet way, and he sat down across from Cora without looking at her, and the camp settled into its evening. He took out a small brown envelope from his pocket and laid it on the table, and Cora saw it was the blacklist file, the one that went up on the labor office wall, the names written in the clerk's hand.

"What's that for?" she asked.

"Tonight's lesson," he said. "Open it."

Cora opened it. There was one sheet of paper, and on the sheet there were four names, and the fourth name was hers. Cora Vogel. The letters were plain and black and looked back at her like the eyes of a thing in the dark.

"Earl writes fast," Miguel said. "He had it in the file before supper. That's how fast it travels, when it's meant to." He took the sheet and turned it over and laid it back on the table, and he took his pencil and began to write on the blank side, numbers, the same patient numbers he always wrote. "Now we learn what the mark means, and what it doesn't mean. The mark means you can't work this valley with a clean name. It doesn't mean you can't work. It doesn't mean you're wrong. It means the grower's scale measures a different weight than yours, and it always will, and you've got to learn to live in the difference."

"I said what I saw," Cora said.

"I know you did." Miguel looked up at her then, and his eyes were tired and something else, something that made Cora look down at her hands. "I watched you say it. It was the bravest stupid thing I've seen a girl do in this camp, and I've seen a lot of them." He wrote a line of numbers and pushed the paper to her. "Here. This is how you read the file. This is how you know what it costs, and this is how you keep working anyway. The thumb is not the scale, girl. The scale is the thumb and the weights and the line of pickers and the man who reads the book, and you can't fight the whole machine with one word. But you can count." He tapped the pencil. "You can always count."

Cora took the pencil. It was the one he had given her, and she had kept it sharpened, and she wrote the numbers he had written, copying them, and the numbers lay down on the paper in a row like seeds in a furrow, and she felt the small cold thing in her chest loosen a little.

"You watch the weigh line tomorrow," he said. "Count every sack that crosses. Not to fight. Just to know. Knowing is a thing they can't take from you."

The next morning the line moved as it always moved, the pickers going up the orchard like water past a rock, and Cora took her place two rows over from Rosa and did not look at the scale. She picked, and she counted her own weight, and she kept it in her head, and when the weighmaster called out a number that was not on the bar, she said nothing. She counted the difference and held it, the way you hold a stone in your pocket to remind yourself the ground is hard, and she carried it through the day, and at evening she went to the plank table and laid her ticket down, and the lamp swung, and the lesson went on.

The wind moved through the orchard all night, and by morning the dust had settled on the canvas awning of the scale, a fine grey grit that lay on the iron like the years, and the weighmaster came out and wiped it off with his sleeve and set his thumb on the bar, and the line began.

Chapter 13: June Alone

The well was eleven years old and the wind had taken the little roof off it twice, and June kept a board nailed over the top and a stone on the board, and on the days the dust was up she kept the board down and drew the bucket from the side gap she had cut, so the wind could not sift the grit down into the water. She was a woman who had learned to keep her water and her hope out of the wind's mouth.

September came on hot and dry, the way August had gone out, and the farm lay under a grey sky that was not a cloud sky but a dust sky, high and thin and yellow at the edges. June watered the windbreak rows in the cool of the evening, the cistern low, and she measured the cottonwoods with her eye against the mark she had cut in the well rope. They had put on a hand's worth of growth, the willows more, the Russian olive more still. She wrote it in the ledger on the back page, the page she had kept blank against just this reckoning, and the numbers lay down small and steady like the rows themselves.

Sammy was gone a good part of most days, down the fence line with his slingshot and, she suspected, the .22 he was not allowed to have. She had emptied the shells out of it once into the dust and told him a gun was a thing you respected or you did not have, and he had looked at her with Walter's eyes and said nothing, and she knew he would find more shells somewhere, because boys find shells the way water finds the low places. She did not forbid the fence line. She forbade the road. The road was a thing she could forbid.

The house was quieter than it had ever been. Walter had taken his voice west with the trucks in May, and Cora had taken hers in June, and what was left was the tick of the mantel clock and the creak of the pump and her own breath, and the silence sat in the rooms like a visitor who had decided to stay. June found herself talking to the chickens, and to the windbreak when she watered it, and once, shamefaced, to Ezra's chair, the small chair that stood in the corner with nothing in it. She did not say anything to it. She just stood in the doorway of that room a moment, the way she did, and let the fact of the chair be a fact, and went on.

On the dust days she washed the dishes and the grit stayed. She would wash the cups twice, three times, run her finger around the rim and find the grit, and set them on the shelf and know the dust would find them again before supper. She hung the wet cloths at the door and they turned grey-brown by noon, and she took them down and rinsed them and hung them again, and the ritual of it was a kind of prayer, the same movements day after day, the same small battle that could not be won and had to be fought.

The almanac was her scripture now. She read it at the table after supper, when the light was going and the lamp was lit, and she read the planting dates and the weather signs and the phases of the moon, and she marked the days off with a pencil in the margin, and the act of marking gave her something the church had never quite given her, a sense that the world ran on a calendar you could hold in your hand. The almanac said the winter would be hard and the spring uncertain, and June read it and nodded, because she had read the same thing in the sky and in her own lungs.

The county relief man came the second week of September. He drove a grey Ford with a star on the door, and he had a ledger of his own, and he sat at the kitchen table with his hat on his knee and told June the county was offering relief seed grain to the farmers who would sign for it, wheat and a little barley, enough to plant a section, against the hope of a spring rain.

June looked at him across the table. "I've never taken relief."

"It's not charity, ma'am," the man said. "It's seed. The county wants to see the ground planted. A planted field is a field that holds, and a field that holds is less dust in the air and less dust in the lungs, and the county figures a man who plants is a man who plans to stay."

The word stayed hung in the air. June thought of Walter, and of the trucks going west, and of the empty chair in the corner of the next room, and she put her hand flat on the table and looked at it, the knuckles red, the wedding ring worn thin, and she said, "I'll sign."

The man wrote in his ledger, and he asked about the windbreak, and June told him about the double row of cottonwood and willow and Russian olive, the extra tree she had planted in the spring for Ezra and not explained, and the man nodded and said he had heard about the shelterbelt program, the government's own windbreak running north and south through the whole country, and he said, "You plant yours on the home place and the government plants theirs across the nation, and the two of them holding the dirt is the same act." He did not sound like a man reading from a paper. He sounded like a man who had seen the dirt go up.

Before he left he stood on the porch and looked out over the farm, the bare fields going grey in the haze, and he said, "You've lost a son." It was not a question.

June did not ask how he knew. A woman running a farm alone with a boy of ten and an empty chair in the corner of a room she did not enter was a woman whose losses were written on her face, the way a field's losses are written in its fence lines.

"Yes," she said.

"Don't plant the wheat where you buried him," the man said. "I seen too many men do that. They plant over the grave thinking it's a kind of keeping, and it's a kind of forgetting, and the crop never comes up right." He put his hat on and went down the steps. "The dead want to be remembered in the open, where the wind can get at it. Same as the living."

June stood on the porch and watched the grey Ford go down the lane, and she thought about the windbreak, and the extra tree she had planted for Ezra in the open, where the wind could get at it, and she did not know if she had done the man's kind of keeping or the other kind, and she decided it did not matter, because the tree had gone into the ground in April and it was still alive, and that was all a keeping could be asked to do.

He left a sack of wheat in the barn, and June stood in the barn door a long time looking at it, a plain burlap sack with the county's stamp, and she thought about all the seed she had ever planted, the saved seed and the bought seed and the seed that had blown away in the storms, and she thought about what it meant to plant on the word of a government that had no guarantee of rain. Faith, she thought. That's all it's ever been. Plant a seed and the land will answer. It's the only prayer I've got left that works.

That night she wrote the ledger entry. Weather column: hot, dry, dust high. Seed column: relief wheat, one sack, county. And in the margin, small, she wrote: Ezra would be ten this fall. She did not know why she wrote it. The ledger was for seed and weather. But the pencil had moved, and the line was there, and she left it.

The chickens laid less as the month wore on, and June fed them the cracked corn she had saved and watched them scratch in the dust of the yard, and she talked to them because they were the only things that answered, and she told them about the wheat in the barn and the windbreak's growth and the color of the sky, and the rooster stood on the fence and turned his head and looked at her with one eye, and she laughed, and the sound of her own laugh in the empty yard startled her, and she looked around to see if anyone had heard.

Sammy came in at dusk with a rabbit, the first of the season, and June skinned it at the pump and saved the hide for the rag box and made a stew, and at the table Sammy asked, the way he asked most nights, "When's Dad coming back?"

June measured the words the way she measured seed. "I don't know, Sammy."

"He's been gone four months."

"Five," said June. "He's been gone five months."

"He said he'd write."

"He did write. Twice. The mail's been bad, is all." She did not say that the second letter had come from some place called the Imperial Valley, and that the first had come from a wheat town in Kansas, and that the two letters had not sounded like the same man. She did not say that Walter's handwriting had gone thin and that she had read the thinness the way she read the almanac, as a sign of a hard season coming. She said, "Your father's working. The harvests move west and he moves with them. He'll write when he can."

Sammy ate his stew and said nothing, and after supper he took his slingshot and went out to the fence line and stood there in the dusk looking down the road that went to the highway, and June watched him from the kitchen window and did not call him in, because a boy standing at a fence line looking down a road was a thing she could not forbid and would not lie to.

That night the wind came up and the dust came with it, and June got up and walked the house in her bare feet, checking the wet cloths at the doors, the windows, the cracks in the walls she had stuffed with newspaper. She stood at the north window a long time, looking out at the dark where the windbreak stood, and she thought about the extra tree, the one for Ezra, and about whether it had held, and about the arithmetic she had lived by for twenty years, the arithmetic of seeds and weather and losses written down so they could not be lost twice.

Loneliness, she had learned, was a physical thing. It sat in the chest like a stone, and it made the rooms bigger and the light dimmer, and it had a taste, a taste like the dust, fine and dry at the back of the throat. She had not known a person could be lonely in a house full of her own children's furniture and her own children's shoes in the corner, but she knew it now, the way you know a winter is coming not by the calendar but by the ache in an old bone. She stood at the window and let the loneliness be what it was, and she did not fight it, because fighting it was a thing she had learned to stop doing, the way you learn to stop fighting the wind and start banking the house against it.

The house had been built for six. It had been built for a man and a woman and four children and the noise of them, the doors, the boots, the arguments in the kitchen, the scuff of a boy's feet on the stairs at midnight for a drink of water. June had not known, when she stood in the kitchen and told Walter she would not sell and would not go, that she was also telling the house to stand empty of its own weather. The rooms had kept their furniture and lost their sound, and sound, she had learned, was the thing a house ran on, the way a windmill runs on the wind. She had felt foolish the day she caught herself talking to the chickens. She felt less foolish now, and more careful, and the carefulness was a thing she carried the way she carried the ledger, close to her chest, where the wind could not get at it.

In the morning the dust had laid a fine line along the windowsills, and June took the cloth and wiped it into her hand and held it, the grit of her own land, the thing that had taken the crops and the topsoil and the color out of the year, and she carried it to the yard and let it go, and the wind took it back. She went to the barn and stood in the door and looked at the county's sack of wheat, and she put her hand on the burlap and felt the seed inside, patient, waiting for a rain that had not promised itself.

She wrote the day's entry in the ledger: the weather column, the windbreak column, the small margin line that was not for seed. Then she closed the book and put it on the shelf between the canning jars, and she stood a moment looking at the row of jars, the tomatoes from a year that had given a little, and she thought: we are still here. The words came to her the way they had come in April, unbidden, the way a windbreak's shadow falls, and she let them lie where they fell, and she went out to the well to draw the water for the windbreak, the stone on the board, the board on the well, her hands on the rope, the dust already settling on the water she had not yet drawn.

Chapter 14: The Neighbor's Axe

The first hard frost came the second week of October, on a wind out of the north that flattened the last of the garden and laid a skin of ice on the water pail by morning. June went out at first light and stood on the porch in her coat and watched the smoke go up from the Halloran chimney, thin and dark against the pale sky, and she counted the distance between the two houses, three fields of grey stubble, and she thought about the Halloran woodpile, which she had not seen stacked this year, and she went back inside and said nothing.

The woodpile told the story of a farm the way the ledger told the story of a season. June had seen woodpiles go the way of hard times before, on this same road: the year of the blowout, when the Murphy place went to town with their wood in the back of the wagon and their furniture standing in the yard; the year of the hoppers, when half the farms on the creek sold their fences for firewood and the fields went open. A man's woodpile was his winter written down in cords, and the Hallorans' had not been written this year, and June read the empty place the way she read a blank page in the ledger, as a thing that would have to be accounted for before spring.

She had known the Hallorans for nineteen years. Abel Halloran had helped Walter raise the barn; his wife Etta had been the first woman to knock on June's door after the moving wagon had gone, with a loaf of bread still warm in a cloth, the bread a new neighbor's introduction. They had buried Ezra in the spring of 1934 with the Hallorans' wagon and the Hallorans' hands, and Etta Halloran had stood at the grave with June and held her hand and not said a word, which was the thing June had needed most and had never forgotten. You do not forget the hand that holds yours at a grave. You remember it the way you remember the color of the sky on the day a thing happens.

So when the wind came up out of the north and the Halloran chimney ran thin, June took a coal-oil can down from the shelf and filled it at the barrel behind the kitchen and walked the three fields over, and she stood at the Halloran door with the can in her hand and knocked.

Etta Halloran opened the door. She was a small woman gone gaunt, her hair in a grey braid, her eyes the pale blue of a winter sky, and she looked at the can in June's hand and then at June's face, and the two women stood a moment in the cold, and neither of them said the thing that was in both their mouths.

"Coal oil," June said. "For the stove."

Etta took the can. "I'll pay you back."

"You'll pay me back when the harvest pays you." June said it to be kind, and it came out plain, and the two of them stood in the doorway and the cold came in around them, and Etta said, "You'd best come in."

The Halloran kitchen was colder than the yard. The stove was lit but it was burning low, and June saw the wood box beside it with four sticks in it, and she saw the faces of the Halloran children, two of them, standing at the table with their coats on, a girl of six and a boy of nine, their hands blue at the knuckles, and she saw the piece of bread on the table that they were sharing, and she looked at Etta and Etta looked at the floor.

"Abel's gone to Boise City," Etta said, "to see about work. There's a crew grading the road out past the courthouse, and he went to get on it. He'll be back tonight or he won't."
