June set the coal-oil can on the counter and stood looking at the two children, and the girl looked up at her with Etta's eyes, and June thought about Sammy, who was in the house down the road with a fire he could keep going, and she thought about the word want, and how it sat in a house and changed the shape of it.

"I've got more coal oil," June said. "I'll bring it."

She walked back across the three fields with her coat buttoned to the throat and the wind at her back, and she brought a second can, and she brought a sack of potatoes from the cellar, and she set them on the Halloran counter and would not take the thanks, and she went home and stood in her own kitchen and looked at her own wood box, which was full, and she did not say anything, because there was nothing to say that was not arithmetic.

Abel Halloran came home that night with no work. June saw the truck go by the lane, the lights pale in the dark, and she saw it stop at the Halloran house, and she stood at her window and watched the light go on in the kitchen, and she thought about the four sticks of wood in the box, and about her own full box, and about the word want, and she went to bed and did not sleep well.

She was at the well the next morning, drawing the first bucket, when she heard it. A sound she knew, the way you know your own name: the ring of an axe in wood, going down hard and fast, the rhythm of a man who has made up his mind. It came from the north quarter, from the windbreak.

June put the bucket down. She did not run, because running was for younger women and for things you could still catch; she walked, and she walked fast, and she came over the rise of the north field and saw Abel Halloran in her windbreak with the axe, and a cottonwood down at his feet, the one at the end of the first row, the one Walter had set the spring before he left, and Abel had the axe back for the next stroke and he had not seen her.

"Abel."

He froze. The axe stayed up. He turned, and his face was a thing June had never seen on a man before, a face that had stopped pretending. His eyes were red, his beard grown out, and he stood in her windbreak with his children's cold in his coat and her tree at his feet, and he said, "June."

"Put the axe down."

He looked at the tree, and at the axe, and at the house behind him where his family was, and he said, "I can't. My kids are cold."

"Your kids are cold and you cut my windbreak." June came up to him, the two of them standing in the fallen tree's branches, and she looked at the raw cut where the axe had gone in, the pale wood showing, and she said, "This row is the thing between this place and the wind, Abel. You cut it down, you cut the farm down. You know what this row is."

"I know what it is. I also know what it is to hear your kids cry at night from the cold." Abel's voice shook, and he steadied it with a hand that was not steady. "I'll pay you for the wood. I'll cut you more than I take. I'll work it off, the whole farm's worth, if you'll let me take this one and the next one and get my family through the winter."

June stood a long moment in the fallen branches. The wind came over the north quarter and it came harder now, over the gap where the tree had been, and she felt it on her face the way you feel a thing missing in a room you have walked into. She thought about Etta's hand at the grave, and the bread in the cloth, and the two children with their blue knuckles and their shared piece of bread, and she thought about her own full wood box and her own warm kitchen, and the arithmetic came down on her like the first hard frost.

"Take the one that's down," she said. "Take it, and the next one over, the one that's leaning, it's half dead already. But that's all. You cut a live one and I'll go to the sheriff, and the sheriff will go to court, and I'll sit in the court and tell them what you did to my windbreak, and I know you, Abel, I know you don't want that on your name."

Abel stood with the axe and looked at her, and something in his face went down and something else went up, and he said, "All right."

"And you tell Etta to come by. I've got potatoes, and coal oil, and there's a quilt in the press that's too heavy for my bed." June turned and walked back across the north field without looking behind her, and her hands were shaking, and she did not let them shake where anyone could see.

Etta Halloran came that afternoon, with the two children and a bowl she meant to return, and June met her on the porch and they stood a moment, and then June said, "You come in and get warm while I find the quilt."

Etta came in. She stood in the middle of June's kitchen with her coat still on and looked at the room the way you look at a place you are embarrassed to stand in, and June watched her and did not hurry, and she lifted the quilt down from the press and folded it, and she put the potatoes in a sack, and she filled the coal-oil can, and Etta said, quiet, into the middle of the room, "I told him not to."

"I know you did."

"He's not a bad man. He's a man who's watched his family go cold and seen the work dry up, and there's a thing that happens to a man like that." Etta's voice was small and hard, the way a voice gets when it has been kept small for a long time. "He'd have cut his own arm off before he cut your tree, June, a year ago. Two years ago he'd have given you the shirt off his back. The cold's changed him, and I've changed with him, and I'm telling you now so you'll know I'm not standing here pretending."

June set the sack on the counter and turned and looked at her neighbor, the woman who had held her hand at a grave and had not said a word, and she said, "I know what the cold does to a man. I've watched it work on the whole country." She did not say that she had watched it work on Walter, and that the two men were going down the same road, one with an axe and one without. She said, "You take the quilt and you keep your children warm, and we'll be square, and you can tell Abel I said so."

Etta's chin went up, and her eyes went bright, and she picked up the quilt and the sack and the can and went out to the wagon without another word, and the children climbed up beside her, and June stood in the door and watched them go, and she thought about the arithmetic of a place like this, where a woman traded a tree and a quilt and a sack of potatoes for a neighbor's winter, and where the trade had a name but nobody said it, and the name was want.

She was at the pump when Abel's truck went up the lane, and she heard the axe again, once, twice, the sound of the dead tree coming down, and she did not go out, and she did not look. She stood at the pump with her hands on the handle and listened to the sound of the thing she had given away, and she counted it, the way she counted everything, into the ledger of the day: one tree down by the axe, one tree given to keep a neighbor's children warm, one row in the windbreak with a gap in it now that the wind would find.

That night she wrote it in the ledger, in the back page where the windbreak column ran: October — Abel Halloran, one cottonwood (the end tree), one dead elm, for wood. And in the margin, in the small hand she used for things that were not seed: the children were cold. She did not know if the line was arithmetic or confession, and she decided it was both, and she closed the book and set it on the shelf between the jars, and she went to the north window and stood looking out at the dark, at the place in the row where the tree had been, and the wind came through the gap and moved the curtain, and June stood with the wind on her face and felt the row's wound the way you feel a tooth after it's pulled, the tongue going to the empty place again and again.

She could hear, across the three fields, the faint ring of an axe in the dark, and she knew Abel was splitting the cottonwood, cutting it into stove lengths by lantern light, and she thought about the sound of it, a man cutting the wood that would keep his children warm, and about how the same sound in her own yard had meant a thing she had loved, and how now it meant a thing she had given, and how a sound could carry two weights at once, the way a word could. She thought about Etta Halloran, who had held her hand at a grave and had not said a word, and she thought about the wood box with the four sticks in it, and she closed the curtain and went to bed, and the wind worked the gap in the row all night like a tongue in an empty socket, and the axe went on across the fields, steady, into the small hours, and June lay awake listening to both sounds, the one in the row and the one on the other side of it, and she did not know which of them would prove to be the truer note by spring.

Chapter 15: The Windbreak Falls

June heard it in her sleep first, the way the body hears the one sound that matters in a night of sounds — the axe, going down in the north quarter, and then the slow groan of a tree going over, and the flat sound of it landing, a sound that went through the ground and up through the floor and into her chest. She was out of the bed and at the window before she was awake, and the dark was full of it, the ring and the groan and the land, and she was running.

She ran out in her nightgown, no coat, no shoes, her feet on the frozen stubble, and the cold went up her legs and she did not feel it. She came over the rise of the north field and the moon was up and she could see the windbreak, and she stopped, because what she saw did not come together in her mind all at once, the way a field does not come together when the hail has gone through it.

The rows were down. Not one tree, not the end tree she had given, but rows of them, the young cottonwoods and the willows and the Russian olives, lying on their sides in the moonlight like a fence that had been walked over, the pale wood of the cuts showing, the whole north quarter open to the sky. And in the middle of it, the wagon, and the lantern, and Abel Halloran's back, and Etta Halloran lifting the last of the greenwood into the wagon bed.

The north quarter had been bare ground once. June remembered the spring it was planted, 1933, the year after the first storms, when the fence lines had begun to disappear under the drift and the whole county had gone grey the color of a dirty nickel. Walter had read about the trees in the paper and had come in with the idea already made up, and they had gone out on a Sunday afternoon, the two of them and Cora, who was old enough to hold a shovel, and they had set the first row of cottonwoods where the wind came hardest off the Canadian line. Ezra had been three then, and he had carried the little willow wands two at a time, one in each fist, stumbling through the furrows, and June could still see the exact picture of it, the boy with his arms out like a scarecrow, the sun on his hair, the way he had stood back and watched her tamp the earth around his wand as if it were a thing he had made. The windbreak was not trees. It was the memory of that afternoon, and every afternoon like it, and the four of them had planted it the way families plant anything, a row each year, the way children grow, and Ezra's tree had gone in the spring after he died, one extra, and nobody had asked why there were more trees than people.

June walked into the fallen trees. She did not know she was walking; her legs were carrying her and her mouth was saying something and she did not know what. Abel heard her and turned, and the axe went down to his side, and he stood in the ruin of her windbreak with the lantern light on his face, and he did not look like a man she knew.

"You cut them all," June said. Her voice came out from somewhere she had not known she had. "You cut them all down."

"June —"

"All of them." She walked up to the nearest cut, a cottonwood not two years in the ground, the one she had watered through the driest July she had ever known, the one that had put on a hand's worth of growth by the well rope mark, and she put her hand on the pale wood of the cut and it was still wet. "These are not yours, Abel. You knew these were not yours."

"My kids are cold." He said it like a man saying a line he had practiced, and it came out hollow, and he heard it too, and he said it again, worse, "My kids are cold, June."

"Your kids were cold and I gave you a tree. I gave you a tree and a quilt and a sack of potatoes and coal oil, and you took them and you came back and you cut down my windbreak." June's voice was rising and she could not stop it, and she did not want to stop it. "This is the thing that holds the dirt. This is the thing that keeps the topsoil in the ground and the wind off the house. You have cut down the one thing on this farm that was standing, and you have cut it down for firewood, and you will burn it in a week and the wind will have the rest."

Etta Halloran came around the wagon. She had the last greenwood in her arms, a young willow with the leaves still on it, and she stood in the lantern light with it and looked at June, and June looked at her, and the two women stood among the fallen trees and the years of it came up between them — the bread in the cloth, the hand at the grave, the potato sack on the counter — and Etta's face did the thing June had seen a face do at a grave, the thing where the features stop holding and the person underneath shows.

"It was me," Etta said.

June turned. "What?"

"I told him to." Etta's arms tightened around the greenwood. "He came home with your tree and your quilt and he was proud of himself, proud that you'd give, and I looked at the wood box and I counted the sticks and I said — I said it wasn't enough. I said the winter was long and you had a whole row and the county was sending you seed and we had nothing, and I told him to take the row." Her voice was flat, the flatness of a thing said out loud for the first time. "He didn't want to. I made him."

June stood in the moonlight and heard it, and the wind came over the north quarter and there was nothing to stop it, and it moved the leaves on the greenwood in Etta's arms and moved the hair at June's temples, and June said, "I held your hand at my son's grave."

"I know."

"I helped you when the cows went down. I sat up with your girl when she had the fever. I gave you a tree out of my windbreak this morning, and you went home and made your husband cut down the rest of it for a fire that will burn out before December."

"I know." Etta's voice broke on it, and she did not let go of the greenwood. "I know all of it, June. I've known it since I told him. There is nothing you can say to me that I have not already said to myself."

"Then why?" June's voice was small now, the anger gone out of it like the light going out of a room, and what was left was something older. "Why would you do a thing like that, Etta? You're not a bad woman. I've known you nineteen years and you are not a bad woman, and you did a bad thing."

"Because I've got children, June, and they were cold, and I would have cut down the courthouse to warm them." Etta's eyes were dry and terrible. "You've got one child left and he's got a fire and a full wood box, and I've got two and they've got nothing, and that's the arithmetic, June, that's the whole of it. You can keep your windbreak or you can keep your neighbor, and I chose, and this is what I chose."

The wind moved through the fallen trees. Abel stood with the axe at his side and did not move, and the wagon stood half-loaded, and the lantern guttered, and June looked at the two of them, the man and the woman she had known for nineteen years, standing in the ruin of the thing she and Walter had planted tree by tree, and she felt the ground go out from under the day in a way she had not felt since she stood at a grave in the spring of 1934.

"Take it," she said.

Etta's face went still. "June —"

"Take it." June's voice was quiet now, and it was the quietest thing in the night, quieter than the wind. "Take the wood and load the wagon and go, and when you're done, don't come back to this house, and don't send Abel, and don't send your children to play with my boy, and if I see you at the store I'll say good morning and nothing else, because that's all I have left for you, Etta Halloran. That's all the nineteen years left me."

She turned and walked back across the north field, barefoot through the frozen stubble and the scattered branches, and she did not look back, and she went into the house and stood in the kitchen with the door closed and the darkness around her and the cold coming up through the floor, and she did not cry, because crying was a thing she had done her last time of at a grave, and she had no more of it in her.

In the morning she went out at first light and walked the north quarter, and she counted. Thirty-one trees gone, and four standing, the four at the far end of the second row, and of those four, two were split and leaning, and two were whole. Thirty-one trees, the growth of two springs, the work of her hands and Sammy's and the cistern's water, laid flat in one night for a fire that would burn out before December.

She found the seedlings. At the near end of the first row there was a crate of them, the replacements the county agent had sent in the spring, the ones that had not gone in because the ground was too dry, and they were unbroken, still bundled in their sacking, and she carried the crate down to the cellar and wrapped the sacking wet and set the crate in the dark where the cold could not get at it, and she stood over it a long time, and she did not say the thing she was thinking, because saying it would have been the end of something.

The seedlings were two-year cottonwoods, a dozen of them, whip-thin and pale as switches, and June had planted her own heart into them in the spring, into the furrow with the water from the cistern, and she had believed in them the way she believed in the ledger, as a thing you set in the ground and let stand against the wind. They had gone into the earth in April, and they had held, and the frost had come and they had held, and now the axe had gone through the rows like a man walking through a house, and the seedlings in the cellar were the only green thing left in the north quarter, and June stood over them in the dark and made herself a promise she did not speak aloud, because a promise spoken in the dark to a crate of seedlings was a thing that had to be true or it would be nothing at all.

It was not the wood they took, she thought. And then she thought it again, and it was not a thought, it was a fact, and it had the weight of a fact, the weight of a tree going over.

The Halloran wagon had gone up the lane in the dark, and by morning there was a line of stove lengths stacked at their door and a scatter of branches in the yard, and the two children were splitting kindling in the thin sun, and June saw them from the rise and stood a long moment looking down at the house of the woman who had held her hand at a grave, and she felt the cold come up through her and she went back to her own yard and did not look again.

That night she wrote in the ledger. She wrote the date, and she wrote the weather, and she wrote the seed column with nothing in it, and then she turned to the back page, the windbreak page, and she drew a line through the column of growth she had kept there, the hands and the inches and the well-rope marks, and she wrote, in the small hand she used for the things that were not seed: October 28, 1935 — the windbreak cut down in the night. Thirty-one trees. The loss was not the wood.

Sammy found her at noon, sitting on the porch steps with her hands in her lap, looking at the gap where the windbreak had been. He came and stood in front of her, and he had the .22 in his hand, and his face was white, and he said, "I heard it last night. I was going to come out with the gun."

June looked at her son. "I'm glad you didn't."

"They took the trees."

"They took the trees."

Sammy stood a long moment, and then he said, quiet, the way a child says the one thing that matters: "That was Ezra's tree too."

"I know it was."

"The one you planted in the spring." Sammy's voice had the careful flatness of a boy holding something down. "I watched you plant it. You didn't say anything, but I knew. It was the extra one, at the end of the second row, and you watered it every night for a month." He looked at the stumps. "It's gone now."

"It's gone."

"If I'd come out last night with the gun, they'd have stopped."

June looked at her son, at the white of his knuckles on the .22, and she said, "No, Sammy. If you'd come out with the gun, you'd have pointed it at a man with children, and you'd have been a child holding a gun, and the law would have come, and I would have lost you too. The gun doesn't stop the axe, boy. It only makes the axe worse." She reached out and took the .22 from him, and he let her take it, and she stood up with it in her hands and looked down the north quarter at the stumps. "What stops the axe is what we plant. What stops the axe is the ground coming back. You and me, we'll plant them again in the spring, and the wind'll have a harder time of it the second time, because it'll know us."

Sammy looked at the stumps for a long time, and then he put the .22 down on the porch steps beside her, within her reach, and he sat down on the step next to her, and the two of them sat in the cold and looked at the north quarter, and the wind came over it, and there was nothing between the house and it now but four leaning trees and a field of stumps, and the wind came through and moved the hair at June's temples, and she looked at the empty place in the world where her windbreak had stood, and she did not cry, and she did not speak, and the wind had the north quarter all day, and the stumps stood in it like teeth, and June sat on the steps and watched the day go grey, and the crate of seedlings waited in the dark of the cellar, wrapped in wet sacking, and the frost came up through the ground at evening and lay on the stumps like the first snow, and the wind kept coming, and there was nothing to stop it.

Chapter 16: Cora's First Pay

The post office in the valley town had a WPA mural over the door — pioneers, a woman with a sack of seed, a man with a plow, a sky the color of a clean shirt — and Cora stood under it a long time before she went in, the money order folded in her pocket, warm from her hand. She had been in line at the telegraph office first, had seen the rates posted on the wall and had stepped out again, and now she stood in front of the post office and read the mural the way you read a sign in a strange country, looking for what it meant.

The telegraph office had been a lesson in itself. She had gone there first because it was the bigger building, the one with the wires coming down off the poles, and the man behind the counter had told her the rate for ten words to Cimarron County and had looked at her the way the weighmaster looked at a sack that was going to weigh short, and Cora had done the arithmetic on her thumb — ten words for a dollar, and what could ten words say that her mother would be able to eat — and she had walked out without sending, and the man had shrugged, and the wires had gone on humming over her head, and she had come down the street to the post office and found the mural.

It meant somebody in Washington had thought the pioneers worth painting, that the dust and the blowouts and the banks and the foreclosures were not the whole story, that a woman with a sack of seed was a thing a country wanted to remember. Cora stood under it and felt something turn over in her chest, something she could not have named, and she went in.

The post office was one room with a counter and a row of boxes and a calendar with a girl on it holding a basket of peaches, and the clerk behind the counter was a woman in her forties with glasses on a chain, and she looked up when Cora came in and she did not smile, because the clerk had seen a thousand girls come in off the camps with money orders, and she knew what they wanted.

"Money order," Cora said. "I need to cash it."

The clerk took the paper and turned it over and looked at the signatures. "Five dollars. You've endorsed it?"

"I did."

"Let me see your identification."

Cora had not thought about identification. She stood at the counter and went through her pockets, and she had nothing with her name on it, nothing at all — no draft card, no letter, no paper, nothing to prove she was Cora Vogel of Cimarron County, Oklahoma, which was a fact she had never had to prove before because she had never been anywhere a fact needed proving.

It was a strange feeling, to be a person with no paper. At home she had been June's daughter and Sammy's sister and the girl who could pick a row clean and quiet the pump with a turn of the handle, and none of it had needed signing. On the road she was a body with a sack and a place on the scale, and the only thing in the world with her name on it was the name she had written on the back of the money order that morning, in a hand that still shook a little from the picking, and the clerk had a way of looking at a girl who could not prove who she was that Cora had seen on the weigh line, on the relief line, at the camp office, a look that said prove it and then there was nothing to prove it with.

"I don't have any," she said.

The clerk looked at her over the glasses. "I can't cash it without identification."

"It's my own money. I earned it. I picked peaches for it." Cora's voice came up hot and she heard it and tried to hold it down, the way you hold a horse that wants to run. "I sent it to my mother. She sent it back. Look —" She unfolded the letter, the one June had written, the handwriting her mother's, the words plain and small, and she put it on the counter. "That's her writing. She's my mother. She signed the back of the order. That's my name on it, the one I wrote when I endorsed it."

The clerk looked at the letter and then at Cora, and something in her face shifted, the way a sky shifts before rain, and she said, "Wait here," and she went through a door at the back and came back with a man in a brown suit, the postmaster, and the postmaster looked at the order and the letter and at Cora, and he asked her where she was from and she told him, and he asked her what her father's name was and she told him, and he asked her the name of the county seat and she told him Boise City, and he nodded and said, "Cash it."

The clerk counted out five dollars in ones and silver and pushed it across the counter, and the postmaster said, "Next time you send money home, you use a money order from this office, and you keep the receipt, and you bring the receipt when you want to cash, and it'll go smooth. The mail's the only thing this country runs on that a poor man's got a share in, and it pays to know the rules of it."

Cora took the money. It was the first money she had earned with her own hands in her own name, and it was real, the way the peaches had been real, and she stood at the counter and looked at it, the worn bills, the silver, and she thought about her mother's egg money, the jar in the pantry, the coins that had gone into Walter's pocket the morning he left, and she thought about the money order as a kind of seed, the thing you send into the ground at home and wait for it to come up.

She mailed the letter at the same counter. It was a short letter, three pages, and it had cost her a week of evenings to write, and it lied about the food. She told June she was eating fine, and she was not; she told her the work was steady, and it was; she told her the camp was comfortable, and it was not, and she signed it your Cora, and she had not written her own name like that since the farm, and it looked strange on the paper and right at the same time, the way a new shoe looks strange and right.

The letter had started as a penny postcard. Cora had bought the card a month before, a picture of the valley on it, the orchards in green, a sky with no dust in it at all, and she had stood at the counter of the little store with the pencil in her hand and the card in front of her and the small square of space for a message, and she had understood that a postcard could not hold it, could not hold any of it, that the whole of what she had to say would not fit in the small square of space, and she had put the card back on the shelf and walked out, and the pencil had stayed in her pocket, Miguel's pencil, and the letter had grown a page a night for a week, the way a root grows, and this morning she had read it through at the edge of the camp in the first light and crossed out the two places where she had almost told the truth about the food, and she had folded it into the envelope, and it was three pages, and it was all lies and all true at once, the way a letter from a girl who has left home is always lies and true at once.

The clerk weighed it and took the stamp money, and Cora watched the letter go into the sack with the others, and she felt it go, the way you feel a root going into the ground, and she stood at the counter a moment longer than she needed to, and then she went out past the mural and stood in the sun and put the five dollars in her shoe, where no one could lift it, and started back down the road to the camp.

The town was busy the way valley towns are busy in November, the fruit trucks lining the street, the stores open, the men standing in front of the feed store with their hands in their pockets. Cora walked past them and they looked at her, and she looked away, the way you learn to look away, and she kept the five dollars in her shoe and the receipt in her pocket, and she thought about the arithmetic of it, the whole long column of what it had taken to send five dollars home: the picking, the heat, the weighmaster's thumb, the tent rent, the water, the store, the blacklist mark in Earl's book, the girl Elsa with her baby, and the number at the bottom of the column was five, and she had made it home.

She passed the telegraph office and did not stop. She passed the labor office and did not stop. She came to the edge of town where the orchard began, and she stood a moment looking back at the street, at the post office with its mural of the woman with the sack of seed, and she thought about her mother's seed packet in her coat, the beans from 1934, carried west unplanted, and she thought about the difference between a seed you plant and a seed you carry, and she did not know which kind she was, and she started down the road to the camp with the money in her shoe and the receipt in her pocket and the letter gone ahead of her like a scout.

At the camp Rosa met her at the edge of the tents. "You sent it?"

"Five dollars."

Rosa's eyes went wide. "Five dollars. That's a month of water and part of the rent." She walked with Cora between the tents, and the camp was coming alive for the evening, the fires going, the coffee smelling, the children running the lanes, and Rosa said, "Your mother's going to get that money order and she's going to stand in her own post office and cash it and she's going to hold five dollars in her hand that you picked, and she's going to know where her girl is and what she's made of."

Cora thought about it, her mother in the Boise City post office, and the picture came to her clear and sharp, the way pictures of home came to her in the quiet hours, and she felt something move in her chest that was not hunger and was not tiredness.

"You don't know my mother," she said.

"I know what a letter does to a woman whose girl is gone." Rosa said it simply, the way she said most things, and Cora looked at her and did not know if Rosa was speaking of her own mother or of a thing she had seen, and she did not ask, because stories cost more than they were worth, and some of them you carried without being asked.

That night at the plank table Miguel was not there. A man said he had gone to town, to a meeting, and Cora sat at the end of the table with her hands in her lap and watched the lamp swing, and she thought about the five dollars going home, and about the letter, and about her mother's hand at the post office window, and about the seed packet in her coat, and the wind moved the tent walls and the jukebox from the highway café came through the trees, the nickel song, the same one, and Cora sat in the dark and counted her money again in her head, the whole column of it, what it had cost and what it was worth, and the number at the bottom was five, and it was the truest number she had ever held.

She slept with the receipt under her pillow and the money in her shoe at the foot of the cot, and in the morning there was frost on the tent wall and the camp rose early and the pickers went out to the cotton, and the letter was already two towns east of her, riding the mail truck down the valley, and the money order lay in its pouch with the other orders, and the woman with the sack of seed looked down from the mural on the empty street, and the first root had gone back into the ground at home, and Cora bent to the row with her sack and began the second month of the arithmetic, counting the pounds, keeping the number in her head, the way a girl keeps a coin in her fist, the way a root keeps going down.

Chapter 17: The Strike Talk

The cotton came in white in November, and the valley filled with pickers the way a field fills with birds, and the camps spread along the ditch lines and the fence rows, and the weigh lines at the cotton scales ran long from before light to after, and the money went up and down with the weather, and the word that was going around the camps was not a word the growers liked.

Cora heard it first at the water truck. A man with a torn ear was filling his can and saying it to the man beside him, low, the way men say a thing they are not ready to say out loud. The word was strike. She heard it again at the cook fire, a woman saying the price had dropped again and a man answering that the only way to make it go back up was to make the picking stop. She heard it at the plank table, where the men had stopped talking when she came up, and had gone on again when they saw she was not one to carry it to the foreman.

She did not know it yet, but she was already a messenger. It was Miguel who told her, on a night in the third week of November, when the frost had come and the cotton was half in and the growers were talking about a fourth cut of the price.

They sat at the plank table and the lamp burned low, and the camp was quiet, and Miguel said, "There's going to be a walkout. The men have been meeting, and the women, and there's a committee now. They mean to put down their sacks and stop the picking until the growers sit down and talk."

Cora looked at him. "They'll be fired. The growers will bring in scabs and the men will be blacklisted, and the county will send deputies, and there'll be blood." She had learned this arithmetic somewhere without being taught it, from the men with torn ears and the women with the flat eyes and the stories that went around the camps in the dark. "You know this, Miguel. You taught it to me. Why would a man walk into a thing he knows the cost of?"

"Because the cost of not walking is worse." Miguel turned the pencil in his fingers. "You think a man picks cotton for a living because he likes it? He picks because the other door is closed. But there's a price where a man would rather have nothing than the nothing he's got, and the growers have found it. When the price drops below what a man can live on, he's not choosing between work and no work. He's choosing between two nothings, and one of them has his name on it and the other one doesn't."

Cora was quiet. She had done the arithmetic herself, had held the numbers in her head the way he had taught her, the weight, the rate, the cuts, the tent, the water, the store, and the number at the bottom had gone red in October and had not come back to black. She knew what he meant, the way you know a thing in your own hand.

"They want you to help," Miguel said.

"Help how?"

"Carry messages." He said it carefully, the way a man places a thing on a table that he wants to stay where it is. "The committee can't meet in the open. The growers have spies in every camp — men with money in their pockets and nothing else, men who'd sell their own mothers for a place in the blacklist file that keeps them fed. So the meetings are small, and the word has to travel, and a girl can travel where a man can't." He looked at her. "A girl with a sack on her shoulder and a canteen on her hip is just a girl going to the weigh line. A man is a man going to a meeting. You can pass where they watch the men."

Cora felt the words settle on her the way dust settles, fine and everywhere. She had been afraid of this the whole time, afraid of the moment the door opened and she had to decide whether to walk through it. She had carried the blacklist mark in Earl's book since September, had felt it ride on her like a thing with weight, and she had thought, in the weeks since, that the safe road was the only road, the road where you count your own weight and keep it in your head and let the world cheat itself quiet. And now here was the other road, and it was not safe, and it was the road the men with the torn ears were taking, and the women with the flat eyes, and Miguel.

"Why me?" she asked.

"Because you can read a ticket. Because you count. Because when the weighmaster's thumb came down you said the words out loud, and half the camp heard it, and they remember." Miguel's voice was patient, the way it was when he taught a lesson, and Cora saw that the lesson had been leading somewhere all along, that the arithmetic had not just been about tickets. "The committee wants people it can trust. There aren't many. You're one."

"Am I?"

"You're one." He said it without smiling, and Cora looked down at the table, at the grain of the door, and she thought about her mother's ledger, about the way June had kept a column for everything, the weather, the seed, the lives, the losses, and she thought about what it would mean to put her own name in a column that was not safe, and she said, "What do I have to carry?"

The first message was a slip of paper folded into a half-pint of tobacco. Cora carried it down the ditch line to the camp at the south end of the orchard, three miles in the heat, a canteen on her hip and the tobacco tin in her pocket, and she sat at the cook fire with the women there and let them read it, and she did not know what was in it, and she did not ask. She carried the answer back in her head, a time and a place and a number, and she gave it to Miguel at the plank table, and he wrote it down and nodded, and the first trip was done.

The trips multiplied. Cora learned the roads of the camps the way she had learned the rows, the ditch crossings, the fence gaps, the places where a man could see a girl coming and the places where he could not. She learned to carry a message in the lining of her coat and in the sole of her shoe and in her hair, and she learned to be plain, the plainest girl on the road, the one the deputies did not look at twice because she looked like every other girl on every other weigh line, a body with a sack and a place in the world and no name.

Once, a deputy stopped her at the crossing below the railroad tracks, a big man with a badge and a belly and a way of standing in the road that meant the road was his until he said it wasn't. He looked at her sack and her canteen and her bare feet in the worn shoes, and he said, "Where you headed, girl?"

"Weigh line." Cora kept her eyes down and her voice flat, the flatness of every girl who had ever been stopped by a man in a road. "North camp's short a picker."

"You don't look like a picker." He was not joking. "You look like you got somewhere to be that ain't a weigh line."

"My sister's sick. I'm going to bring her water." It was the truth, mostly — the message in her shoe was a time and a place and a number, and the water on her hip was real, and the sister was Rosa, who was not sick and who knew what the water was for.

The deputy stood a long moment, and the wind moved the dry grass, and then he stepped out of the road and jerked his chin, and Cora walked past him and did not run, and she felt his eyes on her back all the way to the crossing, and her hands were steady and her knees were not, and she gave the water to a woman with a fever at the north camp and the message to the man who waited behind the tent, and she walked home in the dark with her heart going like a well pump and the dust in her throat, and she lay awake a long time that night and thought about the deputy's belly and his badge and the way the road had been his until he said it wasn't, and about how she had walked through it anyway, and the fear was a thing in her chest like a cold coin, and the walking-through was a thing she could do, and she did not know which of the two was the truer thing about her, and she decided it did not matter, as long as the walking got done.

The meetings happened at night, in a tent behind the camp that was supposed to be a sick tent and was not. There was a committee now, a workers' committee, and the growers' papers had started to write about it, calling it the work of outside agitators, and Cora read the papers the men left on the table and learned the words they used, the words that made her stomach go cold and her hands go still.

On the last night before the walkout was to be called, the committee met in the tent behind the camp, and Cora sat at the back with the water can, filling cups, and she listened to the men talk, and the jukebox from the highway café came through the tent wall, the nickel song, the one she had heard in Amarillo, and the song and the talk made a strange music together, the tune and the numbers, the tune that said the world was a bright place and the numbers that said it was not, and Cora sat in the dark with the water can and felt the two of them run together in her chest like two rivers.

The committee was not the union. The men were careful to say it. There was a man with a scarred hand who said the word union and the whole tent went quiet, and another man said, "Don't say that word here. Say the committee." The growers had broken the union in the cotton country before, had brought in the deputies and the injunctions and the blacklist, and the word itself had a smell of blood and fire to it, and the men in the tent were not fools, they knew what they were walking toward, they walked toward it with their eyes open and their mouths shut.

Miguel spoke last. He stood at the front of the tent with the lamp behind him, and he did not raise his voice, and the tent went still, and he said, "The growers count on us to be hungry enough to work for nothing. That's their arithmetic. Our arithmetic is the same one, worked the other way: if we are hungry enough to work for nothing, we are hungry enough to stop. The strike is not a thing we want. It's the only number left that moves theirs." He looked around the tent, at the men with the torn ears and the scarred hands, at the women in the back with the shawls over their heads, and he said, "We will put down our sacks on the first of December, and we will hold them down until the growers sit at a table and talk, and we will be hungry, and we will be cold, and some of us will be beaten and some of us will be jailed, and the growers' papers will call us outside agitators, and we will still be here, because this is our work and our country too."

The tent was quiet. The jukebox had gone off, and the only sound was the wind in the canvas and the breathing of the people, and Cora sat at the back with the water can in her hands and felt the numbers go through her, the whole long column of them, what it would cost and what it was for, and she thought about the five dollars in the mail and the seed packet in her coat and the ledger in her mother's kitchen, and she understood, in the dark, that she had been learning this arithmetic all along, from the plank table and the weigh line and the blacklist file with her name in it, and that the lesson had a last page, and she was looking at it.

After the meeting she walked with Miguel down the ditch line, and the frost was coming up off the ground and the stars were out, and she said, "I'll carry whatever you need me to carry."

"I know you will."

"I'm not afraid." She said it to make it true.

"You're afraid," said Miguel. "Anybody who isn't afraid doesn't understand the arithmetic. You carry it anyway. That's the whole of courage, Cora. Being afraid and walking the road anyway." He stopped at the edge of the camp and looked at her, and in the starlight the scar on his jaw was a pale line, and he said, "When the trouble comes, you stay with the women. You keep the water moving. A strike is fed by water and by the people who carry it."

"I know."

He turned and went up the lane to his tent, and Cora stood a moment in the frost and looked up at the stars, the same stars that hung over her mother's kitchen, and she thought about the date, the first of December, and she counted the days on her hand, and there were four, and the four days felt like a thing with weight, and she went to her tent and lay down and put her hand under the pillow where the receipt from the post office lay, and she thought about her mother, and about the ledger, and about the road, and the wind moved the tent wall and the camp was quiet, and the word strike hung in the air over the whole valley like the smell of the first rain coming, and Cora closed her eyes and counted the days until she would put down her sack, and the number was four, and it was the truest number she had ever held.

Chapter 18: The Letter Home

The letter came out of her in one night, the way a thing that has been held down for months comes out — not all at once, but in a rush that could not be stopped once it started, page after page, until the lamp was low and the camp was quiet and the frost was on the tent wall, and Cora sat on her cot with the pen in her hand and looked at what she had written and did not know the girl who had written it.

She had started it three times since the post office. She had bought paper in the valley town, a tablet of the cheap ruled kind, and the first two attempts had come out stiff, the way a letter comes out when you are writing to a person you are afraid of disappointing. Dear Mother, I am fine. The work is steady. The camp is comfortable. She had read them and torn them up, because she had stood in the sun with five dollars in her shoe and known that fine and steady and comfortable were not the true words, and that her mother, who kept a ledger of every seed and every loss, would read the lie the way she read the almanac, as a sign of a season she needed to prepare for.

The third attempt came out of her different. She did not plan it. She sat down after supper with the tablet and Miguel's pencil — she had kept the pencil, sharpened it down to a nub, and the pen at the store had cost too much, so it was the pencil — and she wrote the date, and she wrote Dear Mother, and then the words came the way the creek came up after the first rain, not a flood, just a rising, and she let them come.

She wrote about the work. The truth of the work, the way the peaches came off clean if you did the twist and the lift right, the way her hands had gone dark to the wrist, the way the cotton hung in the field at the end of the day like the first snow, the weigh line moving past the scale all day like water past a rock. She wrote about the heat, and about the frost, and about the camp, the tents in the rows, the fires at evening, the smell of beans and coffee, the children running the lanes. She wrote about Lula, who had taken her in, and about Rosa, who had taught her the twist and the lift, and about the man who taught arithmetic at a plank table at night.

She wrote about the road. She had not meant to, but the road came up under her hand the way the creek came up after rain, and she wrote about the trucks and the towns and the first time she had stood on pavement and understood that the land went on past the county line, that the horizon she had read all her life as the end of the world was only the place where the world turned the corner. She wrote about the first jukebox café, the nickel and the song, and about the tent meeting at the crossroads and the preacher's voice, and she wrote that she had seen the country's scale now, the flat land that went on past the telling of it, and that a girl from a farm on the Cimarron flood plain was a small thing against it, and that the smallness had been a comfort in a way she had not expected, because it meant her troubles were not the only troubles, that the whole country was running on the same arithmetic.

She wrote about Sammy. She told June to tell him she had seen the road he wanted to walk down, and that it went on longer than he thought, and that the .22 in the back of the shed was worth more at home than any road was worth away, and she wrote it the way she would have said it to him, teasing and true at once, and she thought about her brother's face, the way he stood at the fence line in the dusk looking down the road to the highway, and the letter's hand went still a moment, and then it went on.

She wrote about Miguel.

She did not mean to write about Miguel. She wrote about the lesson first, the way he had taken her ticket and shown her the cut, the thumb, the rent, the water, the whole arithmetic of what a picking day was worth, and she wrote that it was like her mother's ledger, that a man in a camp in the valley taught the same arithmetic her mother kept in a book in the kitchen, and that she had understood her mother's book for the first time, sitting at a plank table under a peach tree with the lamp swinging. And then the letter wrote what it wrote, that he had a scar on his jaw and a gentleness with the children and a way of looking at a girl that was not the way the other men looked, and that he had been a schoolteacher once, in New Mexico, and that he had taught school till he taught too much, and that she was learning from him the thing the whole country ran on.

She read that part back and sat still a long time. The lamp made the tent wall glow, and the wind moved it in and out, and the pencil was warm in her hand, and Cora looked at the words she had written about Miguel and felt the heat come up her neck, the same heat that came up when he looked at her, and she did not cross it out. She left it. It was the truest thing in the letter, truer than the work and the heat and the camp, and a letter that was going to be all true was going to be all true.

It was strange to write his name to her mother. Cora had not said his name aloud to anyone but Rosa, and Rosa had only nodded, the way Rosa nodded at things she had already understood. To put him on the paper, in the same letter that carried the work and the road and the windbreak, was to let her mother see the shape of her days, the shape that had formed around him without Cora's permission, the way a path forms around a tree. She wrote that he was kind, and that he was precise, and that he had been a schoolteacher, and that when he taught he did not raise his voice, and that the children of the camp came to the plank table at evening because he would show them their letters, and that she had watched him take a boy's hand and shape a letter with it, and she had thought, in that moment, about a one-room schoolhouse on the Cimarron and a woman teaching a row of children their numbers. She did not write that last thought. It came up under her hand and she held it back, because it was not a thought about Miguel at all, and she was not ready to know what it was.

She asked about the windbreak. The words surprised her. She had not meant to ask about the windbreak, had not let herself think about the windbreak, the double row of cottonwood and willow and Russian olive she had helped set in the spring before she left, the extra tree she had watched her mother plant without a word of explanation, and now the question was on the paper, plain and small, in her mother's hand the way her mother's questions came to her: Is the windbreak holding? The trees we planted in the spring — are they growing? And she read it and knew it was the true question, the one under all the others, the one that asked whether anything she had helped plant was still standing.

She could not have said why the windbreak had become the measure of things for her. It was only trees, cottonwood and willow and Russian olive in a double row, and she had watered them with the cistern water through the dry spring and grumbled at the work and felt the sun on her neck and the grit in her teeth like any farm chore. But the trees were the last thing she had planted before she left, the last furrow she had set her hand to on the home place, and every camp she came to, every orchard and cotton field, she had measured against them, against the fact of a double row of young trees going up out of the dust on the north quarter of a farm on the Cimarron, and the question on the paper was the question she had been carrying west with her the whole way without knowing it.

She did not tell about the strike.

She had meant to. She had carried the messages for two weeks, had learned the roads and the crossings and the way to be plain, had felt the word strike go through her like a current, and she wanted to tell her mother, wanted to put it on the paper the way she had put the truth about the work, wanted her mother to know what her daughter had become in the valley, what she was carrying, what she had walked through when the deputy stood in the road. But the words would not come down. Every time she started to write it, she saw the growers' papers, the word agitator in black type, the blacklist file with her name in it, and she thought about what it would do to her mother in a farmhouse three states away, reading that her daughter was part of a thing that would bring the deputies and the injunctions and maybe the blood, and she could not put that on the paper. She wrote around it. She wrote that the work had its seasons, that the growers set the price and the pickers took it, that a girl learned arithmetic on the road the way a girl learned anything, by doing it wrong once and doing it right after. She wrote that she was saving what she could, that the money orders would come as steady as the harvest, that she had a receipt from the post office in her pillow and that she counted her pay the way June counted seed, and she wrote the last page slower than the rest, and the last page was the one she had not known she was writing.

She wrote that she was all right. That was the whole of the last page, in the end, that she was all right, and that the girl who had left in June had not been lost, that she was somewhere down the road, changed the way any road changes a girl, but not lost, and that her mother should not worry, and that the money would come, and that when the season turned she would come home and bring the change with her, the way you bring the weather in on your coat. And she signed it, and the signature came out of her without her meaning to write it.

Your Cora.

She had not signed her name that way since she left, had signed the money-order letter and the postcards with a plain Cora, the way you sign a thing you are not sure of. But the letter was true, and the signature came out true, and Cora looked at it, the two words, in her own hand, and she felt something give in her chest, something that had been held tight since the truck pulled out of the lane in June, and she folded the letter and addressed the envelope in the same careful hand, and she sat with it a long time on the cot, the envelope in her lap, the frost on the tent wall, the camp quiet, and she thought about the road the letter would travel, down the valley to the railhead, across the desert, up through the panhandle, to a farmhouse on the Cimarron flood plain where her mother kept a ledger on a shelf between the canning jars.

She walked to the post office in the morning with the letter in her coat, and it was cold, the first real cold of the season, and the frost was on the weeds and her breath went before her, and she stood at the counter with the letter and the stamp and watched the clerk weigh it and put it in the sack, and she walked out past the mural of the woman with the sack of seed and stood a moment in the pale sun, and she did not go back to the camp. She went to the edge of town where the orchard began, and she stood looking at the rows, the bare peach trees, the cotton going white beyond them, and she thought about the strike and the messages and the deputy and the date that was coming, the first of December, and she thought about her mother reading the letter in the kitchen, the lamp lit, the ledger on the shelf, and she thought about the words she had not written, the ones that were still in her chest like seed not yet planted, and she went back down the road to the camp with her hands in her pockets and the envelope gone ahead of her like a scout.

That evening she sat at the plank table and did not tell Miguel about the letter, and he did not ask, and the lamp swung and the lesson went on, and when she lay down that night she put her hand under the pillow where the receipt lay, and she thought about the words she had written and the words she had not, and she felt the two of them in her chest, the true words and the hidden ones, the way you feel a seed and the ground around it, and she slept, and the camp was quiet, and the frost came down, and the letter went east in the dark, up the valley, over the desert, toward a farmhouse on the Cimarron where a lamp would be lit and a ledger would be open, and the words your Cora rode with it, and the hidden words stayed behind in the dark like seed in the ground, waiting for a season that had not yet come.

Chapter 19: June's Illness

It came on her like the storms came on the country, not all at once but as a gathering, a tightness in the chest that she set down to the cold and the windbreak's wound and the long walk to the well, and then a cough that would not clear, and then the fever, low at first, the kind of fever you walk with, the kind you cook supper with and pour the chickens' water with and do not mention.

June mentioned it to no one. She woke on a December morning with the frost on the window glass and the cough deep in her chest, a cough that came up from a place that felt like the bottom of the well, and she stood at the stove and made the porridge and felt the heat of the stove reach into her and settle, and she went out to the chickens and the well and the north quarter, and she stood at the edge of the stumps a long time with the wind moving her coat, and she came in and sat down and did not get up again for three days.

Sammy found her there that evening. She was on the kitchen chair with her head on the table, the porridge cold in the bowl, and the boy stood in the door a long moment and then came in and put his hand on her back, and she lifted her head and looked at him, and he saw the fever in her eyes the way you see a storm coming on a flat horizon.

"I'm all right," she said. "It's just the cold."

"You're burning." He said it flat, the way he said the hard things. "I'm going for the neighbor."

"No." She caught his wrist, and her hand was hot and dry, and she held him the way she had held him when he was small, a grip that was not a grip, and she said, "I don't want the Hallorans in this house. Not after what they did. You stay with me, Sammy, that's all I need. You stay with me."

Sammy stayed. He was ten years old and he had watched his brother die of the dust and he knew what a fever in the lungs meant in a house on the Cimarron in December, and he did not argue. He banked the stove and hung the wet cloths at the door and drew the water and put the kettle on, and he moved through the house the way a boy moves when he has been left alone with a responsibility, careful and quiet and old.

The fever climbed. June lay in her bed with the camphor rags on her chest that Sammy made from the bottle in the cupboard, and she coughed, and the cough brought up the grit, the fine grey stuff that had been in her lungs since the spring, that had come in with every storm and settled the way the dust settled on the windowsills, and she looked at it in the cloth and knew what it was, and she knew what it meant, because she had watched it take her son.

Ezra had died in the spring of 1934. The fever had come on him in March, the way it had come on her now, a gathering, a tightness, and June had done the same things she was watching Sammy do now, the kettle, the camphor, the wet sheets, and she had sent Walter for the doctor, and the doctor had come too late, and the boy had gone in his mother's arms with the grit in his lungs and the dust on the window glass, and June had buried him in the open ground behind the windbreak and had planted a tree for him and had not spoken of him since, not once, not to Walter, not to Cora, not to the neighbors who stood at the grave and held her hand.

Now she lay in the same bed and coughed the same cough, and she knew, in the way a woman knows a thing she has been dreading for a year, that this was the arithmetic coming for her, the account the land had been keeping, the reckoning she had named at the kitchen table in the spring when Walter left, the words that had gone out of her mouth and had not come back.

The whole of Ezra's dying came up under the fever, the way water comes up in a well you thought was dry. She saw the spring of 1934 again, the way she saw it in the bad hours: the boy small in the bed, the fever red in his cheeks, the sound of his breathing going wrong, a sound like a door closing slowly. Walter had gone for the doctor in the wagon and had come back with him after dark, and the doctor had listened to the boy's chest with his eyes closed and had shaken his head, and June had stood at the foot of the bed and had heard the shake of the head the way you hear a board give under you, and she had taken the boy in her arms and held him through the night, and in the morning the sound of the door closing had finished, and the boy was gone, and the dust had been coming down out of a grey sky all the while, fine and steady, the way it does, and June had stood at the window with the dead boy in her arms and looked out at the grey and understood that the sky had been taking him all along, a little at a time, with every breath. She had buried him in the open ground behind the windbreak and had planted the tree for him in the spring after, and she had sealed the whole of it away in a room of herself and had not opened the door since, and now the fever had the door, and it was open, and she could not close it.

Sammy did not sleep much. He sat by the bed with the lamp low and the kettle steaming and the wet cloths on her forehead, and he talked to her, the way you talk to a sick animal, quiet and steady, telling her about the stove and the water and the chickens and the frost on the window glass, and June listened to his voice the way you listen to a bell in a fog, and she held onto it, and she thought about her children, the ones she had raised and the ones she had buried, and about the ledger on the shelf in the next room, the book that held the weather and the seed and the lives, the book no one but her had ever read, and she thought about what would happen to it when she was gone, whether anyone would read the columns, whether anyone would know what the numbers meant, whether the book would sit on the shelf between the canning jars and hold its arithmetic in the dark like a woman holding her breath.

On the second night she woke out of the fever and found Sammy asleep in the chair beside the bed, his head down on the blanket, and she lay a long time looking at him, at the fine dark hair and the hand still curved on the blanket where it had been holding hers, and she thought about the ledger, and she made herself think it, the thing she had not let herself think: that if she died, the ledger would go unread, that the column she had kept for twenty years, the weather and the seed and the lives, would be a book nobody could read, a record in a language nobody spoke, the way a seed catalog is a record of a season that never came. She thought about Cora, who had the arithmetic in her now, who had written home about a man teaching the grower's arithmetic at a plank table, who would understand the columns the way June understood them, and she thought about Walter, who had never once asked to see the book, who had watched her write in it every spring and had never asked, and she felt the ledger's weight on her chest like a second fever, and she closed her eyes and made a plan, the way she made plans, column by column, and the plan was simple: she would not die with the book unread. She would get up. She would teach the book to whoever was left to read it.

The neighbor women came on the third day. Mrs. Whitcomb from the south quarter came first, and then two others, women of the road, women who had watched the fever go through a dozen houses, and they came in with their hands full and their mouths quiet, and they took the kettle and the rags and the sweeping up, and they sent Sammy to the barn for more wood, and they did not ask about the Hallorans, and June did not tell them.

Mrs. Whitcomb sat by the bed in the evening and looked at June's face and said, "We've sent for the doctor. He's out on the creek road but he'll come."

"No," June said. Her voice was thin and it surprised her, how thin it had got. "No doctor. I know what this is. I've watched it before. The doctor can't un-bury a thing that's been breathed in."

"You let the doctor come, June. You let him see you. That's what the doctor's for."

June looked at the ceiling, at the crack in the plaster that ran from the corner to the window like a road, and she said, "Ezra died with the doctor in the house. The doctor came and Ezra died anyway. I don't want the doctor." She closed her eyes. "I want my girl."

The words came out of her without her meaning them to, and she lay in the fever and heard them in the room, the way you hear a thing fall and break, and Mrs. Whitcomb was quiet, and then she said, "Your girl's out in California. She sent you money. The money's on the shelf."

"Five dollars." June's voice was a whisper. "She sent me five dollars, and I spent it on coal oil and potatoes and a sack of county wheat, and I never wrote her back." She opened her eyes. "I never wrote her back, did I?"

"You've been sick, June."

"I was sick before. I had a letter from my girl in my hand, the first true letter she ever wrote me, and I set it down to answer it the next day, and the next day the windbreak was down, and then I was sick, and I never wrote her back." The tears came up in her eyes, hot and sudden, and she had not cried in a year, had not cried since the grave, and the tears were a kind of breaking, and she let them come. "She's out there alone, and I never wrote her back."

The letter was on the bedside table. Sammy had put it there the night it came, had read it to her in the dark because she could not hold the page, her son's voice reading her daughter's words, and June had lain in the dark and listened to Cora's voice come out of Sammy's mouth, the work and the road and the windbreak, and she had thought she would answer it in the morning, and the morning had brought the fever, and the letter had sat unread in her own hand since.

Mrs. Whitcomb picked up the letter and turned it over. "You want me to write her?"

"No." June's hand found the letter and held it. "I want to write her myself. I want to hold the pencil and write the words. I want to tell her about the windbreak." She closed her eyes, and the fever came up like a wave, and she said, quiet, into the dark, "The windbreak is gone, and I have to tell her. It's the first thing she asked me, the first true thing, and I have to tell her it's gone."

Sammy came in with the wood and stood in the door with the armload of it, and he looked at his mother in the bed and at the women around her, and he set the wood down and came to the bed and took her hand, and he said, "You'll tell her yourself, Ma. You'll get up and you'll tell her. She's not going to come home and find you sick."

June looked at her son, the boy with his father's eyes and his own old quiet, and she held his hand, and she felt the fever move in her chest like a thing with a will, and she thought about the spring of 1934, about the boy who had gone in her arms with the grit in his lungs, and she thought about this boy, the one left, the one who had banked her stove and made her camphor rags and read her daughter's letter in the dark, and she said, "You shouldn't have to do this, Sammy."

"Somebody's got to," said Sammy, and the women were quiet, and June looked at her son and saw in his face the man he would be, and she held onto the sight of it the way you hold onto a rope, and she lay back and let the fever take her under, and the letter sat on the table beside the bed, unread, and the lamp burned low, and the women kept the kettle going, and the wind worked the north quarter where the stumps stood in the frost, and Sammy sat by the bed with his mother's hand in his and did not sleep, and the night went on, and the dust came down out of the sky in the dark and lay on the window glass like grain in a hopper, and June breathed it, and coughed it, and slept, and dreamed, and in the dream she was writing the letter, the one to her girl, and the words came out true, and she woke in the cold dark with the camphor on her chest and the boy's hand in hers and the dream still in her, the true words still in her, waiting for the morning, waiting for a hand steady enough to hold the pencil, and she closed her eyes and held onto the words the way she held onto her son's hand, and the kettle steamed, and the frost lay on the window glass, and the dust came down out of the sky all night like the years coming down, and June breathed it in and fought it, and fought it, and held on.

Chapter 20: The Dust School

The district schoolhouse stood a mile and a half down the county road, one room of grey boards with a bell tower and a stove and two privies in the yard, and on the January morning when the teacher did not come, the children stood in the yard in the cold with their lunch pails and their dust masks and waited, and the wind moved the dead grass, and the schoolhouse door stayed shut.

The teacher had been a young man from Tulsa who had lasted one term and then taken the bus west, the way everything took the bus west in that county, and the board had advertised for a replacement and had got no takers, and the children had been let out for a week, and then for two, and on the morning of the second week Mr. Putnam, the school board chairman, came up the Vogel lane in his Ford and stood on the porch with his hat in his hands.

"June," he said, "I know you're just up from a sickness, and I don't like to ask it, but the children are standing in the yard, and the term's half gone, and there's nobody in the county who'll take it." He looked at the ground, at the frozen ruts, and then at her. "We want you to teach the school."

June looked at him across the porch. She had been up from the fever for two weeks, and her hands were still thin on her, and the cough still came in the cold mornings, and she had been sitting at the kitchen table with the ledger open, writing the winter entries, the weather column filling with the grey days, when his Ford had come up the lane.

"I'm a farmer's wife," she said. "I've never taught school. I read the almanac and I keep a seed ledger and I've buried a son. That's not a teacher."

"That's more than the county's got right now," said Mr. Putnam. "You can read and you can cipher, and you keep a ledger, and a woman who keeps a ledger for twenty years knows more about arithmetic than any teacher I've had in ten. And the children —" He stopped, and June saw him work for the word, the way men worked for words in that county. "The children have had nothing but dust and boarded-up schools and fathers gone down the road. They need a woman who's stayed. They need to see somebody who stayed."

June stood in the porch cold and looked down the county road, toward the schoolhouse she could not see but knew was there, the grey boards and the bell tower and the children standing in the yard, and she thought about the teacher who had gone west, and about Walter, and about Cora, and about all the things and people that had gone west out of that county and had not come back, and she said, "I'll take it till the term ends."

She walked to the schoolhouse the next morning, a mile and a half in the cold, with the ledger under her arm and a box of books the county had lent and a jar of something for the stove, and the children were waiting in the yard, eight of them, in coats that were too thin and shoes that were too big, and they stood in a knot by the door and watched her come the way you watch a stranger come down a road.

She opened the schoolhouse and the cold came out at her, and she got the stove going, and the children filed in and sat at the desks, the double row of them, the little ones at the front and the big ones at the back, and they looked at her, eight faces in the grey light, and she looked back at them, and she did not know the first thing to say.

She said, "We're going to put on our masks, because the air's been bad, and then we're going to learn the arithmetic of a seed."

She had brought the seed catalog from the house. It was the one the county agent had left in the spring, the pictures of the beans and the corn and the tomatoes in colors that the dust had never seen, and she held it up and the little ones leaned forward and the big ones pretended not to, and she taught them their letters out of the catalog, the names of the seeds, the A of the beans and the C of the corn, and the numbers out of the back pages, the weights and the prices and the measure of a bushel, and the map of the road.

The map was the thing she had not planned. It had come up out of her the way the letter had come out of Cora, the way anything true comes out of a person who has been holding it. She had drawn the road on the blackboard with the chalk, the road west out of Cimarron County, through the panhandle and the Texas towns and New Mexico and into California, and she had marked the places with the names of the children's fathers, a pin for each man, a flag for each gone father, and the children sat and looked at the road on the blackboard and the names under it, and they did not ask the question, and June did not answer it, and the road stood on the blackboard all day like a thing that had been named out loud at last.

She taught them the ledger. She brought her own, the repurposed composition book begun in 1919, and she showed the children the columns, the weather and the seed and the lives, and she showed them how a loss went down and did not go away, how you wrote the weather first and then the seed, how a column was a kind of prayer, a way of saying this happened and this is what it cost and this is what we plant next. The children passed the book around the way you pass a holy thing, careful, and the little ones put their fingers on the columns and traced the years, and the big ones read the entries quiet, and nobody laughed at the book, because it was not a thing to laugh at, it was a thing to read the way you read a map.

There was a girl named Alice Betts, eleven years old, whose father had gone to a pea camp in the Imperial Valley and had not written in four months, and she sat at the front desk with her hands folded and her eyes on the blackboard, and on the afternoon they did the columns she raised her hand and said, "How do you write down a father who's lost?"

June stood at the blackboard with the chalk in her hand and looked at the girl, and the room was quiet, the way a room gets quiet when a true question is asked, and she said, "You write him in the lives column, and you leave the weather column open for him, because a man on the road is weather, and weather comes back." She wrote the name Betts in the air with the chalk, not on the board, and she said, "That's the column. That's where he is till the mail brings him home."

Alice Betts looked at the empty blackboard where the name had been, and she nodded, the way you nod at a thing you will carry, and she turned back to her page, and June stood with the chalk in her hand and thought about the column where a woman's own man was written, the weather column, the road column, and she did not say anything, because some columns you keep to yourself.

There was a boy named Henry who could not keep still, who had been in the school three years and could not keep still, and June found that if she gave him the seed catalog he would sit for an hour and trace the pictures of the machines, the tractors and the binders and the combines, and she let him have the catalog every afternoon, and she did not call it letting him be, she called it teaching him the way you teach a thing that has to grow in its own direction, the way a willow grows along the water.

There was the day of the storm, the last of the bad ones, when the sky went the color of a dirty nickel at noon and June made the children put on their masks and move their desks away from the windows, and the dust came against the schoolhouse like a hand, and the stove coughed, and the children sat in the dark with their masks on and did not cry, because they had done this before, this was the arithmetic of the county, and June stood at the blackboard and wrote the weather column on it with the chalk, the date and the hour and the color of the sky, and she taught them to write it down, because a thing written down is a thing you have stood against, and the children wrote the storm into their slates, and the dust moved over the schoolhouse, and when it passed the sun came back thin and pale, and the children took off their masks and looked at the grey film on their desks, and June said, "Now we know the weather. That's the first column. The rest is seed and lives, and we'll get to them all."

Mr. Putnam came to the schoolhouse at the end of the first week and stood in the back with his hat in his hands and listened, and when the children had gone home he said, "You're a teacher, June."

"I'm a farmer's wife keeping a ledger," June said. "The ledger's the only teacher I know."

"Then teach the ledger," said Mr. Putnam. "It's more than the county's had in years."

The schoolhouse became June's second windbreak. She walked the mile and a half in the mornings, the cough easing as the days warmed and the worst of the winter wore off, and she stood at the blackboard with the chalk and the dust and the eight faces, and she taught the arithmetic of the seed and the map of the road and the columns of the ledger, and the children grew in the weeks the way the windbreak seedlings grew, a little at a time, in the direction of the sun.

She wrote the term dates in the ledger on the last page, the page where the windbreak column ran and the note about the cottonwood and the note about Ezra, and she wrote: January 1936 — I am teaching the school.
