And in the margin, in the small hand she used for the things that were not seed: the children's fathers are all on the road. She did not know if the line was arithmetic or a map, and she decided it was both.

The dust school was the name the county gave it. The mothers said it at the store, "the dust school," and it was not a joke, it was a name for a thing that had no other name, a school taught by a woman who had stayed, in a county that had watched everything go west, the children sitting in their masks in the grey light learning the names of seeds and the shape of the road their fathers had taken. June taught them their letters and their numbers, and she taught them the ledger, and she taught them the road, and on the last day of the term, when the county had found a man from Boise City to take the school in the fall, she stood at the blackboard and looked at the map of the road, the chalk line going west out of the county, the names under it, the pins and the flags, and she took the eraser and she did not erase it.

She left the map on the blackboard. She left the chalk line going west, the names under it, the road that every father had taken, and the children sat in their desks and watched her leave the road there the way you leave a thing you mean to come back to, and she walked the mile and a half home in the cold with the ledger under her arm and the cough quiet in her chest, and she stood at the edge of the north quarter and looked at the stumps, and the wind came over them, and there was nothing to stop it, and June stood with the wind on her face and the ledger under her arm and thought about the map on the blackboard and the road going west and the eight children who knew the names of seeds now, and she went into the house and set the ledger on the shelf between the canning jars, and the canning jars stood in a row with the winter light on them, and the dust lay fine on the windowsill, and the wind worked the stumps in the north quarter, and the map went on holding its road on the blackboard in the empty schoolhouse, the names under it, the pins and the flags, the road that went west out of the county and came back only in the letters, the way all roads came back in that county, in the mail and in the money orders and in the voices of the children reading their fathers' names out loud in the grey light of the dust school, one room, one stove, one woman who had stayed.

Chapter 21: The Organizers

The tent stood behind the camp, out past the last row of tents, where the privies ended and the orchard gave way to alkali. It was a feed tent, patched in three places, and the patchwork held a weather in it that was not the weather. Cora came the way Miguel had told her, past the wash line and the dead fire, and lifted the flap.

Inside, the lanterns were already lit. Six men at the plank table, a woman named Mrs. Alcott who kept the camp's mending, and there, at the head, Miguel. He did not look up when she came in. He was drawing on the table with the butt of a pencil, the same pencil he had given her in the hospital tent, drawing lines the way he drew them on paper, rows and rows, and the rows were numbers.

She had carried his message for the first time that week. It was not a hard thing, what they asked. A folded paper in her shoe, walked to the camp on the far side of the county road, given to a man named Reyes who kept a mercantile under a flatbed tarp. Nothing was written on it. She had not looked. She had felt it burn against her arch with every step, and that was a feeling she kept.

"You come," Miguel said now, and she could not tell if it was a question.

"I come."

"Sit where you can hear." He did not tell her to leave, and the other men did not look at her twice. That was the arithmetic of the thing. A woman moving between camps was a woman fetching water, a woman visiting a cousin, a woman gone to the store. Nobody counted women. The growers counted sacks and weights and men. Women were the free currency of the camp, and the committee spent them carefully.

The committee had a name now, though nobody said it aloud in the daytime. The cotton workers' committee, Miguel called it on paper, and he was the only one who wrote it. The rest of them called it the meeting, or the tent, or simply us. Names were things the growers' men wrote down in books, and books were where people went to be lost.

"Read the demands," Miguel said to a man named Padilla, who was the oldest, a picker from the Delta cotton who had worked the fields since he was a child. Padilla cleared his throat. He had no papers; he read from his hands, from the map of calluses that was the only ledger he carried.

"We want the scale weighed fair," he said. "No thumb. We want the water carried to the field, not the twenty-cent canteen sold at the gate. We want the store to sell at what it costs, not what it can. We want a dollar and a half a hundred for the first picking, and a dollar sixty-five for the second, because the second is the dirtier work."

Miguel nodded once, slow, the way a man nods at a row he has counted and found true. "Say the wages again," he said.

"A dollar and a half a hundred. A dollar sixty-five."

"Now say what the grower pays."

Padilla's mouth went thin. "A dollar and a quarter, and the thumb on the scale, and the water from the tank at a nickel a dipper, and the store prices set where the store can set them. He pays what the land lets him pay, which is to say nothing."

"He pays what we let him pay," Miguel said, and the tent went still. Even the jukebox from the highway café, which bled through the canvas night and day like a second pulse, seemed to hold its breath. Then it spun on, a nickel a song, some tune out of the radio about a sweetheart and a growing old, and the silence inside the tent was not the same as the music outside it.

They went over the date three times. The first of December, when the second picking would be in full swing and the sacks would be full and the grower would need the crews most. That was the whole arithmetic, Miguel said, the one they had learned at the plank table all those evenings. A man is worth what he can withhold. The grower had the land and the money and the law. The workers had one thing. They had the sacks, and the sacks were the crop, and the crop was the only thing the grower could not grow without them.

The men at the table had a look she had come to know in the camps, the look of men who had been through the other strikes, the ones the papers had not written about, and she watched them the way she had learned to watch the sky, for the signs. Padilla with the map of calluses for a ledger. Vargas, who could not read and kept the list in his head, counting on his fingers in the dark. The two quiet men from the Reyes camp who said nothing and missed nothing. Mrs. Alcott, who had seen a hundred camps come down and knew what the coming down looked like, and who had said, the way you name a tool, that they had beaten a man at the water tank. These were the people, she understood, who would be beaten and loaded and moved and told to walk the road to the border, and they sat at the plank table with the demands folded in their pockets and they went over the date three times, because a date that is not kept is only a word, and a word is not a demand, and a demand that is not made is only a hunger.

"We walk out on the bell," Padilla said. "The bell at the field edge, noon. When it rings for the rest, we do not pick up the sacks."

"Half the crews are already pledged," said a younger man named Vargas, who kept the committee's list in a head that could not read. "I counted them at the water tank and at the fires. The Mexicans are in. The Filipinos are in. The white crews from the Panhandle, the ones who call themselves by their home counties, they are watching to see which way the wind blows."

"The wind," Miguel said, "will blow from the walkout."

There was a kind of music in the way he said it, and Cora filed it away with the other things he had taught her. He taught arithmetic at the plank table in the evenings, the arithmetic of the pay stub, the arithmetic of the weighmaster's thumb, the arithmetic of how a family of five could pick a hundred pounds and still be short at the store. He taught the way a schoolteacher teaches, which was the only way he knew, laying it out in rows, making them count it themselves until the counting was theirs. She had seen her mother do the same thing with the seed ledger, and it had taken her all these weeks to understand that the two of them were the same species of animal, her mother and this man, one counting seeds and one counting men.

"One thing more," Miguel said, and he looked up then, and his eyes found her across the tent. The lantern light did odd things to the scar on his jaw, the pale seam of it, the mark the Pixley strike had left him. "The growers have men who watch. Men at the road, men at the water tank, men who sit in cars and write down the faces that go in and out of this tent. We are known, some of us. The file at the growers' association has photographs."

He let that sit. Cora felt the word photograph pass over the table like a weather, and she thought of the folder she had never seen, the one that carried his face in it, the one that could be brought out and shown and made to mean agitator, made to mean what it wanted to mean.

"So the messages," he said, "go by the hands that are not watched. A woman moving between camps. A boy with a slingshot who runs the ditches. Cora."

She had not heard her name in this tent, in this company, and it startled her the way a dropped plate startles a quiet room.

"You know the way to the Reyes camp now. You know the way to the packing shed at Modesto Crossing. You know the county road that runs behind the orchard, where the cars do not go. We need you to carry again, tomorrow and the day after. The date is set. The word must travel."

She thought of her mother, at that hour, three states and a world away, marking the weather column in a ledger by lamplight. She thought of the five dollars she had sent home, and the letter that had lied about the food. She thought of what her mother would say if she could see her now, a girl in a patched feed tent, being given a job by a man with a scar and a blacklist photograph, being made a part of something.

Her mother would have marked it in the ledger. Weather: fair. Seed: one girl, sent out, may or may not come back. There was a kind of faith in that arithmetic that Cora was only beginning to understand.

"I'll carry," she said.

Mrs. Alcott leaned forward. "They beat a man last week at the water tank. The growers' men. They beat him and they took his sack and they told him to walk the road to the border, and he walked." She did not say it to frighten Cora. She said it the way you name a tool before you hand it over, so that nobody can say they were not told.

"I know," Cora said.

"Then you know what you are carrying for."

The meeting did not end so much as dissolve. The men stood and drifted out the back of the tent in twos and threes, no lanterns, no goodnights, men turning into the dark like water finding its level. Padilla last, laying a hand on Miguel's shoulder that said what years of shared work say. Mrs. Alcott gathered the lamp and the stub of pencil and went out after them, and the tent went dark around the two of them, and Cora stayed at the flap and listened to the camp settle around its fires, the low talk, the cough of an old man, a child crying and being soothed, the whole fabric of the night that the growers did not see and did not want to see.

"You do not have to do this," Miguel said. "The carrying. You have a way out of this valley, and most of them do not. You have a mother who writes you letters. You could go home."

"I could," she said.

"And still you sit here."

She thought about her answer for a long moment, and what she said surprised her, because it came out in her mother's cadence, in the plain-church rhythm of a woman counting out what she has.

"I came west with a seed packet in my coat that I have never planted. I have carried it through every camp and every county line and every pay stub, and I have not opened it, because opening it seemed like giving up. But a seed is a thing you put in the ground because you believe the ground will answer. I am tired of carrying a seed that does not go in the ground. This is the ground." She lifted her chin at the tent, at the dark beyond it where the camp slept. "You taught me the grower's arithmetic, Miguel. This is me putting it to work."

He looked at her a long time, and in the lantern light the scar on his jaw went soft, the way a scar does when the face it is on is not braced against anything.

"Your mother," he said, "must be some woman."

"She is," Cora said. "She keeps a ledger. She would have liked you."

He smiled, and it was the real thing, not the careful one he wore in the day. Then he reached into his shirt and held out the pencil, the one he had given her in the hospital tent, the one she had given back so he could keep drawing his rows on the table.

"Keep it," he said. "You will need to write the date down where the growers cannot find it."

She took it, and it was warm from his chest, and she put it in her shoe where the message had been, where the burning was. And she walked out into the camp night with the jukebox still spinning its nickel song on the highway, and the first of December sitting on the calendar like a held breath, and the taste of the dust in her mouth like a promise she had not meant to make and now could not take back. Behind her the tent stood empty in the dark, its patchwork holding a weather that was not the weather, and above the orchard the first stars came out, the same stars that would come out over a farm on the Cimarron at that hour, over a mailbox at a fence line, over a woman who was at that moment walking her dusk vigil and counting days like seeds.

Chapter 22: The Walkout

The bell was a length of rail hung from the cotton scale frame at the field edge, and for three years it had rung the crews to their work and rung them away from it. On the first of December it rang once, at noon, and it was the last bell the growers would own for a month.

Cora was at the water tank when it happened, filling the dipper for an old woman who could not bend. The bell rang, and the old woman's hand stopped halfway to the dipper, and Cora looked out across the field and saw the pickers straighten. They had been bent over the white rows since before light, a thousand backs, and now a strange arithmetic ran through them. They did not pick up the next sack. They stood, and one by one they let the full sacks fall from their hands, and the sacks lay in the rows like so many dropped names.

She had carried the word to seven camps in three days. She knew what was supposed to happen. Knowing and seeing were two different things, and she stood at the water tank and watched the thing she had carried happen, and it was bigger than the word for it.

The first men reached the field edge. They did not run. Running was for the guilty, and they had done nothing but stand up. They walked, and the crews behind them folded in like a wind taking a field, and by the time the grower's car came down the county road with its plume of dust, there were eight hundred of them standing at the fence in the noon heat, empty-handed, empty-sacked, present.

Earl the foreman was the first out of the car. Cora had worked his fields since July, had watched his thumb on the scale, had been marked by him in the fall for saying the cheat aloud. He walked the line of the fence looking at them, and when his eyes found her at the water tank his mouth did something ugly.

"You, girl," he said. "What do you think you're doing?"

"Standing," Cora said.

The word went around. Earl's face went dark, and then the grower himself, Mr. Gillis, was out of the car, and Earl stepped back because the grower owned the ground they stood on and the scale and the store and, for the moment, the weather.

Mr. Gillis was a thin man with a ledger of his own in his head, and he stood at the fence and surveyed the crews the way a man surveys a field he means to harvest. He did not shout. He had learned long ago that shouting was a tool that dulled.

"There is a hundred dollars a day in that cotton," he said, to the air, to the crews, to the foreman. "It is ripe. It will not wait. You pick it, or it rots, and you do not eat."

Padilla stepped forward. The oldest picker, the one who had read the demands from his hands, and he stood a head shorter than the grower and carried himself like a man who had been paid short so many times the difference no longer surprised him.

"Pick it for what?" he said. "For the thumb on the scale? For the nickel dipper? We pick it for a dollar and a half a hundred, fair weight, and we pick it tomorrow. The cotton will still be there."

"It will be there," the grower said, "and so will a man from the county sheriff's office. And so will the trucks."

The word trucks was meant to do something, and it did. Cora saw it move through the crews like a hand through grain, the ones who had been through the Pixley strike, the ones who knew what the trucks carried besides scab crews. Miguel had told her about the trucks, the strike trucks, the deputy cars that rode in behind them. She had seen his face when he said it, and she had understood that the trucks were not the worst part.

The crews did not move. Padilla stood his ground, and behind him the line held, and the grower looked at them a long moment and got back in his car, and Earl got back in beside him, and the car turned around in the road and took its dust back down the county road to the house.

That was the first day.

The picket line formed where the field road met the county road. They made it out of what they had, a rope of sacking run between the two fence posts, a sign that Padilla's son had lettered on a length of crate wood. The sign said: WE WANT A FAIR SCALE. It was the only demand that fit on one board, and it was the one that mattered.

The camp turned itself into something it had never been. Women took up the cooking in shifts so the men could hold the line. Mrs. Alcott organized the mending. The children, who had picked since they could reach, were set to carrying water and running messages, and Cora saw the old woman from the tank walking the line with a wet cloth tied over her nose against the dust, walking it like a sentry, and something in Cora's chest went tight and full.

The singing started on the second day. It was not the singing she knew from the road, the jukebox songs, the revival songs. It was working songs, picking songs, songs in Spanish and English mixed, the kind of songs that grow in a camp the way dust grows in a field, out of nothing, out of the space between the rows. They sang them at the line and at the fires, and the growers' cars that drove past at dusk to count them slowed down to listen and then sped up, as if the singing were a thing they were afraid of catching.

The songs had words for the weighing, and the words were old, older than the camps, older than the valley, and Cora heard in them the same arithmetic she had learned at the plank table, the thumb and the tare and the store, sung in a minor key so that they came out as something you could hold, a weight that had a shape. She learned one of them by the second day, a short one, and she sang it under her breath while she carried the water, and the carrying went faster with the singing, which was the whole point of a working song, and the old woman from the tank sang it with her, their two voices thin in the dust, and Cora understood that the singing was the strike's other ledger, the one the growers could not read, because the growers could not hear what the songs were counting.

Cora carried water to the line. It was the work the committee gave her, and it was the work she was good at, moving where the men were watched, unhurried, a girl with a canteen and a dipper, nobody. She walked the length of the line three times a day, and each time she passed the men she had carried the word to, the men from the Reyes camp, the men from the packing shed, and she watched them hold.

On the third day the strike trucks came.

They came down the county road at first light, three flatbeds full of men, and behind them a deputy's car, and behind the deputy's car a second car with two men in suits who were not sheriffs. The trucks stopped at the field gate, and the men on the flatbeds looked out at the picket line and at the field beyond it and at the white cotton standing in the rows, and Cora saw what was in their faces. They were not strikebreakers in the way the word meant it. They were men out of work, men who had ridden the rails into the valley looking for a day's pick, and the growers' men had found them at the freight yards and the labor office and promised them a dollar a hundred and a truck ride.

"Scabs," somebody said on the line, and the word was a wound, and Cora saw the men on the trucks hear it and go still.

Miguel came up beside her. He had not slept, she could tell, his eyes were the color of the road. He looked at the trucks for a long moment, and then he did something that surprised her. He walked to the fence and climbed the gate and stood where both sides could see him, and he raised his hand.

"Men," he said, and his voice carried, the schoolteacher's voice, the voice that had taught a whole town to count. "You are being paid a dollar a hundred to cross a line of men who are asking for a dollar fifty and a fair scale. You will carry that cotton for a dollar. We will carry it for a dollar fifty. The difference is fifty cents a hundred, and it is not yours and it is not ours. It is the grower's thumb. When you cross this line, you are not taking our work. You are taking his thumb. You are doing for free what we are refusing to do for short."

There was silence on the flatbeds. The men looked at each other, and the driver of the first truck gunned the engine, and the deputy got out of his car and stood with his hand on the door, watching.

One man on the first truck climbed down. He was young, Cora saw, younger than Miguel, with a sack over his shoulder and the look of a man who had not eaten well in a week. He climbed down and he stood in the road, and the line on the picket fence opened for him like a gate, and he walked through it to the camp side.

"His wife's sick," the man said to nobody, to everybody. "I been promised work since the truck yard. But I got no stomach for the thumb."

Two more climbed down. Then the driver of the first truck got back in and drove it through the field gate anyway, into the rows, and the deputy followed on foot, and the men who stayed on the trucks went with the cotton.

They picked that day, the scab crews, and the picket line watched them. But they picked slow, and they picked light, and Cora saw the foreman moving among them with his ledger, and she saw the men straighten when his back was turned and stand and look out at the line with a kind of longing, and she understood that the strike was working, was working in the only way it could work, which was slowly, the way a seed works.

That night the deputy arrested Padilla.

They came for him at the camp fire, two deputies and the man in the suit who was not a sheriff. They read a paper that said he was inciting, and they took him by the arms, and the whole camp stood in a ring around the fire and watched, and nobody moved, because the men who moved were the next ones in the cars. Padilla did not fight. He went the way a man goes when he has been through it before, quiet, his head up, his hands open at his sides.

"He's an old man," Cora heard a woman say, and it was not a complaint, it was a kind of counting.

The deputy's car took the county road back toward town, and the camp stood in the dust of it, and the fire burned down, and the singing started again, low, a picking song, and the men took it up until the whole camp was singing it, and the sound of it followed the deputy's car down the road like a thing that would not stay buried.

Cora carried water to the line the next morning, and the morning after, and the canteen was heavy and the dust was in her lungs and her hands were cracked from the dipper's handle, and she did not know yet that the worst of it was coming, that the arithmetic of the strike had a term she had not been taught, that the growers' men had a book with her name being written into it. She only knew that the line held, and that the men who had crossed it from the trucks were being fed at the camp fire now, and that somewhere in a county jail an old man was holding a demand in his head like a seed, and that the singing went on.

The line held nine days. She would remember the days the way you remember the days of a fever, by their shape rather than their number, by the way the light changed and the way the singing changed and the way the growers' cars slowed at the bend and did not stop. On the sixth day a woman from the county relief came out to the line with a basket and asked the pickets what they needed, and they told her water and the scales and their men out of the jail, and she wrote it down in a notebook and drove away, and Cora watched her go and understood that the county had begun to count them too, the way the growers counted them, the way the papers counted them, and the counting was the thing, the counting was the whole of it, and the line held, and the singing went on, and she would learn the rest soon enough.

She would learn the rest soon enough.

Chapter 23: Cora Beaten

The raid came at midnight, and it came with the lights off.

Cora was awake. She had been awake past midnight for a week, sleeping in the slivers between her turns at the line, and the sleep she got was the shallow kind that hears everything. She heard the trucks first, engines cut to idle, rolling down the county road dark, and she was on her feet before the first tent flap moved, because she knew the sound the way a picker knows the sound of a frost coming.

The tents were not ready. That was the thing she would turn over in her mind in the weeks to come, the arithmetic of it. They had held the line by day, and at night they had let themselves believe the day was the whole of it. The men who watched from the county road had watched at dusk and seen the camp at rest, and they had waited for the rest.

The first crash was a tent pole going over. Then a second, and the dark filled with noise, with shouting and the flat crack of wood on canvas and the low answering sound of men being hit, and the camp came alive all at once, badly, the way a startled animal comes alive.

Cora ran for Rosa's tent. It was not far, three tents down the row, and she went through the dark without a lantern because a lantern was a target, and she heard Rosa's voice before she reached it, a thin high sound that stopped, and then the sound of the tent being torn open.

What happened next she would never be able to tell in a straight line. There were men in the tent, and Rosa was small and she was fighting, and Cora came in behind the biggest of them and took hold of his arm with both hands and pulled, and he turned, and the dark did the rest.

She had been told the word for it. Vigilantes. Deputies. The growers' men. But the dark did not care about words, and the boot did not care, and the fist did not care, and they came from every direction and none, and she went down in the mud of the tent floor with Rosa's name still on her tongue and the sound of canvas ripping and the smell of dust and sweat and the sharp smell of the oil the men had brought to burn the tents.

There was a moment — she would find it later, whole, like a thing that had been saved for her — when the noise stopped. The men stood back, and somebody struck a match, and in the match's light she saw them all at once, the faces, the suits, the boots, the man who was not a sheriff standing in the torn flap of the tent with a folded paper in his hand, and behind him, in the circle of the light, the face of Mr. Gillis.

The grower looked down at her. She was on her knees in the mud, and she was bleeding from somewhere, and she could not feel the place it was bleeding from, and his face was a ledger with the page already turned.

"We'll teach you to reckon," he said.

She heard it. The word, her word, her mother's word, the word she had carried across a continent in her coat pocket and her chest. The word that meant counting, and summing, and the arithmetic of justice. He gave it to her twisted, the way you give a man back his own knife, and she understood in that moment that he did not know what he had said, that he thought he was using her word against her and did not know it was the one word in all the world that could not be used against her, because it was not a threat. It was a promise. A reckoning was a thing that came due.

Then the match went out, and the boot came, and the dark closed over her like a hand.

She came to in water. That was the first thing she knew, that she was in water, and the second thing she knew was that it was raining, a thin cold rain over the camp, and the third was that the mud had her and she was face-down in it and the rain was falling on her back and she could not tell how long she had been there.

She tried to move and the pain came in and took the breath out of her. Ribs. She had heard of cracked ribs, she had heard the men at the camp fire describe them, and now she had some of her own and they were a thing you could not describe to someone who did not have them. She got one hand under her and pushed, and the world went gray and came back, and she pushed again.

The memory of the night came back in pieces, the way a thing comes back through water. The tent going over. Rosa's voice, cut off. The man turning, his face not a face, a shape in the dark. The match. Mr. Gillis. The word. The boot. She lay in the mud with the rain on her back and she took the pieces and she tried to fit them into a shape she could keep, the way you sort a broken crate to see what can be saved, and the pieces she could not fit were the ones with Mr. Gillis in them, the ledger of his face, the folded paper in the hand of the man who was not a sheriff, the calm of the whole thing, the calm of a thing that had been planned and written down and counted out, and she understood that the raid had not been a storm that came on them. It had been a weighing, and the camp had been weighed in the dark, and the dark had found it wanting.

The camp was gone. Not the people — the tents. The tents were torn and flat, some of them burning, the fires the only light in the rain, and the people were moving through the dark carrying what they could, and over it all the rain fell, thin and patient, the first rain of the season, falling on the burned tents and the scattered belongings and the white cotton still standing in the rows.

She heard a voice. An old voice, calling a name, calling her name, calling it over and over the way you call a name into a field, and she tried to answer and the answer came out as a sound that was not a word.

It was Lula who found her. Lula, the camp elder, the woman who had made the crossing since 1929, who had taken her in at the first camp on the Texas-New Mexico line, who had taught her what a wash costs and what a canteen costs and how a woman alone is priced. Lula came down the row through the rain with her skirts gathered, and she stopped at the place where Cora lay, and she did not cry out. She had been through the camps too long to cry out. She knelt in the mud beside her and she took Cora's face in her two old hands and she looked at what the dark had done to it, and her face did not change.

"Hush," Lula said. "Hush now. You hush."

She got Cora up, and the world went gray again, and when it came back she was half-carrying, half-dragging her down the row past the flat tents and the burning ones, and the rain was washing the mud off her and the blood off her and it did not seem like enough water in all the world.

Somewhere in the dark Miguel was calling her name. She heard it the way you hear a voice in a dream, and she tried to answer, and then there were hands lifting her, gentler hands, and she was in Miguel's arms and he was carrying her and his face was the last thing she saw clearly, the scar on his jaw pale in the rain, his mouth moving, saying her name, saying other things she could not hear, and she thought, he is alive, they did not take him, and the thought was a door closing softly behind her.

The rain went on. It fell on the camp and the burned tents and the scattered belongings, and it fell on the white cotton still standing in the rows, and it fell on the place in the mud where she had lain, and by the time the light came the mud had closed over the mark of her, the way the dust had closed over the fence lines and the stumps and the grave's marker at home. The rain was washing the camp away, and she did not know yet whether it was washing it away or washing it clean.

In the gray hour before dawn, while the last fires smoked and the people moved their salvaged things through the wet, a deputy's car came down the county road and stopped at the field gate, and a man in a suit got out and tacked a paper to the gatepost and drove on. Cora did not see it. She was past seeing, being carried now through the wet orchard toward the road where a truck idled with its lights off, Lula's hand on her back, Miguel's arms around her, and the rain falling. She would learn about the paper later, the way she would learn everything about that night, in pieces, from other mouths: that it was the blacklist, posted where the whole camp could read it, and her name was on it, and the name of every man and woman who had stood at the line or carried water to it or been seen going in and out of the feed tent behind the camp.

There was a moment on the wet road, before the truck, when the rain let up and the sky went gray toward dawn, and the orchard stood around them in the half-light, the wet leaves hanging, the drip of the rain, and Miguel set her down against the truck's tailgate and Lula came up with a blanket and wrapped it around her, and the three of them stood there in the gray with the whole ruined camp behind them, and nobody said the easy thing, and then Lula said, "You are not alone," and it was the truest thing that had been said all night, and Cora held onto it the way she held onto the word in her chest, the word from the grower's mouth, the word that was hers now, and the truck took them away down the wet road toward the fairgrounds, and the gray came up, and the rain went on.

She would learn it in the weeks to come, in the hospital tent, lying flat with her ribs taped, learning the shape of what had happened to her the way you learn a country you have been carried through in the dark. But in that hour she knew only the rain and the mud and the sound of Miguel's voice saying her name, and the seed packet in her coat, folded in its square of paper, a little wet at one corner, the thing that had crossed the whole country with her and had not been opened and would not be opened, holding its small faith against the dark the way she held hers.

When she woke it was light, and she was not in the mud. She was on a cot, and there was canvas over her, and a woman in a white dress with a nurse's cap was looking down at her, and the first word that came to her mouth, the word she said before she remembered her own name, was her mother's word.

"Reckoning," Cora said, and the nurse did not know what she meant, and Cora did not know how to explain it. She only knew that she had heard it said to her in the dark by a man who thought he was breaking her with it, and that it had not broken her, and that now it was hers in a way it had not been before, the way a seed is yours once you have carried it through the mud. She held onto it the way you hold a thing in your fist so that no one can take it, and the rain went on over the camp, thin and patient, and somewhere down the row Lula was gathering what could be gathered, and Miguel was alive, and the cotton stood white in the rows, and the line had not been broken. The line had been beaten, but the line had not been broken.

Chapter 24: The Hospital Tent

The relief hospital was a long canvas tent set up on the county fairgrounds, and it had a smell that Cora came to know better than any smell in her life. It was the smell of lye and bandages and the wet canvas and, underneath all of it, the particular smell of people being hurt, which was not the same as the smell of people being sick. She had time to learn the difference. They kept her three weeks.

Nurse O'Dell ran the tent the way a farmer runs a fence line, checking every post, every wire, every day. She was a big woman with a flat voice and hands that were always warm, and she had seen the camps and the strike and the busted ones come through her canvas for years, and nothing surprised her, which was the most comforting thing about her. She set Cora's ribs with her own hands, taping them so tight Cora could only breathe shallow, and she stitched the cut above Cora's eye with a needle she sterilized over the spirit lamp, and she did not ask what had happened, because she knew, and asking was a luxury the tent could not afford.

"Your ribs will heal," she said. "Your face will heal. The rest is up to you."

Cora lay on the cot and watched the tent fill and empty. The first day they brought in three more, a man from the Reyes camp with his arm in a bad way and two boys who had been caught in the tent fires. The second day, a woman whose husband had been taken. The tent was a ledger of the strike, and Nurse O'Dell wrote the entries in her head.

Rosa came on the third day. She came in the afternoon with a jar of something in her hands, and she sat on the edge of the cot and did not look at Cora's face, and then she looked, and her eyes went bright and she did not let them spill.

"They burned our tent," Rosa said. "Everything. The mattress, the blanket, the picture of my mother. Lula is taking us in, her and the girl."

Cora tried to sit up and the tape held her still, and she lay back and looked at the canvas overhead. "Are you hurt?"

"I am not hurt. I hid in the ditch. I watched them do it." Rosa's voice was flat, the way a voice goes flat when it has seen too much to fit in a voice. "They had a list, Cora. I saw it in the man's hand, the one who was not a sheriff. Names. And they went down the row and they did not read it, but they knew where to go. They went to the tents of the men who had been to the meetings. They did not go to the others."

The word list went into Cora like a splinter. The blacklist file, Miguel had said. The file at the growers' association, with the photographs. She had thought of it as a thing in a drawer somewhere, a paper, a folder. It was not a folder. It was a map of the camp, and somebody had drawn it with names, and the names had feet to walk on.

"Miguel?" she said.

"He is all right. He is not in the camp. He is staying at the Reyes place across the road, and he sends word every day, and he says to tell you that the strike holds." Rosa looked at her then, straight, and her voice changed. "He asked about you three times. I told him you would be angry to hear that."

Cora laughed, and the laugh caught in her taped ribs, and it came out as a wheeze, and Rosa smiled, and for a moment the tent was almost a tent again, a place where two girls had worked the same row and shared the same canteen.

Rosa left her the jar. It was pickled peaches, Rosa's mother's recipe, and Cora ate them one by one over the next week, saving the syrup, and each peach was a small proof that the world outside the tent still had sweetness in it.

Miguel came at night.

It was the ninth day, and she was stronger, sitting up against the cot's frame with the stitches above her eye pulling when she blinked. The tent was dark and the lamp was low and the other cots were sleeping, and she heard the flap lift and saw his shape against the dark and knew him before she saw his face.

He came and sat on the edge of the cot the way Rosa had, and he did not speak for a long moment, and in the lamplight she saw the scar on his jaw and the lines around his eyes that had not been there in July, and she understood that the strike was costing him too, in a currency that did not show in the papers.

"Your face," he said. It was not a question.

"It will heal. The nurse says the rest is up to me."

"She is right." He looked at his hands, the schoolteacher's hands, the callus from the pencil where he gripped it too hard. "I should have known they would come at night. I have been through this before, and I should have known. I set the line by day and I forgot that the day is not the whole of it. The men who beat you — they are not stupid men. They are patient. They watch the camp at dusk and they see the rest, and they know that rest is where the line is weakest."

"You did not beat me," Cora said. "You taught me to count. They are not the same thing."

He looked at her a long time, and something in his face gave way, the careful thing he wore in the day coming down for a second, and she saw the man underneath it, tired, precise, unillusioned, and she understood that he was carrying a weight he did not let the camp see.

"The strike holds," he said. "That is what I came to tell you. Padilla is still in the county jail, but the jail is not the strike. The crews are out. The cotton is rotting in the rows or being picked slow by the men from the trucks, and the growers are losing more every day than it would have cost them to give us the scale. The papers have written about it. The one in San Francisco sent a man. They have a name for us now."

"What name?"

"Strikers." He said it flat, and then he almost smiled. "It is not a good name, and it is not a fair name, and it is ours. They have given us a name, and a name is the first thing you need to hold a thing. We are no longer the men in the camp. We are the strikers, and the papers know our number, and the growers cannot arrest a number."

The word went into her the way the other words had gone into her, the word from the grower's mouth twisted into a threat, and now this word, strikers, given to them like a name given to a child, and she held them side by side in her chest, the threat and the name, and she did not know which one would win.

"Write nothing home," he said. "The letters go through the county post office, and the county post office is a window with eyes. I know it is hard. But the letters you send now will find their way into a file, and the file has your name being written into it. Write nothing until it is over. When it is over, you will write the truest letter of your life."

She thought of her mother, three states and a world away, marking the weather column by lamplight, waiting at a mailbox that was a shrine, and the letters she had not sent since the strike began sat in her chest like unsown seed.

"When will it be over?" she asked.

"When the growers count what it costs them and find it costs more than the scale." He stood, and he looked down at her, and in the lamplight his face was tender and precise and tired, and he reached out and touched the edge of the bandage above her eye, the way you touch a thing to see if it is real. "I will come when I can. Keep the pencil. It is the only thing of mine they have not found a way to take."

She reached up and caught his wrist, and he stopped, and they stayed that way a moment, her hand around his wrist, the pulse of it under her fingers, and she said, "The night of the raid. I heard you calling my name. Before I lost the light. I want you to know I heard it." He looked at her a long moment, and then he sat back down on the edge of the cot, and he did not take his wrist away, and they stayed that way in the low light of the tent, the way you stay with a thing you have both nearly lost, and neither of them said anything, and the tent breathed around them, and it was enough.

He was gone before she could answer, the flap falling closed behind him, and the tent went back to its breathing, the slow sounds of the sleeping and the hurt, and outside the rain had stopped and the sky had cleared and the stars were out, the same stars that would be out over the Cimarron at that hour, over a farm where a woman kept a ledger and a mailbox that was a shrine, and Cora lay on the cot with her taped ribs and her stitches and the taste of pickled peach in her mouth and she did not write home. She did not write anything. She held the name they had been given and the word they had taken from her and the pencil in her shoe, and she waited, the way a seed waits, in the dark, underground, where the only proof of life is the holding still.

In the days that followed she learned the shape of the tent's other hours. In the mornings Nurse O'Dell made the rounds with a pan of water and a rag, and the tent smelled of lye, and the old man from the Reyes camp with the bad arm learned to whistle between his teeth to keep from moaning, and the two boys from the tent fires played a card game on the cot between them with a deck that was missing three cards, and they played it seriously, the way children play at a thing that is the only order they have. In the afternoons the visitors came, the camp women with jars and loaves and the word of the line, and the tent filled with the low talk of the strike, the prices, the weather, the men who had been taken. Cora lay in the middle of it and listened, and she learned more about the arithmetic of the strike in those afternoons than she had learned in all the meetings, because the women did not talk in rows. They talked in the spaces between the rows, the true arithmetic, the cost of a sack and the cost of a man and the cost of a season, and she heard the growers' thumb weighed against the camp's hunger and found short.

And each night, when the lamp was low and the tent was sleeping, she took out the pencil Miguel had given her and she wrote on a scrap of paper the entry she would not send, the way her mother wrote the entries she would not send, and the entries were the same entry, the weather column and the seed column and the lives column, and the lives column said: one girl, still holding. She kept the scraps folded in the tin with her other things, and on the last night, the twentieth, she wrote the one she would send, the letter to her mother that said only what could be said, and she folded it small and put it in her coat beside the seed packet, and she lay back on the cot and listened to the old man breathe and the boys breathe and the canvas move in the wind, and she understood that the holding still was not a stopping. It was a gathering, the way a seed gathers, the way a root gathers, and the gathering was the work, and the work was hers.

Chapter 25: Miguel's History

He came on the twelfth night, and this time he stayed.

The tent was quieter than it had been. The three men from the Reyes camp had gone out, mended enough to walk, and the woman whose husband had been taken had been taken herself, by a cousin, into a camp up the valley. Only Cora and an old man with a bad lung were left, and the old man slept through everything, his breathing a slow saw. Nurse O'Dell had gone to her cot behind the curtain, and the lamp was trimmed low, and when the flap lifted she knew it was Miguel before she saw him, because the night had a way of announcing him.

He brought a chair, the one from beside the nurse's desk, and he set it at the foot of her cot and sat in it backwards, his arms folded on the back, the way a man sits when he means to stay a while. He looked at her, and she looked back, and neither of them said the easy thing.

"You asked me once," he said, "how I got the scar."

She had asked him, weeks ago, at the plank table, in the middle of a lesson about the weighmaster's thumb, and he had not answered, and she had not asked again. It was the kind of question you learned not to ask in a camp, the way you learned not to ask a man where he was from or what he had done, because the answer was always the same answer, wearing different clothes.

"Pixley," he said. "October of 'thirty-three. Do you know the name?"

"The strike," Cora said. "The one everybody says not to say."

"That is the one." He said it without bitterness, the way a man names a scar on his own body. "The cotton was in. The growers had a good year, the first in a while, and they did not raise the wages, and the crews walked out. I was not an organizer then. I was a teacher, and I had been a teacher for six years, and I did not know the first thing about a strike except that I had read about them in books, and books are not the same as a field."

He had been born, he told her, in Las Vegas, New Mexico, the town with the name of the other country, the town where the railroad came through and the river ran green in the spring. His father had worked the section gang for the railroad, laying and lifting the ties along the line, and his mother had kept a garden in the yard behind the company house, a garden that was the talk of the street because it grew things the neighbors said could not grow there, tomatoes and beans and a single apricot tree. The apricot tree was the one thing he spoke of with tenderness, the way other men spoke of mothers or of home. In the fall of his ninth year the tree bore a full crop, and he had eaten the first apricot off it with his sister, the sister who stayed, the sister who was three years younger and had his mother's face.

"My mother kept a ledger," he said, and Cora felt the words go through her like a current. "A composition book. She wrote in it the seeds she planted and the weather of each season and what came up and what did not. I never understood it. It seemed to me a waste of pages to write down a bean. But when she died, in 'twenty-eight, the ledger went to my sister, and I know my sister reads it still, the way you read a letter from someone who cannot write anymore."

The school in Las Vegas. He had gone to the teachers' college in Santa Fe, the first in his family, and he had come back to teach in the district school on the south side, the one the county said was a Mexican school and the parents said was their school. He had taught arithmetic and reading and the names of the states, and he had kept a second ledger, the attendance one, in which he wrote the names of children who came hungry and the marks of the days they did not come at all.

"The school-fund protest," Cora said. "The one that got you fired."

"The board cut the free lunch program," he said. "The county said there was no money, which meant there was no money for the Mexican children, and the board chairman, Mr. Putnam — "

"Putnam," Cora said. "My mother teaches at a school. The chairman of her board is named Putnam."

He looked at her, and the coincidence sat between them for a moment, two men with the same name in two schools a thousand miles apart, and then he went on. "Mr. Putnam said the money was not there, and I wrote it down in my own ledger, the amounts, the years, what the school had bought and what it had not bought, and I took it to the parents' meeting, and the parents took it to the county paper, and the county paper printed it, and the board voted to dismiss me in November of 'thirty-one. The dismissal said I had failed to maintain discipline. The parents' meeting said I had taught the children to count, and it was true. I had taught them to count, and it was the one thing the board could not forgive."

He went to Pixley because the work was there, and the work was all there was. He picked cotton. He had a teacher's hands and he learned the picker's hands, and the scars came, and the calluses, and he learned the arithmetic of the scale, the thumb, the tare, the wet-weight trick, the same arithmetic he had taught her at the plank table, because it was the arithmetic of a whole life, and he could teach it because he had lived it. When the strike came in October of 'thirty-three, he was not an organizer. He was a picker who could read and write, and the men who could not read and write came to him with their papers, their pay stubs, their letters from home, and he read them aloud by the fire, and the growers' men came to the camp and took his name.

The night of the beating he was at the water tank. He had carried water all day to the line, the way she had carried it, the job they gave the ones who could not be seen, and the men had come for him at the tank, four of them, and they had not asked his name because they had it written down, and the mark they left him was the mark that stayed.

"They told me to walk the road to the border," he said. "It is what they always tell us. It is the arithmetic they know. You are not a man, you are a number in a file, and a number can be moved. But I did not walk to the border. I walked down the road and around the hill and back to the camp, and I have been walking back ever since. It is the only walk I know how to make."

He was quiet then, and the tent was quiet, the old man's breathing a slow saw, the lamp low, the night pressing on the canvas like a weight. Cora lay on the cot with her taped ribs and her stitches and she looked at the man sitting backwards on the chair, and she saw the scar on his jaw and the file in the growers' association with his photograph in it and the ledger his mother had kept and the sister who stayed, and she understood something about him that she had been circling for months, which was that he carried his family the way she carried hers, in a ledger that could not be taken.

"You remind me of my sister," he said. "Not your face. Your stillness. She was still the way you are still. When I was beaten in Pixley and the news got home, she wrote me a letter, and in the letter she said, you will come back now, you have taught enough, and I could not go back, and I have not gone back, and it is the one thing I have not been able to teach myself." He looked at her, and his voice was gentle, the way it was gentle when he taught her the arithmetic and she finally understood it. "When you go home, Cora, and you will go home, because you have a home that will have you — when you go, go the way she did not let me go. Go in the day, and go with your eyes open, and do not carry the dark out with you."

"How do you know I will go home?"

"Because you carry a seed packet," he said, "and you have never planted it. A person who carries a seed unplanted across a whole country is a person who is saving it for the ground that will answer. This is not your ground." He said it without sadness, the way you say a true thing. "It is a good ground. It has taught you. But it is not your ground, and a seed knows."

The word went into her, and it sat where the other words had sat, the grower's word and the name they had been given, and she turned it over and over, the way you turn a coin to read both sides. She had come west with her mother's seed in her coat and her mother's ledger in her blood, and she had thought the west was the ground she was meant for, and here was the man she had come to believe in telling her that the ground was not hers, that the seed would not answer here.

"I have a pencil for you," he said, and he reached into his shirt and brought out the pencil, the one he had given her in the hospital tent, the one she had given back so he could draw his rows on the table. He held it out, and she took it, and it was warm, and he said, "I brought it back because I want you to have it. Not for the strike. For the ledger. When you go home, you will have need of a pencil, and I want you to have mine."

She held it, and she did not know what to say, and she said the only true thing she had. "I will keep it," she said. "On the shelf with my mother's ledger. It will be the proof that I learned to count here, from a man who taught me that a number is a thing you can be."

He smiled, and it was the real smile, the one he wore when the careful thing came down, and he stood and he set the chair back where he had found it, and he looked at her a long moment from the flap, and he said, "The strike holds. Padilla is out of the jail as of this afternoon. The growers have made an offer. We are counting it, the way you and I counted the pay stubs, line by line."

"Will you take it?"

"I do not know yet. I will know when I have counted it to the end." He lifted the flap, and the night came in, cool and clear, and he said, "Goodnight, Cora. Keep the pencil. It will outlast all of it."

The flap closed, and she lay in the dark with the pencil warm in her hand, and she thought about his mother's ledger and the sister who stayed and the apricot tree that bore a full crop in the fall of his ninth year, and she thought about the seed packet in her coat that she had never planted, and she held the two of them together, his tree and her seed, and she understood, for the first time, that the choice she thought she had a year to make was already ripening in her chest, the way a seed ripens, in the dark, underground, where the only proof of life is the holding still.

Outside, the night went on over the fairgrounds. A truck went down the county road, and the sound of it faded into the distance, and an owl called from the orchard and was answered from the far side of the tent, and the old man with the bad lung turned in his sleep and his breathing changed and steadied. Cora lay awake with the pencil in her hand, and she went over the whole of what he had told her, the section gang and the company house and the apricot tree and the teachers' college and the ledger that went to his sister and the school-fund protest and the board that could not forgive a man for teaching children to count, and she understood that his history was a ledger too, the same book her mother kept and the same book the growers kept, and that the only difference was whose hand was writing in it. His hand, she thought. He has kept his hand on the pen. And she held the pencil he had given her, his hand, her hand, the one instrument that could not be taken, and she slept at last, and in her sleep she was carrying water to a line of men who stood in a field of white cotton, and the line held, and when she woke the pencil was still warm in her hand.

Chapter 26: June's Vigil

February came in on a dry wind, and the mail did not come with it.

June had known the date of the last letter the way she knew the date of the first frost. It was December the ninth, and it was the shortest letter Cora had ever written, two lines about the work and the weather, and it had been signed with the name she had not used since she left. Your Cora. June had read it so many times in the month after that the paper had gone soft at the folds, the way a page goes soft when it is turned too often.

Now it was the middle of February, and the mailbox stood at the fence line like a small dry church, and the road beyond it was empty, and the wind blew down it from the west, from the direction of California, and it brought nothing but dust.

She walked the fence line at dusk. It was a thing she had taken to doing without deciding to do it, the way you take to a walk that has a shape. Down the east fence to the corner post, across the back fence where the windbreak had stood, the stumps still raw in the failing light, and then along the front fence to the mailbox, where she stood and looked west down the road until the light went out of the sky. She told herself the walk was for the chickens, to see them safe to the coop, and for the well, to check the rope, and for the fence, to see what the wind had taken. It was not for those things. It was for the mailbox, and the mailbox knew it.

Sammy watched her do it. He did not say anything. He had the old man's watchfulness that had come on him that winter, the watchfulness of a child who has been left by two people and has decided not to be surprised by the third. He did his chores, he carried his .22, the one he was not allowed to have, and he asked fewer questions than he had in the fall, which was a thing that cost June more than the questions had.

"Dad coming back when the mail comes?" he asked one evening at the table, and she could not tell if it was a question or a weather report.

"The mail comes every day," June said. "It is the letters that decide themselves."

She wrote letters she did not send. It was a ledger of another kind, a stack of papers in the tin box on the kitchen shelf, and she wrote to Cora and to Walter in it, to Cora the news of the farm and the seed and the windbreak's survivors in their wet sacking in the cellar, to Walter the news of the grave and the land and the rain that had not come. She wrote them in her plain-church prose, short lines, weather and seed and the arithmetic of survival, and she did not send them because the county post office was a window with eyes and the letters from a farm whose daughter had gone west into a strike would be read before they were posted. She knew this the way she knew the frost dates. She had been a farmer's wife for twenty-three years, and she had learned that a woman who cannot write what she means learns to mean what she does not write.

The seedlings came up in the canning jars on the windowsill.

She had started them the week after the axe came, the unbroken ones she had saved from the windbreak's ruin, the cottonwood and the willow and the Russian olive, a dozen small slips in a dozen jars of water, set on the sill above the sink where the light came. She had not told herself what she was doing. A woman who has had her windbreak cut down in the night does not announce the replanting of it. But the slips had rooted, small pale roots threading down into the water, and she checked them each morning the way she had checked the mailbox, a counting, a vigil.

She kept the seed ledger on the table at night. She had read it so many times that winter that the entries had become a litany, the seed bought and saved and planted and lost, the weather column, the years, 1919 to 1935, a whole life in a composition book. She read the entries for the spring of 1934, the ones she had written around Ezra's fever, the weather column running down the margin like a river in flood, and she closed the book and set it on the shelf and did not open it again that night.

The ledger was the third root, the one that did not go to the mailbox. The mailbox went west, down the road, to the two children who were out on the road in the two directions a mother can lose a child. The ledger went down, into the ground, into the years, into the columns of weather and seed that were the only way she had of making the days hold still. On the nights when the vigil was the whole of her she opened the ledger and she read it the way she read the sky, and the reading was not the reading of the past. It was the reading of the pattern, the way the seed corn had come through the drought years because it was the seed of seed that had come through before, the way the weather column ran in cycles, dry and wet and dry, the way the lives column ran in the same cycles, born and planted and died and planted, and she read the pattern the way the county men read the almanac, looking for the line where the drought broke, and the line was not there yet, but the pattern said it would come, the way the pattern had always come, and the vigil was the keeping of the pattern, the way the ledger was the keeping of it, and the mailbox was the keeping of it, and the three of them held her, the ledger and the mailbox and the walk at dusk, the three roots of a woman who had been left by the weather and would not be left by the land.

The neighbor women came on the first of February with the cold. Mrs. Halloran did not come. June had not spoken to Mrs. Halloran since the night of the axe, since the words between women in the yard, since the betrayal that had nearly broken her. But the other women came, Mrs. Petty with a jar of canned beans, old Mrs. Jansen with a loaf, and they sat at her table and they did not speak of the windbreak or the Hallorans or the letters that did not come. They spoke of the weather and the seed and the men who were gone, and they spoke of a girl named Cora the way you speak of the weather, because there was nothing to be done about either.

The vigil had a shape, and the shape held her. In the morning the jars on the sill, their water clouded with the day's dust, the root ends checked. In the afternoon the chores, the chickens, the well, the kitchen, the dust wiped from the window glass in its hopper-line. At dusk the walk, down the fence to the corner, across the back where the stumps were, along the front to the mailbox. In the night the letters she did not send, written at the table with the lamp turned low, the same lamp her mother had written by, the same table. And over all of it the counting, the counting of days and jars and roots and miles, the arithmetic of waiting, which is the hardest arithmetic there is, because the answer never comes out right.

On the fourth of February she stood at the mailbox longer than she had ever stood, and the light went out of the sky and the cold came in and she did not feel it, and when she turned to go back up the lane she found Sammy standing behind her in the dark with his coat on over his nightshirt, and he had not come to fetch her. He had come to stand with her. They stood a long time without speaking, and then he said, "It's cold," and she said, "It is," and they walked back to the house together, and neither of them had said the name, and the name had been said.

The seventh of February. A penny postcard. It was not from California. It was from Amarillo, from a man named Dutch, the truck boss, and it was four lines long, and it said the harvest had scattered and the camp was not the place it had been and Cora had gone north toward the valley with a girl named Rosa and a man from the committees, and that he had not seen her since before Christmas and he did not know more, but he knew a mother would want to know that much.

June read it at the mailbox in the failing light, and she read it again at the table by the lamp, and she folded it and put it in the tin box with the letters she did not send. She did not cry. She had not cried since the night of the axe, and she had learned that a woman who has a ledger and a mailbox and a child and a farm does not have room for crying, which is not the same as having no reason for it.

Sammy found her at the mailbox the next dusk. He came up the road with his .22 over his shoulder, the way he came home from his wandering, and he stood beside her and looked west down the road with her, and he did not ask what she was looking for, because he knew, and after a while he said, "When the mail comes, I'll run it up from the road myself. You won't have to come down here."

It was the first thing he had offered her since the fall, and it was the thing she needed, and she put her hand on his shoulder and they stood together at the mailbox in the dusk, mother and son, the wind coming down the road from the west, the stumps raw behind them, the jars rooting on the sill behind the kitchen glass, and the vigil went on.

That night she opened the ledger to a blank page and wrote, in her small plain hand, the entry for the day. The weather column said: dry. The seed column said: the slips root. The lives column said: two letters overdue. The mailbox said nothing, and would say nothing, and she closed the book and set it on the shelf between the canning jars, and the wind went around the house all night, and the dust came in under the door like a dog that had always lived there, and she did not sleep, and she did not stop counting.

There were nights, in that February, when the shape of the vigil was all that held her. Nights when she sat at the table with the lamp low and the tin box open and the letters she could not send spread out like seed on a winnowing sheet, and she read them over, and the reading was a kind of prayer, the only kind she had left, because the church had been dust and the preacher had been dust and the congregation had been the rows. She read the letters to Walter, the ones that said the land and the grave and the rain that had not come, and she read the letters to Cora, the ones that said the windbreak and the seed and the schoolhouse, and she did not send any of them, and in the reading she was both places at once, the way a root is both the seed and the tree.

On the night of the twelfth she wrote the letter she could not send and could not stop writing, and the letter said the thing she had not said to anyone since the spring of 1934, and it was addressed to Walter, and it said: I am afraid. I have been afraid since Ezra, and I hid the fear in the land, and the land has answered me with dust, and now the children are gone from me in the two directions a mother can lose a child, and I stand at the mailbox every dusk and I count the days, and I am learning that a woman can hold a ledger and a farm and a grave and a house and still be holding nothing, if the people are not in the house. She folded it and put it in the tin and did not send it, and she blew out the lamp and stood at the window in the dark, and the jars stood on the sill in the dark, their water silver with the cold light of the stars, and the roots went down into the water, and she watched them a long time, and the counting went on, and the dust came in under the door like a dog that had always lived there, and she waited, the way the roots waited, in the dark, in the water, holding.

Chapter 27: The Seed Swap

The farm women's swap had been held every February since before the dust came, and the dust had not stopped it, because the dust could not stop a thing that was not about the harvest. It was about the seed, and the seed was about the spring, and the spring was a thing the women of Cimarron County would not let the dust take.

It was held at the Halloran house that year. The houses went in rotation, and the rotation was older than any of the women living, and it did not stop for an axe or a betrayal, because the rotation was not about the Hallorans. It was about the holding of the ground, and the holding of the ground required the women to sit in one another's kitchens and trade what could be traded.

June almost did not go. She stood at the table in her own kitchen in the morning, the jars rooting on the sill, the tin box with the letters she did not send, and she told herself the walk was too far and the wind was up and the chickens needed her, and she told herself these things the way a woman tells herself a thing she does not believe, and then she took down her seed basket and she put in what she had to trade and she went.

The Halloran house sat at the bend of the county road, and the woodpile beside it was gone, and the yard had the stripped look of a place that has been cut bare for the winter, and June saw it and made herself look at it, the way you look at a wound. She had known the Hallorans for twenty years. She had stood beside Mrs. Halloran at the grave of her first child and at the grave of Ezra, and the women of the county had a law about such standing, and the law was older than the windbreak and it had not been broken by the axe, though the axe had come close.

Mrs. Halloran met her at the door. The woman was smaller than June remembered, or June was angrier, and they stood in the doorway a long moment, and the wind came down the road, and neither of them said the thing that was between them, and then Mrs. Halloran stood aside and June went in.

The kitchen was full. The women of the county were there, Mrs. Petty and old Mrs. Jansen and the younger women, the wives of the men who had gone west with the harvest and not come back, the wives of the men who had stayed and were breaking their backs on a dead land. They sat in the chairs and on the bench and on the edge of the wood stove, and the table was spread with what they had brought, seed packets and cuttings and jars and twists of paper with kernels in them, and the air had the particular smell of a seed swap, which is the smell of the spring held in a fist.

They did not speak of the men who were gone. It was a law of the swap. A woman could ask about another woman's garden, but she could not ask about her husband, because the husbands were a ledger that each woman kept alone, and the swap was for the seed.

The swap went around the room the way it had gone around the room for twenty years. Mrs. Petty traded a packet of radish seed for a cutting of old Mrs. Jansen's thyme. The younger women, the wives of the men who had gone west and not come back, traded what they had, seed saved from gardens that had not come up, and took what they could, seed for gardens that might not come up either, and the taking and the giving had the shape of a thing that did not depend on the harvest. June sat at the edge of the table with her basket in her lap and she watched the women of the county trade the spring back and forth across the oilcloth, and she understood that the swap was a ledger of another kind, a ledger that was kept in seed instead of numbers, and that the seed was the only currency the women had that the dust could not take, because the seed was the thing that remembered, and the women were the keepers of the remembering. The men kept the land, when there was land to keep. The women kept the seed, and the seed was the land's whole memory, the years of it, the dry years and the wet years and the years in between, folded into a kernel no bigger than a fingernail, and the seed went around the table in twists of paper, and the remembering went with it.

Mrs. Halloran put a jar on the table. June saw it from across the room, a canning jar with a cutting in it, water going green at the roots, and she knew before she was told what it was, because every woman in the county knew the story of the lilac. It had come out of Kansas with Mrs. Halloran's grandmother in a covered wagon, a slip no bigger than a finger, and it had grown into a bush at the corner of the Halloran house that put out flowers every spring that could be smelled from the road, and the women of three counties had begged cuttings of it, and Mrs. Halloran's grandmother had given one to her wedding, and that was the last one she had given to anyone.

"The lilac," Mrs. Petty said, and the room went still, because the lilac was not the kind of thing that went into the swap. The lilac was an heirloom, and the heirlooms did not trade. They were passed down, from the hand of one keeper to the hand of the next, and the hand of the next was chosen.

Mrs. Halloran did not look at June. She looked at the jar on the table, and she said, to the room, to the jar, "My grandmother said the lilac will root anywhere it is watered. She said it rooted in the wagon, and she did not know a ground it could not hold in. I have had my cutting from it, and I am past the age of taking more, and there is a woman in this county whose windbreak the frost took, and I thought she might have use of a cutting that roots in anything."

The frost, she said. Not the axe. And the women of the county, who knew the whole of it, the night and the woodpile and the stumps and the words between women in the yard, were still, and every one of them looked at June.

June stood at the table. She had come prepared to sit in the same room with the woman and trade seed for seed and not speak of the windbreak, and the woman had not spoken of it either, and yet there it was, the lilac in the jar, the heirloom, the thing that could not be bought or traded or taken, being offered across the table the way you offer a thing you have no words for.

"I have nothing to trade for the lilac," June said, and her voice came out steady, the way it came out steady when she read the weather column aloud. "The lilac is not a trade."

"It is not a trade," Mrs. Halloran said. "It is a cutting. My grandmother said a cutting is not taken. It is given, and the giving is the whole of it, and the woman who gives does not count the cost, and the woman who takes does not owe, because the debt is not between the two women. The debt is to the bush, and the bush does not keep a ledger."

The room held its breath. June looked at the jar, and she looked at the woman across the table, the woman she had helped at the grave of her first child, the woman whose family's woodpile had been gone in October, the woman whose husband had cut June's windbreak down in the night, the woman June had caught loading the last greenwood in the wagon, the woman who had lied about it, and she understood that the frost the woman spoke of was not the weather's frost.

She reached across the table and took the jar. The water was cool in her hands, the roots pale threads of green, and she held it the way you hold a thing you mean to keep.

"The last of my saved corn," June said. "I have it in my basket. It is the seed corn I have kept through every year of the dust, and it is the corn that will feed us if the spring answers, and I have not traded it with anyone, because a woman keeps the seed she means to plant." She set the jar down gently on the table and she said, "I will not trade it for the lilac. I will keep them both. And I will plant the lilac where the windbreak stood, at the corner of the house, where it can be smelled from the road, the way the grandmother's could."

Mrs. Halloran looked at her for a long moment, and something in her face gave way, the careful thing, and she nodded once, the way a woman nods at a fence line she has counted and found sound, and the room let its breath go, and the swap went on.

June came home in the failing light with the jar of lilac water in her basket and the seed corn still in its twist of paper, and she set the jar on the windowsill beside the rooting slips, the cottonwood and the willow and the Russian olive and now the lilac, the windbreak and the heirloom side by side, and she stood at the window and looked out at the stumps and the empty road, and she said nothing, and the wind came down the road from the west, and the mail had not come, and she went to the table and opened the ledger and wrote the entry for the day.

The weather column said: cold, clear, the wind from the west. The seed column said: the corn kept, the slips root, a lilac given that was not traded. The lives column said: a woman offered what she could not be asked for, and I took it, and the debt is to the bush, and the bush does not keep a ledger.

She closed the book and set it on the shelf between the canning jars, and she went to the window and looked at the lilac in its jar, and the roots threaded down into the water like a slow green arithmetic, and she thought, it will root anywhere it is watered. And she did not know yet that the water was coming, that the rain that would wash the dust away was months off and gathering, and that the lilac and the slips and the saved corn would all go down to the cellar in the flood and come up out of it, but she would learn it, and the bush would keep its ledger.

The swap went with her down the road that dusk, the talk of it, the way the county news travels.
