The lilac had been given, and the giving was the whole of it, and the women of the county read the giving the way they read the weather, and they understood that the frost the woman had spoken of had thawed, and that the two women who had stood at a grave together and then stood apart in a yard would be seen together at the fence line before the spring was out. She would not have said it herself, would not have named it, would not have let the word forgiveness pass her lips, because forgiveness was a word for churches and the women of the county did not use it, but she knew the shape of it the way she knew the shape of a jar on a windowsill, and the shape was this: an old woman had carried an heirloom a quarter mile in a jar of water and set it down on the table in front of a woman she had wronged, and the woman she had wronged had taken it. The giving and the taking were the whole of it. The debt was to the bush, and the bush did not keep a ledger, and the lilac would root anywhere it was watered, and the water, the true water, the water that had been months off and gathering, was coming, and the bush would hold it, and the woman would hold the bush, and the ledger would hold them both, the way a ledger holds the weather and the seed and the lives, all the columns, all the years, all the small green arithmetics of a county that would not let the dust take the spring.

Chapter 28: Cora's Second Pay

The packing shed stood at the edge of the valley where the citrus groves ran out into the flats, and the work there was different from the fields. The fields paid by the pound and the weather. The shed paid by the crate and the hour, and it was the first steady money Cora had held in her hands since the strike broke.

The strike had broken the way it had to break. She understood that now, standing at the long table in the shed with the oranges going past her hands, the way you understand a fever after it has broken. It had held through December, and the papers had written about it, and the growers had made an offer, and the committee had counted it the way Miguel had taught them to count, line by line, and the counting had not come out even, and in the last week of January the scab crews had come in force and the camp had been evicted at dawn and the tents had come down and the blacklist had gone up at the labor office with the photographs on it, and the strike was over.

She did not say the word busted. It was a word the men used, and the men had gone their ways, the way men go when a thing is over, Padilla to the south, Vargas to the coast, the men from the trucks back to the trucks, and the camp had scattered down the road like dust before a wind. Rosa had gone with her aunt to the north. Miguel had said he would go to the Imperial Valley with the pea harvest, and he had said she could come or stay, and she had said she would think, and she had come here, to the shed, because the shed was work and the work was money and the money was a root going home.

The shed boss was a man named Cutter, and he did not know her name and did not want to. She was a pair of hands, and a pair of hands that had been through a strike was a pair of hands that carried trouble, and he watched her the first week the way you watch a dog you are not sure of. But she packed crates the way she had picked rows, fast and clean, and by the end of the week he had stopped watching her and started counting her crates, which was the only trust the shed offered.

The pay was less than the fields. It was the first thing she counted, standing at the window with the envelope in her hand, the arithmetic her mother had taught her and Miguel had sharpened. The fields had paid by the pound and the weather, and a good day had been a dollar, and a bad day had been nothing. The shed paid a dollar and a half a day and it paid it every day, and the arithmetic came out to more than the fields, and it came out to less than the strike had asked for, and she held the two numbers together and understood that she had traded the weather for the cage, and the cage was a place to heal.

She worked two weeks at the shed. Her ribs ached in the mornings, the tape off now, the bone mended crooked or straight she could not tell, and her hands, which had picked and carried and held, went soft at the shed's work, and the softness was a thing she did not trust. The cut above her eye had healed to a pale line, the way the scar on Miguel's jaw was a pale line, and she caught herself touching it in the mornings, the way you touch a mark to remind yourself it is there.

The letters she wrote home. She wrote them at night in the boarding room she shared with three other women, at the table by the lamp, with the pencil Miguel had given her, and the letters were the hardest thing she had written since the letter from the hospital tent that she had not written. She wrote around the bruises the way the strike had taught her to write around the truth, which was the way you write when the county post office is a window with eyes. She wrote about the shed and the crates and the work, and she wrote that she was fine, and the word fine was a door she closed carefully behind it, and she signed the letter the way she had signed them since the strike, not your Cora, but the name she wore in the west, the name that did not have a farm attached to it.

The boarding room was a shed of another kind, a long room over the feed store where the shed women slept in bunks, and the walls were the same walls and the window was the same window and the lamp was the same lamp, and the women around her were the same ledger, the ones who had come through and the ones who would go on, and they talked at night the way the camp women talked, in the low spaces, about the weather and the work and the men who were gone, and Cora listened and did not talk, and she wrote her letters at the table by the lamp with the pencil Miguel had given her, and the letters went into the mail with the name that did not have a farm attached to it, and the farm waited, and the mailbox waited, and the letters crossed the miles the way roots cross the dark, blind, holding.

She sent three dollars home.

It was the second pay, and she counted it out on the boarding-room table by the lamp, the quarters and the dimes and the silver dollar that made the difference, and she folded the money order the way her mother had taught her to fold a letter, and she wrote the amount in the column on the back of the envelope the way her mother wrote in the ledger, and she sealed it and held it in both hands for a moment, the way you hold a thing you are about to give away.

The money order was cashed at the post office window. It was a different post office from the one in the valley where she had sent the five dollars home in November, but the window was the same window, the grate, the teller's hands, the fee taken off the top, the smile that was not a smile. She stood at the window with the money order in her hand and she watched the teller count it, and she understood, with a clarity that sat in her like a cold coin, that the money she was sending home was a root, and the root was wounded, and the wound did not show in the mail.

She mailed it and she walked back to the shed through the groves, and the citrus was coming in, the oranges heavy on the branches, and the air had the sharp clean smell of the groves after the dust of the fields, and she walked with the money order gone from her hand and the receipt in her pocket and the pencil in her shoe, and she did not know that the letters she had written around the bruises had crossed in the mail with a letter she had not written, that her mother was walking a fence line at dusk in another state, waiting at a mailbox that had gone silent, counting jars on a windowsill.

She knew the root had gone back. That was the whole of it. The root, wounded, had gone back. The money was not much, three dollars out of a shed that paid a dollar and a half a day, and the shed was less than the fields and the fields were less than the strike had asked for, and the strike had asked for a dollar and a half a hundred and a fair scale, and she had sent three dollars home with a letter that lied about the food, and she understood that the arithmetic of the west did not come out even, and that she was sending what she could, which was the only arithmetic there was.

That night she wrote the letter that told the truth. It was the letter she could not send, the way her mother kept the letters she could not send in a tin box on a shelf, and she wrote it in her mother's cadence, the plain-church lines, the weather and the work, and she wrote the truth in it, the strike and the beating and the hospital tent and the man who had taught her to count and the pencil he had given her, and she wrote the line she could not send: I am fine, and the fine is a place I have made, and I do not know if I can come home yet, but I am sending what I can, and the root is going back.

She folded it and put it in the tin with her things, and she lay on the cot in the boarding room with the sound of the other women breathing, and she held the receipt from the post office in her hand, and she thought about her mother standing at a mailbox in the dusk of another state, and she thought about the seed packet in her coat that she had never planted, and she understood that the seed was the root, and the root was the money order, and the money order was the letter, and the letter was the only way she had of being two places at once, a girl at a packing shed in the west and a daughter on a farm in the east, and the root went back, wounded, and the root held.

The root held because it had no choice, the way a root has no choice. It was the only thing she had that went both ways, that could be in the shed and at the farm in the same day, that could be three dollars and a lie about the food and still be true, and she had learned, in the camps and on the road and in the strike, that a woman is not a thing that can be two places at once, and that the only way to be two places at once is to send a piece of yourself out ahead, the way a root sends a runner, and the piece she sent was the money order, and the runner was the letter, and the root was the name she signed, the name that did not have a farm attached to it, and she would sign the other name, the farm name, when the runner had reached the ground and the ground had answered, and until then the root went back, wounded, and the root held, and the holding was the whole of the west, the whole of the strike, the whole of the year, and she held it, and she slept.

The shed took its own arithmetic as the weeks went on. She learned the packs, the way the oranges were graded and wrapped and counted into the crates, and she learned the foreman's rules and the foreman's hours, and she learned that the women of the shed, the steady ones, the ones who had been there for years and would be there for years, had a ledger of their own, the ledger of the women who had come through and gone, the girls from the fields who lasted a week and the girls who stayed, and the ledger was kept the way her mother's ledger was kept, in the spaces between the talk, in the way a woman held her back at the long table and the way she counted her crates and the way she looked at the window when the pay came around. Cora sat in the middle of that ledger and she read it the way she had learned to read the strike, and she understood that the shed was not the fields and the fields were not the strike and the strike was not over, not ever, that it had simply changed its shape, the way a root changes shape underground, and that the shape it had taken here was the cage, and the cage paid steady, and the steady pay went home, and the root went home, wounded, and the wound was the cost of the root, and the root was the cost of the home, and the arithmetic did not come out even and did not have to, because the sending was the whole of it, and the sending was hers.

Chapter 29: The Growers' Court

The injunction hearings were held in the county courthouse, and the courthouse was the first building Cora had been inside in nine months that had not been a tent or a shed or a truck. It had walls of stone and a floor of worn marble and a ceiling with a painted border of clouds, and the clouds had been painted by a man who had never seen the valley's real sky, which was the color of a dirty nickel most days and did not have clouds in it.

She had come because Miguel had come. He was the last of the committee still in the county, the last one not on the blacklist's photographed pages or already riding the rails south, and he had said, at the camp's last meeting in the rain, that the growers had filed papers, that the judge would hear the injunction against the workers' committee, and that a man accused should be seen by the court it was accusing him in. She had come down from the shed on her one day off, and she had worn her good dress, the one she had worn at the first camp and had not worn since, and she had walked the two miles in the early cold, and she sat in the back of the courtroom on a hard bench, and she watched the growers' arithmetic work.

The courtroom was full of the growers' side. That was the first thing she counted, the way she had learned to count. The benches on the right held the men in suits, the growers and their foremen and their lawyers, and among them she saw Mr. Gillis, sitting with his hands folded on the handle of a walking stick, and she saw Earl the foreman, and she saw the man who was not a sheriff, the one who had carried the paper into the tent on the night of the raid. The benches on the left held the workers, and there were fewer of them, and they sat close together, and their clothes were the clothes of the fields, and the bailiff had looked at them when they came in, the way you look at something that might need watching.

The judge was an old man with a face like a ledger page, and he read the papers before him the way a farmer reads a sky, and the growers' lawyer stood and made his argument, and it was an argument she had heard before, in different words, in the fields and at the scale and in the dark of the raid. The workers' committee was an illegal combination, the lawyer said. It had interfered with the picking of the cotton. It had held the crop hostage to an impossible wage. It had, through its agents and its printed matter, threatened the peace of the county, and it had a file to prove it.

The file. She saw it come out of the lawyer's case, a folder, manila, fat with papers, and the lawyer opened it on the table in front of the judge, and he began to read names into the record, and each name was a man she knew, Padilla, Vargas, Reyes, the men from the meetings, and each name was followed by a description, and she understood that the file was the growers' ledger, the dark twin of her mother's book, the book in which the workers were counted the way a crop is counted, and the crop had gone bad.

Then the lawyer said the name Miguel Otero, and he held up a photograph, and the photograph passed from the lawyer to the bailiff to the judge, and Cora looked at it across the room and saw his face, younger, the scar already there, the eyes already watching, and she understood what it meant to be in the file. It meant you were no longer a man. You were a number, and a number could be moved.

Miguel took the stand. He had not been called; he had asked to be, and the judge had allowed it, and he walked up the aisle between the benches the way he walked through a camp, unhurried, precise, and he sat in the chair and he looked at the judge and he answered the questions the growers' lawyer put to him, and Cora heard, for the first time, the full sound of his voice in a place that was not a tent.

Are you a member of the workers' committee? Yes. Did you organize the walkout of December first? I helped the workers organize it. What is the committee's purpose? A fair scale, a fair wage, water in the fields, a store that does not take the wage back. The lawyer's questions came at him like a foreman's voice, and Miguel's answers came back like a row being counted, even, exact, and the judge listened, and the growers' benches rustled, and Cora watched the lawyer begin to lose the thread of his own arithmetic.

The lawyer held up the photograph. "This photograph," he said, "was taken at the Pixley strike of October, nineteen thirty-three. It was taken at a scene of riot. You were present at the scene of a riot, were you not, Mr. Otero?"

"I was present at the Pixley strike," Miguel said. "I was picking cotton. I was carrying water to the line, the way a man carries water when the line is his people. I was beaten there." He touched the scar on his jaw, and his voice did not change. "The photograph in your file was taken after the beating. It is not a picture of a riot. It is a picture of a man who has been through one, and the county that beat him has kept it to prove he is the kind of man who gets beaten."

The courtroom went very still. The judge looked at the photograph, and then he looked at the scar, and Cora saw something pass across the old ledger of his face, a line she could not read. The lawyer pressed on, the questions coming faster now, and Miguel answered them the way he had answered the first ones, even, exact, and the lawyer began to repeat himself, and the judge raised a hand.

"Mr. Otero," the judge said, "what is your profession?"

"I was a schoolteacher, Your Honor," Miguel said. "In Las Vegas, New Mexico. I taught arithmetic and reading and the names of the states, and I was dismissed for teaching the parents how to count what the county owed them. I have been a cotton picker since."

"A schoolteacher," the judge said. He looked at the file, and he looked at the photograph, and he looked at the man in the chair, and he said, "The court notes the file. The court further notes that a file is a thing that can be written by one hand, and that the workers' committee has not been shown to have incited violence, and that the record of the strike contains no evidence of riot on the part of the workers." He set the photograph down. "The injunction is denied as to the charge of violence. The workers' committee may assemble, and may publish, and may carry water to a line, so long as it does so peaceably. The court is adjourned."

The growers' lawyer began to gather the file, and then he stopped, and Cora saw him look at the judge and make to speak, and the judge looked at him over the tops of his glasses and said, "The record will show that the court finds the blacklist in evidence to be what it is. A county that keeps a list of its citizens in a drawer does not weigh well on any scale, and this court will not be the scale that gives it weight. The file is returned to counsel." And the lawyer closed the folder, and Cora watched the photograph go back into it, and she understood that the file was not closed, that a file never closes, that it is a ledger that is written in by more than one hand and read by more than one eye, and that the growers' hand was not the only hand that could write in it now.

The growers' benches stood. Mr. Gillis did not stand. He sat with his hands folded on his stick and his eyes on Miguel, and the look on his face was the look of a man who has lost a row and intends to count it again on his own paper, and the lawyer went down the aisle ahead of him carrying the file, and the file went out of the courtroom the way a thing goes out of a room when it has been weighed and set aside, and Cora watched it go.

She came out of the courthouse into the February light, and Miguel was standing at the bottom of the steps, and he looked up at the building behind her, the stone and the worn marble and the painted clouds, and he said, "They will not come at us with the law again. The law is a scale, and today it weighed for us, and the growers know it, and they will not use a scale that has weighed against them."

"What will they use?"

"The file," he said. "They will use the file. But the file has a page with my name on it, and it is not the last page, and I intend to keep writing in it." He looked at her, and the careful thing came down, and he said, "I am going to the Imperial Valley with the pea harvest, and the committee will go with me, the men and the women and the lessons we have learned here. It is not the end of the reckoning, Cora. It is the first line of it. You can come, and you can stay, and either way the file will have our names in it, and the names will keep."

She stood at the bottom of the courthouse steps with the February wind coming down the street, and she looked at the building with its painted clouds and she thought about the mural she had stared at through the whole of the hearings, the WPA mural of pioneers on the far wall, men and women in a covered wagon crossing a plain, their faces turned west, and she had stared at it because it had seemed to her a picture of the thing she had done, the crossing, the going west, and now she understood that the pioneers in the mural were not going west to find a new world. They were going west to find a place to plant, and the planting was the whole of it, and the planting could be done anywhere the ground would answer.

The mural had not been there in the years of the dust. It had been painted by a man sent out from the WPA, and it had gone up on the wall of the county courthouse the way the murals had gone up in courthouses all over the country, the pioneers and the harvest and the faces turned toward a horizon that was painted and not real, and she had stood in the back of that room during the whole of the hearings and she had watched the painted pioneers and the real judge and the real file and the real man on the stand, and she had understood that the courtroom was a scale, and the scale weighed what was put on it, and the growers had put their file on it and the judge had put the law on it, and for one day the law had weighed heavier, and the file had gone back into its case, and the photograph had gone back into the folder, and the folder had gone back into the lawyer's case, and the case would be carried out of the courthouse and down the street and into another room, where another hand would open it and another scale would weigh it, and the file would go on weighing, and the name would go on being in it, and the reckoning would go on being counted, line by line, by hands that could not be put in any file.

She did not know her answer yet. She only knew that the root went home, wounded, and the root held, and that somewhere in the east a woman was standing at a mailbox in the dusk, counting jars on a windowsill, and that the file had a page with her name being written into it, and that a man she had come to believe in was going south to the pea fields, and that the courthouse behind her had a mural of pioneers on its wall and a painted sky on its ceiling, and that neither of them was the real sky, which was the color of a dirty nickel, and which she had learned to read better than any painter.

Chapter 30: The Busted Strike

The strike broke in the last week of January, and it broke the way a dry thing breaks, all at once, with a sound that was mostly absence.

Cora was at the shed when she heard. A man came through the groves at noon with the word, one of the men from the Reyes camp, and he stood at the long table where she was packing crates and he said, "The camp is coming down. The growers brought the crews in force at dawn, and the tents are down, and the blacklist is up at the labor office, and the men are being loaded on the trucks to the county line." He said it flat, the way you say a thing you have seen too many times to make a shape for it. "Otero says the committee is meeting tonight at the wash. He says if you want to see it through, come."

She set down the orange she was holding, and the foreman's eyes moved to her, and she did not look at him. She wiped her hands on her apron and she walked out of the shed, and the crate she had been packing was a quarter full, and she did not go back for it.

The wash was a dry creek bed behind the orchard where the committee had held its meetings when the tent could not be used, a sandy bottom with a steep bank, out of sight of the road. She came down the bank in the early dark and the committee was there, and it was not the committee she had known. Padilla was gone south. Vargas was gone to the coast. The men around the small fire were the ones who had not scattered yet, the ones who had nowhere to go but the road, and Miguel sat at the center of them with the rain beginning to fall, the thin cold rain of the last week of January, and his face was the face of a man who has counted a row to the end and found it short.

"The growers have won this round," he said. "That is the truth of it, and I will not count a short column and call it even. They brought the crews in force, and the crews were men out of work, and out-of-work men cannot refuse a day's pay no matter what the day is worth. They have the land, and the money, and the law, and the file, and this time they used all of them together." He looked around the circle, the rain beading on his shoulders, the scar pale in the firelight. "But a strike is not a crop, and it does not rot in one season. It is a seed, and this seed is in the ground."

The rain came down. The men around the fire said nothing, and the silence was the sound of the strike breaking, the last sound of it, the absence where the singing had been. The camp up the road was coming down in the dark, the tents flat on the ground, the belongings being sorted into what could be carried and what could be burned, and the trucks were at the county road taking the men south, and the blacklist was up at the labor office with the photographs on it, and Cora sat in the rain at the edge of the fire and she understood that the strike was over, that the word the grower had given her twisted in the dark of the raid was going to sit in her chest with its other word, the name they had been given, and that both of them were going to have to live there now.

Miguel turned to her. The fire was low and the rain was coming harder, and the men had begun to drift, the way the camp had drifted, in twos and threes, into the dark, the way water finds its level. He said, "I am going to the Imperial Valley with the pea harvest. The committee will be there, the ones who are left, and there will be another scale and another thumb and another strike, and I intend to be there for it." He looked at her a long moment, and the rain ran down his face and he did not wipe it. "You can come. There is a place for you on the truck, and a place in the committee, and I will teach you the pea arithmetic and you will teach me the cotton arithmetic, and we will be a thing together that neither of us is alone."

She sat in the rain and she looked at the fire and she did not answer, and the men were gone now, the wash empty except for the two of them and the rain, and she heard herself say the thing she had been carrying since the courthouse steps, since the hospital tent, since the night of the raid, since the first lesson at the plank table when she had seen her mother's ledger with new eyes.

"I came west with a seed packet in my coat," she said, "and I have carried it through every camp and every county line and every strike, and I have never opened it, because opening it seemed like giving up on home. But a seed is a thing you plant in the ground because you believe the ground will answer, and I have learned, here, in this valley, with this rain and this fire and these men, that a seed will not answer a ground it was not made for." She looked up at him, and the rain was in her eyes and she did not wipe it. "This is not my ground, Miguel. You taught me that, in the hospital tent, the night you told me your mother's ledger and your sister's stillness. You told me to go home in the day, with my eyes open, and not to carry the dark out with me. I am going to go home. Not because I am afraid of the pea fields, and not because I am done with the reckoning, but because I have a ground that will answer, and I have kept it waiting long enough."

He looked at her a long moment, and the careful thing came down, and she saw the man underneath it, tired, precise, unillusioned, and she saw that he was not surprised, that he had known, that he had told her the truth in the hospital tent and the truth had taken a year to ripen and now it was ripe. He reached into his shirt and he brought out the photograph, the one from the file, the one the lawyer had held up in the courtroom, and he held it out to her and he said, "They will not miss it. They have the negative. I want you to have it, so that when the file is closed, and the file is always closed eventually, you will have a page of it that is yours."

She took the photograph. It was creased and damp with the rain, his face younger, the scar already there, the eyes already watching, and she held it the way you hold a thing that has cost something, and she put it in her coat beside the seed packet, the two things that had traveled with her, the seed and the face, and she understood that she would carry both of them home.

They stood in the rain a long moment, and then he put his hand on her shoulder the way a man puts his hand on a thing he is letting go, and he said, "Write to me when you are home. The letters will find me, the way they find all of us, eventually, in some camp, on some road, in some county that has not yet learned our names. Tell me the seed came up." And he turned and walked up the bank into the dark, and the rain came down, and the wash was empty, and the strike was over.

She sat by the fire until it went out, and then she walked up the bank and through the camp, and the camp was gone, the tents flat, the belongings sorted, the trucks gone south, the blacklist up at the labor office with the photographs on it, and the cotton stood white in the rows, unpicked, the last of it going to the men who had crossed the line, and the camp scattered down the road like dust before a wind, and she stood in the rain at the edge of the field and she looked west, where the trucks had gone, and east, where the road went home, and she did not know yet that the east was where the water was gathering, that the rain that was falling on the valley was the same weather that would flood the Cimarron in April, that her mother was standing at a mailbox in another state counting jars on a windowsill, and that the seed packet in her coat was about to be planted in a ground that had been waiting for it all along.

She went back to the shed in the morning, and she packed crates until the light failed, and at the end of the week she drew her pay and she counted it out on the boarding-room table by the lamp, and she wrote the letter to her mother, the one she could send, the one that said she was coming home, and she mailed it with the pencil Miguel had given her, and the receipt from the post office went into the tin with the other receipts, and she began the arithmetic of going home, which is the hardest arithmetic there is, because it is the sum of a whole life, and it does not come out even until the last line.

The letter she wrote said less than the one she had carried in her chest for a year, and it said more. It said she had finished the work at the shed. It said she was taking the Greyhound as far as Amarillo and then a truck north on the county mail road. It said she would be home by the middle of March, if the roads held. And it said, at the end, the thing she had not written in any letter since she left, the name she had not signed, the farm name, the name that had a ground attached to it. She wrote it small, at the bottom of the page, the way you write a thing you are not sure the mail will carry: Your Cora. And she mailed it, and she stood at the post office window a moment after she had mailed it, the way you stand at a grave a moment after you have planted it, and then she went back to the boarding room and she took out the seed packet from her coat and she held it in her hand, and she did not open it, and she put it back, and she began to fold her things, and the arithmetic went on.

The week of the going home had its own arithmetic, and she kept it the way she had kept the strike's arithmetic, in a column. The first column was the money, the pay from the shed and the money orders and what the road would cost, and she counted it out and it came out thin, and she counted it again, and the thinness was the whole of it, the thinness was the west, the thinness was what the road cost a girl who had come out with a jar of tomatoes and a folded seed packet and nothing else. The second column was the leaving, and the leaving was the harder column, because the leaving was not the money. The leaving was Rosa gone north and Lula gone south and the women of the shed who had kept their ledger and the man from the Reyes camp who had carried the word at noon, and the leaving was the camp that was not there anymore, the tents flat, the fires out, the wash empty, the singing gone down the road with the trucks. The third column was the carrying, and the carrying was the seed packet and the photograph and the pencil and the receipts and the scar above her eye that she touched in the mornings, and the carrying was the word from the grower's mouth and the name they had been given and the arithmetic she had learned, and the carrying was what she would bring home, the whole of it, the dark and the light of it, the way you carry a thing you have carried through the mud and the rain and the fire and the salt, and the carrying was the column that did not come out even and did not have to, because it was not money and it was not leaving and it was not any of the things you could count, it was the thing you were, and she was going home, and the root was going home, and the root held, and the ground was waiting, and the seed was in her coat, and the east was where the water was gathering, and she went east, and the rain followed her all the way to the state line, the same weather, the same water, the same reckoning, and the truck that carried her north on the county mail road carried the whole of it, the seed and the face and the pencil and the scar, a girl going home with a ground that would answer, and the land keeping its reckoning, and the counting going on, and the last line not written yet.

Chapter 31: Sammy's Sickness

JUNE — the farm — February 1936

Sammy coughed in the night and the cough had the old sound in it, the sound she had taught herself to forget. She lay still in the dark and counted. One. Two. She waited for the second cough, the one that came doubled and scraped, and when it came she was already out of bed with her feet on the cold boards.

His face was hot. She put the back of her hand to his forehead and the heat came off him like the wall of the oven in canning time. He did not wake. She stood over the bed with the dark moving around her and she did not think of the spring of 1934. She kept it off the way you keep a shutter closed against a wind that wants in.

In the kitchen she lit the lamp and pumped water into the kettle. Her hands moved the way they had moved before, two years before, in this same room, in this same hour of the night, and she made herself move different. She measured the water the same. She set the kettle on the stove and struck the match and watched the flame take.

The house was cold to the bone. February had come in lean, the coal nearly gone, the woodpile thin, and she burned what the field gave her, the cornstalks and the dry roots the wind had uncovered. The stove took them like tinder. She banked the fire and carried the kettle in and set it on the stand beside the bed and she sat in the chair and she watched him.

Sammy slept with his mouth open, his breath a thin whistle, the same way Ezra had slept when he was small and the dust was bad. The whistle had a seam in it. She had known that seam all winter and had told herself it was nothing, a boy's chest in a dry year, and now the seam had split wide open.

The morning came grey and the cough was worse. Sammy sat up in bed with his shoulders shaking and brought up phlegm that was not clear, that was the color of the water that stands in the road ruts after the first rain. She made him drink the tea, hot, with the honey she had been saving against just such a morning, and he drank it and coughed again and the tea came back up.

"Ma," he said. "It's just a cold."

"It's February," she said. "Everything's a cold in February."

But she watched him, and all that day she watched him, and when the fever climbed in the afternoon she stopped pretending. She brought the camphor from the shelf and rubbed it on his chest and it cut through the smell of the room and hung there sharp and clean, and under it she could still hear the rasp, the little gate in his chest swinging in a wind.

She found the .22 under his pillow while she turned the sheets. He had kept it, after the day she took the shotgun from under his mattress and emptied the shells into the dust; he had hidden this one better, small, cold, oiled, with a loop of twine on the stock. She stood with it in her hands and did not scold him. She set it on the top shelf of the pantry, because a boy who keeps a rifle under his pillow through the winter is not a boy who means to give it up, and that was not the argument to have in a room where he could not keep his eyes open.

She did not go to the Hallorans'. She did not go to the mailbox. She stayed in the room with the lamp turned low and she read to him from the almanac because it was the only book on the table, and he asked her to keep reading even when he could not keep his eyes open, and she read about the frost tables and the phases of the moon and the weather for March which the almanac promised would be fair.

The almanac lied. She knew the almanac lied. It had lied every year since 1933 and she read it anyway because his breathing had gone shallow and she needed a sound in the room that was hers.

The wind came up at dusk. She heard it first at the door, then in the eaves, then everywhere, the way a dog will push at every window before it settles. Dust rattled the glass. She had hung wet cloths over the window sills the way she always did and the water in them turned brown and she changed them and the water turned brown again and she stood at the window with her hand flat against the glass and felt the house lean.

She thought of Ezra. She had kept it off all day and now it came in through the room, through the sound of the wind, through the smell of the camphor, and she could not keep it off any longer because the boy in the bed had Ezra's hair, the same dark hair that lay against the pillow in the same way, and the same ribs showing through the flannel, and the same cough.

Spring 1934. She had not told it whole to anyone, not to Walter, not to Cora, not even to the ledger. She had kept the whole of it locked in the arithmetic she did with her hands, and now it came out in the room the way water comes out of a ground well, slow and total.

The fever had come on a Tuesday. Ezra had been at the north quarter, where she had been setting the windbreak, two years before the Hallorans' axe, and he had come in coughing and she had put him to bed and he had been fine that night, or she had told herself he was fine. On Wednesday the cough was deep, and there was the water to haul because the well had gone dry and the cistern was low, and the chickens to feed and the seed corn to turn in the jars so it would not sour, and no telephone at the farm then, the nearest one at the crossroads six miles, and a woman does not leave a sick child to walk six miles on the chance.

So she had waited. One day, then another, because the cough would break, because the weather would turn, because children coughed and children got well. On Friday she had hitched the wagon and driven for the doctor, and the dust had come up and the road had filled and she had walked the last mile in the drift with her skirts up, and the doctor had come back with her and had stood in this same room with the stethoscope and had looked at her, and the look had told her everything.

He had come too late. That was the fact that would not be moved, the stone in the ledger of that spring: he had come too late, and she had made him late, her waiting, her arithmetic that counted a day's hauling and a jar of corn and a man's fence posts worth more than her son's lungs.

Ezra had died on a Sunday. The rain had come that day, the first in months, the way it mocked, and they had buried him in the wet ground and she had planted the extra tree for him, the one nobody asked about, and she had sworn in her own heart, without words, that she would never again count a jar of corn higher than a child's breath.

And she had broken that oath since. She had let the land take her attention the way it takes everything, and she had sent Walter west and Cora west and kept this one boy, this last boy, and now he lay in the bed with the fever and the cough and the thin whistle, and the room was filling with the sound of the spring of 1934, and she could not shut it out.

She went to the bed and lifted Sammy in her arms. He was light, too light, the way Ezra had been light, and she carried him to the chair by the stove and held him, and his breath rattled against her chest, and she held him and rocked him the way she had not rocked a child in two years.

"Ma," Sammy said, half asleep. "Don't cry."

"I'm not crying," she said. "There's dust in the room."

But she was crying, and she held him, and the wind worked at the house all night, and in the morning the fever was a hundred and three and the boy's lips were cracked and the cough was worse, and this time she would not wait a day, not an hour.

She wrapped him in the quilt. He protested, weakly, like a kitten. She built up the fire, put her boots on, and took the wagon through the drifted sand to the blacktop, then along the blacktop to the crossroads where the telephone was, and she stood in the cold wind of the February morning and cranked the handle and asked for Dr. Whitfield, and the operator said he was out on a call to the west, and she left word that it was the Vogel boy and the fever was bad and the cough was deep, and she made the operator repeat the message back.

"Vogel boy," the operator said. "Fever. Cough."

"And tell him," June said, and her voice caught, and she stopped and started again. "Tell him it is the same as the other one. Tell him that. He will know what it means."

She drove home through the sand and sat with him and fed him tea and wet his forehead and counted his breaths, and she thought about the arithmetic she had done wrong, and she prayed the way she prayed, not with words, but with her hands: she changed the wet cloths, she turned the pillow, she smoothed his hair, she counted.

In the afternoon the wind shifted and the sand stopped and for an hour the light came down clean, and she saw Mrs. Halloran coming up the road, a dark shape against the white, walking slow with her coat pulled up over her mouth and a jar in her hands. June went to the door. Mrs. Halloran stood at the foot of the porch with the wind gone and the quiet strange around them, and she held out the jar.

"Chicken," Mrs. Halloran said. "For the boy. I heard he's sick."

June took the jar. It was warm. She looked at the woman whose husband had cut down her windbreak in the night, at the woman she had caught loading the last greenwood, and she could not find the old words, the words about the trees, because there was a boy in the bed and the boy was what mattered.

"Thank you," June said.

Mrs. Halloran nodded. She stood a moment with her hands empty, and then she said, "I put extra pepper in it. That's what my grandmother did for the lung fevers." And she turned and walked back up the road, and June stood in the doorway with the warm jar and watched her go, and she took it in and fed the broth to her son a spoon at a time.

The wind did not let up that night. It scoured the yard and buried the fence line in the north quarter and she watched it from the window and she said, aloud, to the wind, "Not this one. You can have the trees and the field and the barn. Not this one."

And the wind did not answer, and the boy coughed, and she waited, and waiting was the thing she had sworn she would never do again, and she did it anyway because the doctor was on the road somewhere to the west, and because a woman alone in February cannot carry a sick boy forty miles through the sand.

That night Sammy's fever spiked and he shook and she held him through it, and in the grey hour before dawn his breathing changed, went shallow and fast, the way water runs thin over a stone, and she laid her hand on his chest and felt the little gate swinging and she did the only arithmetic left to her.

She got the shotgun from under the mattress, where she had put it back after the day she emptied the shells, and she loaded it with the shells she had saved, and she put her coat on over her nightgown, and she stood on the porch in the wind with the gun in her hands and she fired once into the dark, into the dust, into the nothing.

The sound went out flat and the wind ate it. She stood with the gun smoking and the cold coming through her coat and she said, "That is for the doctor, if he can hear it. That is for anyone. Bring me help. Bring me help or bring me nothing, but do not make me wait again."

She went back in. She put the gun back under the mattress, unloaded, the way a woman puts a thing back in its drawer, and she sat with her son, and she counted his breaths, and the wind blew, and the dark held, and she did not take her eyes off him.

She watched the road through the window until the light came, then through the day, the sand hissing at the glass, Sammy coughing and sleeping and coughing, the fever holding, her vigil holding, and in the evening, when the light was going grey and the wind was finally dropping, she heard it.

An engine. A car, coming slow down the drifted road, grinding in the sand.

She went to the window. The headlights were two weak eyes in the dusk. The car stopped at the gate and a man got out, an old man, stooped, carrying a black bag, and he came up the path with the wind at his back, and she was at the door before he knocked, and she opened it and she looked at him and she could not speak.

"Mrs. Vogel," he said. "I came as fast as I could. The sand near took my car twice."

It was Dr. Whitfield. He stood in her doorway with the dust on his coat and the bag in his hand and the cold coming off him, and behind her, in the room, her son coughed, and she stood aside and let him in.

The wind had dropped. For the first time in three days, the house was still.

The kettle was on the stove. She went to it, and her hands were shaking, and she held them together over the steam and watched the old doctor go into the room where her son lay, and she heard the springs creak as he sat on the bed, and she heard his voice, low and even, asking the boy to open his mouth.

She stood in the kitchen with her hands over the steam and she did not pray with words. She prayed with the kettle, with the camphor, with the wet cloths, with the lamp, with the house, with the seed corn in the jars on the shelf, with every seed she had ever saved, and she heard the doctor say something in the room, and then his voice calling her, and she went in.

The lamp was on. Dr. Whitfield had Sammy's shirt open and his stethoscope against the small chest, and he was listening, his eyes closed, and she stood at the foot of the bed and watched his face and read nothing there, and then he opened his eyes and looked at her.

"His lungs are bad," he said. "The dust has been in them. But he's a strong boy, Mrs. Vogel. And you got to him in time."

She stood very still.

"You got to him in time," he said again. "That's the difference. You got to him in time."

Chapter 32: The Doctor

JUNE — the farm — February 1936

Dr. Whitfield did not take off his coat. He sat on the edge of the bed with the black bag open on his knee and he worked slow, the way old men work, saving their hands. He looked at the boy's tongue, at the color under the fingernails, at the whites of the eyes. He pressed his stethoscope to the small chest in four places and listened a long time at each one, his eyes closed, his head cocked like a man listening at a door.

June stood at the foot of the bed with her arms crossed and then uncrossed and then crossed again. The room was warm now, the fire up, the lamp burning, and the warmth felt like a lie against the cold she had carried for three days.

"Help me get him up," the doctor said. "Propped. I want his chest raised."

They lifted Sammy between them, the boy a bundle of quilt and bone, and they wedged the pillow and the folded blankets behind him. Sammy's eyes opened once and found June and closed again. His lips were cracked white at the corners.

"Heard your shot last night," the doctor said, working at the bag. "Three miles out, coming up the south road. I thought at first it was somebody killing stock, and then I thought, no, that's a signal. Somebody's got somebody sick. I was on my way to the Bell farm on the west end, but I turned around. A shot in the dark says more than a telephone call."

June said nothing. She had not known, standing on the porch in her nightgown with the gun in her hands, that the sound would carry. It had carried. It had carried through three miles of dust and dark and brought the old doctor up the road with his bag, and that was more than she had dared to ask of the wind.

"Car stuck twice," he said. "First time at the wash. Second time at the railroad crossing, where the sand's drifted over the ties. I walked the last stretch. Good thing I did — you can't hurry a car out of sand the way you can hurry a man out of a chair."

The doctor took out a glass bottle and a spoon. "Eucalyptus and honey. I've got nothing for the fever but time and this. He has to breathe this in. I want the kettle going day and night, and I want the steam in this room so thick you can't see the window."

"Where'd you come by eucalyptus?" she said. It was a real question, a farmer's question, a woman who measured worth in where a thing came from.

"Chico, California. I was out there in '29, at the clinics in the orchard camps. They grew eucalyptus by the mile to hold the wind down, and the old ones in the camps chewed the leaves for their chests, and I watched a boy come back from a lung fever on eucalyptus and steam and nothing else the drugstore had ever sold. I've kept a bottle on my shelf since."

June went to the kitchen and built the fire up and filled the kettle and set it where the steam would roll out into the room. She came back and stood with her hands at her sides.

"Sit down, Mrs. Vogel," the doctor said. "You've worn the floor out."

She sat. The chair was the one by the stove, and from it she could see the boy and the doctor both, and the window, and the grey evening going dark beyond it.

"Tell me about his chest," the doctor said. "How long the cough?"

"All winter. I told myself it was the dust."

"And the fever?"

"It came on two nights ago. Fast."

The doctor nodded. He put the stethoscope back in the bag and closed it and set his hands on his knees, and he looked at her across the room, and she had seen that look before, two years before, in this same room, and her stomach went cold with it.

"The lungs are full of it," he said. "The dust has sat in there and the dust has started to rot, and the rot has become a fever. I've seen this chest a hundred times since '33. More than a hundred. It is the same chest every time, and the children die of it, and the old people die of it, and the men and women in between die of it, and the ones who live are the ones whose mothers got to them early."

"Early," June said.

"Early. You got to him in time, and that is the whole of my medicine tonight, that and the steam and the honey and the prayers you don't say out loud."

She sat very still. The doctor's eyes were pale and tired and kind in the way of a man who has seen too much to be cruel.

"He's got a strong heart," he said. "I felt it through the rattling. A boy's heart will carry a boy's lungs a long way if it's not asked to carry them alone. That's what you're for. You're not to leave this room until I tell you he's out of it."

In the morning the neighbor women came. Mrs. Halloran first, with a basket, and behind her two others from the section, women June had not spoken to in weeks, women whose men had gone the way Walter had gone, leaving them alone with the stock and the wells and the sick. They did not come in. They stood on the porch in their coats and handed in what they had — bread, eggs, a jar of beef — and they asked after the boy in low voices, and Mrs. Halloran said, "He's a tough one. He takes after his mother," and they went back down the road the way they had come, and June stood with the basket and watched them go, the way she had watched Mrs. Halloran go the day before, and she thought about the seed swap, about the frost-nipped truce, about the women of the section holding the land up between them, and she took the basket in and set it on the table and went back to the room.

The night went on the way nights go in a sickroom, in pieces. The doctor dozed in the chair with his coat still on, waking at every change in the boy's breathing. June moved between the bed and the kitchen, the kettle, the cloths, the spoon. She wet his forehead, she held the spoon of honey and eucalyptus to his lips, she turned his head so the steam rolled across his face, and all the while she counted his breaths the way she counted seed, the way she counted the jars on the shelf, the way she counted everything, because counting was what she had, the arithmetic of staying alive.

Around midnight the fever came up again and the boy shook. The doctor was awake before she was, on his feet, his hand on the boy's chest.

"Help me hold him," he said. "Don't let him thrash. It takes his air."

She held his shoulders, and the doctor held his hands, and the shaking went through the bed like the shudder of a wagon over bad road, and she held him and talked to him in a low voice, not words, just sound, the way you talk to an animal in a storm, and the shaking eased and the boy lay still and his breath came shallow and fast and the two of them watched him, and the doctor said, "This is the test. He's got to push through this or the fever will have him."

The hours went grey. The wind came up again outside and then dropped, and the wind was nothing to them, the two of them in the lamplight with the boy between them. The kettle sang. The steam hung in the room so thick the far wall was a smear. The doctor wiped the boy's face with a cloth and felt his neck and nodded at nothing.

At one point, near the small hours, the doctor sat back in his chair and talked to her the way men talk to keep themselves awake. He told her about his father, who had been a doctor before him, who had ridden a horse between the same farms in the same county in another dry time, the drought of the nineties, who had buried two of his own children in that time and had kept going, because that was the only thing a man in his line of work could do.

"The dust changes names and the fever changes nothing," he said. "My father used to say the land will take a third of everything you love and call it weather. I've spent forty years trying to prove him wrong, and I've got half a case to show for it. But I keep the other half — the ones I got to in time. I keep them the way a farmer keeps seed. They're the argument for the spring."

Near four in the morning the boy's breathing changed. June heard it in her sleep, the seam in the whistle closing, the breath coming even, and she was out of the chair and at the bed before she was fully awake.

The doctor was already there. He had the boy's wrist in his hand and his eyes closed and his head bowed, listening, and then he opened his eyes and looked at the window where the first grey of the false dawn was coming up behind the dust.

"He's sweating," the doctor said. "The fever's breaking."

June put her hand on Sammy's forehead and it was wet, wet the way the ground is wet after rain, and the heat was going out of it, and the boy's breath came deep and even, and she stood with her hand on his head and she could not move, and the tears came and she did not stop them, and the doctor let her stand there and did not say anything, because there was nothing to say that would not be too small.

The dawn came up grey and the wind stayed down and the boy slept. June sat in the chair and did not sleep, watching him, the way she had watched him for three days, and the doctor sat across the room and drank the coffee she made him and watched her.

"Will you tell me something?" he said.

She looked up.

"The boy who's buried in the north corner. The one with the tree over him."

The room went still. She had not spoken of Ezra to anyone since the spring of 1934, not the whole of it, and here was this old man in her kitchen at dawn, saying it plain.

"That was dust pneumonia too," she said. "I waited too long. I sent for you too late."

"I remember," the doctor said. "I was too late. That's on me as much as you, and I've carried it. The roads and the weather and the distance between this farm and help — that's the killer, more than the dust. The dust is only what fills the lungs. The distance is what fills the graves."

He set the cup down.

"Children are dying of it all up and down this county," he said. "I've buried eight since Christmas. Eight. And every one of them, I got there after. Not because I was slow — because a doctor can't be in forty places at once, and a farm can't raise a telephone, and a woman can't carry a sick child through a storm on her back. That's the arithmetic of it, and it is a hard arithmetic, and it is not your fault."

She sat with her hands in her lap and looked at the floor.

"You kept this boy alive," he said. "You got him to me and you kept him alive on the way, and you've kept him alive tonight. That's what I mean when I say you got to him in time. It wasn't luck. It was you."

She did not answer. The room was filling with the grey light and the smell of steam and coffee and the slow even breathing of the boy in the bed, and she let the words sit in her, the way seed sits in the ground, and she did not know yet what they would grow into, but she did not refuse them.

The doctor packed his bag at full light. He stood in the doorway with his coat on and looked at the morning, at the sky the color of old pewter, at the flat white land.

"The weather will break," he said. "I've seen springs come back from worse. When it rains, you'll think you dreamed the dust. It's the nature of water to make you forget."

"Will you come back?" she said.

"I'll come back day after tomorrow, if the road holds. Keep the steam up and the honey in him and don't let him out of bed till I say. A boy whose lungs have been through this has to learn to breathe all over again, the way you learn to walk after a broken leg. Give him a week and he'll be asking for his rifle."

June followed him onto the porch. The wind was down and the air had a cold wet edge to it, and she stood in the doorway with her arms folded and watched him get his car started and turn it in the yard and take the road east, the way he had come, the dust smoking up behind the wheels and settling again, and she thought about the eight children he had buried since Christmas, and the distance, and the arithmetic, and the boy asleep in the bed behind her, breathing.

She went in. She built the fire. She made the kettle sing again and set the steam rolling into the room, and she sat in the chair by the stove and watched her son sleep, the even rise and fall of the quilt, the color coming back into his lips, and when she was sure, when she had watched the breath come even ten times and twenty and a hundred, she went to the shelf where the ledger lay and she took it down and opened it to a new page, to the page after February, and she wrote in her hand, small and even, the way she wrote everything:

"Feb 1936 — Sammy lived."

She closed the ledger and put it back on the shelf between the jars of seed corn, and she stood a moment with her hand flat on the cover, the way you touch a thing you have been afraid of losing, and then she went back to the chair by the stove, and she sat with her son, and she waited for the day to come the rest of the way.

Chapter 33: Cora's Return

CORA — the road home — March 1936

The Greyhound let her off in Amarillo in the dark. She stood on the pavement with her bag in her hand and watched the bus pull away and take the last of the west with it, the red tail light going small down the street, and she thought about the year she had just crossed, and the year felt like a road, and she was standing at the place where it turned back.

She had bought the ticket with the money she had saved at the packing shed, and she had stood at the window in the valley with the cash in her hand and the clerk stamping the date on it, and it had felt like a door closing and a door opening at once. Rosa had walked her to the depot, carrying her bag, and they had stood on the platform in the mild valley air with the orange trees heavy behind them, and Rosa had not cried, because Rosa never cried in front of people.

"You'll come back," Rosa had said. "You always said home was a house you couldn't sleep in. Now you're going to sleep in it."

"I'm going to try," Cora had said.

"And the organizer? You're not going to wait for him?"

Cora had looked down the platform at the rails going east, out of the green and into the brown. "He's not waiting for me. He's got a whole valley to organize. I'll write him. I'll tell him where I land."

Rosa had nodded, and the bus had come, and Rosa had hugged her once, hard, and stepped back, and the last Cora had seen of the valley was Rosa's arm raised, and beyond it the orchards going grey in the dusk, the camp where the tents had been torn down, the labor office with the blacklist posted in the window, the whole exploded west receding behind the glass.

Lula had come too, at the last minute, unsteady on the platform with her cane and her old coat, and she had taken Cora's chin in her hand and turned her face to the light.

"You've got the mark now," Lula had said. "You got the mark the same as Miguel — you stood up and you got hit, and you got up. That mark's not shame. It's the tally. When you get home, you tell your people what a pound of cotton costs in blood. That's what you carry back. That's the wage."

Cora had kissed her cheek, and the old woman had smelled of sage and coal oil, and she had said, "Take care of your mother. The ones who stay carry the load, but they don't get to see it carried. You go be the one who carries it in front of her, so she knows she's not alone."

The bus had swallowed her then, and she had watched the valley fall away behind her, the green going brown at the pass, the orchards giving way to the desert, the desert giving way to the endless flats, and all the way across Arizona and New Mexico she had thought about the mark, and the tally, and what you carry back.

She had ridden the bus as far as she could afford, and the driver had looked at her ticket twice, and the man next to her had smelled of onions and diesel and had slept against her shoulder for a hundred miles, and she had let him, because it was easier than waking him and because a sleeping man was a kind of company, and all the way across the panhandle she had watched the land flatten out and the fences fill up with dust and the houses sit low against the horizon like boats gone dry.

The bus station was warm and loud and full of men. She stood at the counter and asked how far north a truck would carry her, and the man behind the counter laughed and said, "As far as you can talk a driver into," and she went out and stood on the loading dock and watched the trucks come and go, and she waited.

She knew how to wait now. The valley had taught her. A woman who has stood in a picket line knows how to stand. She leaned against the wall of the station with her bag between her feet and she watched the trucks, and she could read them now the way she could not read them a year ago — the load, the owner's name on the door, the driver's eyes, the way he handled the gears. She had learned to read men on the road, and she had learned to read them close, and she had learned that most of them were just tired and a few of them were wolves, and she had learned the difference, and the learning was part of the patina the year had put on her.

A truck driver with a load of feed sacks and a route up the county mail road took her north for the price of her helping unload at two stops. She rode high in the cab above the flat land and watched the morning come up over the endless brown, and the driver asked her where she was bound and she said Boise City, and he said, "Nothing up there but dust and wind and people waiting on rain," and she said, "I know. It's home."

The truck dropped her at the crossroads where the county road met the mail road, because the feed store was there and the truck had business, and she stood with her bag in the cold March wind and watched it go, and she was alone then, with twenty miles of dirt road between her and the farm, and no money for a ride, and she started to walk.

The road was drifted. The wind had been at it for weeks, burying the ruts and uncovering them again, and she walked in the middle of it where the sand was thin, and the sun went up and the light got flat and white and the land ran away to the edge of the world on every side. She had forgotten how big the sky was here. The valley had walls; the sky was a ceiling. Here the sky was the whole of it, a white lid over the brown, and the horizon was a rumor, the way Dutch had said, the way she had learned on the trucks.

She walked past farms she knew. The Bell place, empty, the windows boarded, the windmill throwing its shadow on a dry stock tank. The old Carter place, where a woman she did not recognize stood at a well with a bucket, and the woman looked at her and did not wave, and Cora did not wave either, because there was no telling who was friend and who was gone and who was stranger now. The land was emptier than she remembered. The fences were buried in places, the fence posts showing like teeth through the drift, and the farms stood farther apart than they had a year ago, and she felt the emptiness in her chest like a thing she had swallowed.

She thought about the west as she walked, the way you think about a fire you have just walked away from — the heat still on you, the smell in your clothes. The strike, the beatings, the busted walkout, the camp scattered down 66 like dust before a wind. Miguel gone to the Imperial Valley with the pea harvest. Rosa somewhere in the orange groves. Lula's tally. And under all of it, the arithmetic Miguel had taught her, the one that had put her mother's ledger in a new light: the grower's scale, the tare, the wet-weight trick, the deductions that ate the wage, the arithmetic that said a pound of cotton costs more than it pays. She had come home with that arithmetic in her head, the way her mother carried the weather column in hers, and she did not know yet what she would do with it, only that it was hers now.

She thought about the seed packet in her coat lining, the zinnias, and she put her hand over it without meaning to, the way you touch a pocket where a letter is. She had carried it west unplanted. She had almost planted it once, at the fruit camp, in the good black soil of the orchard rows, and she had stopped, because to plant it there would have been to say the camp was home, and she had not been ready to say it. She had told herself she was saving it. The truth, she understood now, was that she had been keeping it against the day she came back, the way a sailor keeps a jar of harbor water.

The feed store man had offered her a ride the last two miles, but she had shaken her head, because she wanted to come up the rise on her own feet, the way she had left.

By afternoon her boots were full of sand and her legs ached and the feed store and the road and the sky had all gone the same brown. She passed the old schoolhouse where her mother was teaching now, the one-room building with the stovepipe, the windows dark, the flag gone, and she stopped and looked at it, at the place where her mother stood in front of eight children and taught them the seed catalog and the map of the road, and she thought about the letters, the ones that had come and the ones that had stopped, and she walked on.

The sun was going down when she came to the rise. The county road climbed a low ridge of sandhill where the windbreak used to run, and she came up out of the last drift onto the rise and she stopped, and the farm was below her, small in the failing light.

The house was smaller than memory. She had carried it in her head for a year, the way you carry a house, big with the weight of a family, and the real house sat low and grey on the flood plain with the barn behind it and the root cellar door showing and the cistern catching the last light, and it was smaller than she remembered and older and it was still standing, and that was the thing that moved her, that it was still standing.

The windbreak was gone. She had known it from the letters, from June's letters that had finally come through the winter, but knowing was different from seeing. The double row of cottonwood, willow, and Russian olive that had run the north quarter for two years, the rows she had helped water from the cistern in the spring of '35, the extra tree for Ezra at the end — it was stumps now, a ragged line of broken teeth against the sky, and the bare ground beyond it was already drifting.

She stood on the rise a long time. The wind came up and took her coat and she let it. She looked at the house and the stumps and the barn and the thin smoke coming out of the kitchen chimney, and she knew her mother was inside, that her mother had lit that fire, and the knowing put a weight in her chest that was half fear and half something she had not let herself feel for a year.

She went down the slope. The road curved through the pasture where the fence had been and she walked the last quarter mile with the light going grey around her and the smoke climbing thin and straight in the windless dusk, and she came to the gate, the old wire gate that had hung crooked since the storm, and she put her hand on it and it was cold, and she opened it and went through, and the dogs came up the yard to meet her, the old collie, barking, and she said, "Hush, it's me," and the collie knew her and turned and ran ahead to the house.

Her mother came out on the porch. June stood in the doorway with the light behind her, wiping her hands on her apron, and she looked down the yard at the girl coming up the path, and she did not move, and Cora kept walking, and when she was close enough she saw her mother's face, and she saw the year on it, the grey in the hair, the new lines at the mouth, the way the shoulders had gone thin, and she stopped.

"Ma," she said.

June came down the steps. She did not run. She walked the way she walked everything, steady, and she came to Cora and she stopped a hand's breadth away and she looked at her, at the patched coat and the brown face and the ribs that had healed, and she said, "You're thinner," and her voice was the same, the same even plain voice, and Cora stood in the yard with the cold coming down and the smell of the woodsmoke in her nose and she could not speak.

"It's good," June said. "You're home. Come in and wash. There's soup on the stove and I've got a letter from your father I'll have you read, and there's news, and the boy's up and around and he'll want to see you before he'll believe it."

Cora stood in the yard. The dog circled them. The light was almost gone and the wind was down and the first cold stars were coming out over the north quarter where the stumps stood, and she looked past her mother at the house, at the warm window, at the smoke, and she said, "The windbreak's gone."

"Yes," June said. "It is."

They stood a moment in the cold.

"We'll plant it again," Cora said. "We'll put it back."

June looked at her a long moment, and something moved in her face, something she banked the way she banked the fire, and she said, "That's the first thing you've said that sounded like staying."

She turned and went up the steps and held the door, and Cora followed her into the warm and the light and the smell of soup.

The kitchen was the same. That was the thing she had not expected, how the sameness would undo her. The same table, the same oilcloth, the same jars on the high shelf, the same crack in the same window pane that had been there when she left, the same ledger on the same shelf between the seed jars, and her mother moving the same way at the stove, dipping the same soup, and it was all the same and she was not, and the sameness held her the way a hand holds a wrist.

Sammy came in from the other room, thin and pale and alive, and he stopped in the doorway and looked at her, and then he grinned, the old grin, and said, "You're back," and she said, "I'm back," and he came and hugged her, and he was taller than she remembered and lighter in the chest, and she could feel the boy's ribs under the shirt, the boy who had nearly died in February, and she held him a moment longer than she meant to.

At the table, over the soup, June told her the winter: the boy's sickness, the doctor, the eight children. Cora told her the west: the strike, the walkout, the hospital tent, careful with the bruises, the way she had been careful in the letters, and June listened and did not press, and the lamp burned, and the wind came up the yard and went over the roof, and the stumps stood in the dark outside the warm window, and the smoke climbed, and the house held the light.

Chapter 34: The Dust Storm Again

JUNE — the farm — late March 1936

The sky turned green at noon. That was the thing that had always marked the bad ones, the color before the dark, and June was at the well when she saw it, the green coming up over the north quarter where the windbreak stumps stood, and she felt the old lurch in her chest, the one she had felt a hundred times since 1933, and she set the bucket down and went in to close the windows.

Cora was on the porch. She had been sitting on the steps in the thin March sun, mending a strap on her bag, and she stood up when the wind took the light, and she looked at the green sky the way you look at an old enemy come back, and June came out and stood beside her and they watched it come, the wall of it, the brown rolling up under the green like a tide.

"Three years," Cora said. "I'd forgotten what it looks like from this side."

"Get the cloths," June said. "Wet them. Same as always."

They moved the way they had always moved, the drill of it worn into them — June at the windows with the rags, Cora hauling the wash water, Sammy coming in from the yard with the chickens shut and the coop door latched. The three of them worked the house shut, the way you batten a ship, and when the first wave hit, the brown wall of it striking the side of the house like something with shoulders, they stood in the kitchen with the lamp lit and listened to the dust work its way in anyway, through the cracks, under the door, the fine sifting that no rags and no water ever fully stopped.

Sammy stood at the window with his hand on the glass, watching. He was out of bed and thin and pale, still learning to breathe, and the doctor had said no dust for him, not a mouthful, and June had him masked, the wet cloth tied over his nose and mouth, and he looked small and old behind it, the way all the children of the county looked behind their masks.

"I thought it was over," Sammy said, his voice muffled. "You said it was over."

"It's the last of them," June said. "The doctor said the weather would turn. This is the old weather going out the door, slamming it."

She said it to steady him, and she half believed it herself, and she watched the sky through the sifting glass and she remembered all the storms, the first one, the one that buried the farm in '33, the one that took the fence line, the one that took Ezra's marker, the Black Sunday storm that buried them a third time and sent the family west in pieces, and she counted them off in her head the way she counted seeds, and she thought about the ledger, about the weather column, about how the column had a shape now, a long downward stroke of dry, and how she had written the entry for April 14, 1935, "the third burial. We are still here," and how she had not known, writing it, whether the column would ever turn.

The storm was the worst in a year. It came in three waves, each one filling the house with the brown sifting, and between the waves there was a false calm, a stillness that was worse than the wind because you knew it was a breath being taken. June had learned that stillness the way you learn a neighbor's habits, and she moved in it, filling the lamps, banking the stove, checking the wet cloths at the door, and she did not waste the calm the way she had wasted it in the early years, running to the well and the coop, scattering herself thin against a thing you cannot outrun.

The house went dark at the third wave. The noon light died like a lamp turned down, and the dust came in earnest, fine and total, and June lit the lamp and the flame burned small and brown, and the three of them sat at the table with the lamp between them, the way you sit out a storm, and the dust worked into the food and the sheets and the air, and it did not matter, because the dust had always gotten in.

Sammy coughed once, and June's hand went out, and he waved her off. "It's the dust," he said, muffled through the mask. "Not the other. I can tell the difference now."

She watched him, and she believed him, and the belief was a new thing in the room, the boy alive, the boy breathing, the boy telling her which cough was which.

Cora stood at the other window. June watched her watch the storm, and she saw the year on her daughter, the way she stood, the way her hands were still, the way her eyes went over the brown wall and did not flinch, and June thought about the girl who had left on the harvest trucks, hungry for the world, and the woman who had come back up the rise, and she understood that the storm meant something different to each of them now, and she said, "Tell me about the storms out there."

Cora did not turn. "They didn't have them. That's what they said, out west. We left the dust, and there's no dust where we're going. But there's other weather. There's the weather of men."

"Men," June said.

"The growers. The deputies. The arithmetic. You taught me the ledger is the arithmetic of the land. Out there I learned the arithmetic of men, and it eats you the same way a drought does, and it doesn't wait for a dry year. It runs all year round."

June watched her. The light through the window was brown and strange, and the house had gone dim in the middle of the day, and the dust sifted and the wind worked at the walls, and she said, "Are you hurt?"
