Cora was quiet a long moment. "I got marked," she said. "Same as the land. I stood up and I got hit, and I got up. That's all."

June came to stand beside her at the window. They stood shoulder to shoulder, the two of them, watching the brown wall roll past the glass, and June said, "I got marked too. The Hallorans took the windbreak for firewood in October. I caught her loading the last greenwood into the wagon, and we said things to each other that can't be unsaid. And then this winter the boy nearly died, and she brought me chicken broth, and I took it."

"The same woman."

"The same woman. The land marks us and we still have to live next to each other. That's the whole lesson of the section, if there is one."

It blew like that for hours. The light came and went, the brown thinning and thickening like a man breathing, and they worked through it the way they always had, June at the stove, Cora at the cloths, Sammy on the bed with the almanac, and they ate at the table with the lamp burning at noon, and no one said much, and the quiet was not the quiet of defeat, the way it had been in the other storms, but something else, something held.

Cora talked a little, at the table, in the brown light. She talked about the valley, about the green, about how the orchards had looked in the spring of that year, the peach rows in bloom, the cotton coming up white under the irrigation ditch, and June listened and did not ask the questions that were in her, the questions about the beating, about the man who had taught her arithmetic, about the letters that had stopped. She let her daughter give her what she could give, the way you let a horse come to the fence, and she gave back the things she could give, the winter, the boy, the doctor, the ledger, and the two of them traded their years across the table like women trading seed across a fence.

In the afternoon the wind changed. June felt it first at her back, at the door, the way the pressure shifted, and she went to the window and the brown was thinning, and through it she saw a patch of sky, the pale high blue that had not shown over the county in weeks, and she watched it widen like a window opening.

She stood at the glass and watched it come back, the light, and she thought about the last time she had stood in a storm's wake, about the times she had stood in the same place and watched the brown roll on, about the arithmetic of the weather column, the long account of dry, and she did not trust the light yet. She had trusted the light before and the light had lied. But the air at the door had changed, and the change was not in the sky, it was in the ground under her feet, in the way the silence settled after the last of the wind, in the way the dust did not rise again off the yard.

She went to the door and opened it. The wind was still going but the grit was settling, and she stood on the porch and watched the storm go east, the wall of it moving off across the pasture, the light coming back over the stumps, and the air after it was different. She stood with her hand on the doorjamb and she breathed it, and there was something in it she had not tasted in years.

Cora came out behind her. Sammy came out, mask pulled down. The three of them stood on the porch and watched the east, where the brown was going, and the sky was clearing to the high thin blue, and the light was coming down clean, and June stood in the new air and she knew it the way she knew the taste of water after a dry well, and she said, "It's done. It's over."

Sammy looked at her. "The storms?"

"The storms," she said. "I can't prove it. The almanac won't say it. But I can smell it. The dust is done. Something's coming in behind it."

Cora looked at her mother. The light was full on them now, the first clean light in a long time, and she said, "How do you know?"

June was quiet. She looked out at the north quarter, at the stumps, at the drifted field, at the bare ground where the windbreak had stood, and she said, "The dust has a smell when it's going. It's been going all winter, and I didn't see it because I was watching the boy. Now I see it. There's wet in the air. I can feel it in the wells. I can feel it in my hands. The land's not done yet, and the land doesn't lie the way people do."

The wind had dropped to nothing. The evening came up clear and cold, the first true dusk in months, the stars coming out over the flood plain, and the three of them stood on the porch a long time and watched the sky go dark, and the air was still and clean, and the house stood with the windows open behind them, the dust being swept out, the lamps being lit, and June stood with her hand on the doorjamb and she let herself believe it, the way you let yourself believe in seed, on faith, against the evidence of the years.

In the morning she went to the ledger. She opened it to the weather column, to the long dry stroke that had run from 1933 to this spring, and she looked at the last entries, and she dipped the pen and she wrote, in her small even hand, at the bottom of the column, not a date, not a forecast, just the two words she had said on the porch, the two words that were not a prayer because she had never learned to pray in words:

"It's over."

And she closed the ledger and set it back on the shelf between the seed jars, and she went out into the clean morning, into the light, and she stood in the yard with the air coming down new and cold around her, and she smelled the wet coming, the way you smell a thing before it arrives, the way the land knows the rain before the sky shows it.

The sky was clear. The stumps stood in the sun. And somewhere to the west, out of sight, the air was carrying the first rain.

Chapter 35: The Sandbags

CORA — the farm and the Cimarron — April 1936

The rain came the second week of April, and it was not the rain of memory, the rain that came down grey and thin and blew away. It came in the night, and it came hard, and Cora woke to the sound of it on the roof, the drumming, the sound she had not heard in a year, and she lay in the dark and listened and did not dare believe it.

By morning the yard was mud. The ground that had been dust for three years drank and drank and the water stood in the wheel ruts and the hooves and the old furrows, and the air was full of the smell, the wet-earth smell that the county had forgotten, and Sammy stood on the porch with his face lifted and the rain coming down on it and he did not put up his hands, he just stood there with his eyes closed, and Cora watched him and she remembered the valley, the irrigation ditches, the green, and she thought, we are going to be green now.

The county woke up changed. Every woman went to her door that morning and stood looking out at the mud the way you look at a stranger, and every man went down to the dry creek bed that ran through his place, and the men came back up with the word passing ahead of them, the river was up, the river was rising, the river had taken the bridges. There was no telephone that reached across the flood plain, but the news moved anyway, the way news moves in a county that has been dry together for three years, carried by riders and wagons and the men who walked the fence lines to see the water with their own eyes.

The feed store in Boise City did a business in canvas and twine that day. The county agent came out of his office with a list of the low farms, and the Vogel place was near the top, and he sent word down the county road by a boy on a horse, and the boy stood in the rain on the Vogel porch and told them the bridges were gone and the river was still coming and they were to move the stock and the women and the children to the high ground, and June stood in the doorway and listened and did not argue, and Cora watched her and saw the ledger's weather column running in her mother's face, the dry years, the long account, and now this, the account going the other way, all of it at once, the river settling the years of drought in a week.

June knew the river. She had been born on the flood plain, and her father before her, and she knew the river's habits the way you know a relative's moods, and on the third day she stood at the north pasture fence and watched the water moving beyond the trees, and her face was not glad, and Cora came up beside her and said, "What is it?"

"Too much," June said. "Too fast. The ground can't take it. It's been hard as brick for three years and now it's taking all of it at once, and the river's got nowhere to put it but here. This farm was built on the river's floor. My father lost a barn to it in '12. It remembers this land."

The county men came up the road that afternoon with word from Boise City: the bridges were gone upriver, both of them, the timbers taken out by the flood and the approaches washed, and the water was still coming, and the farms on the flood plain were to move their stock and their families to the high ground and take what they could carry.

June stood in the yard and listened to the county man, and she did not argue, the way she had not argued about the land in the dry years, and when he was done she said, "We're not leaving the house," and he said, "Nobody's saying leave the house, Mrs. Vogel. We're saying move the stock and the seed and the people, and be ready," and she said, "We'll be ready," and she went in.

Cora watched her mother go in, and she watched the river, and she did the arithmetic the way the year had taught her. The house sat low. The porch steps came up off the yard, and the yard came up off the pasture, and the pasture came up off the river, and the whole of it lay open to the east where the water would come, and she stood in the rain and she understood that the flood plain was not a figure of speech, that the plain was the river's bed when the river chose to take it back.

Abel Halloran came that night. Cora had not seen him since the night of the axe, and she watched him come up the lane through the rain with a roll of canvas sacks over his shoulder, and he stood at the foot of the porch and took his hat off and let the rain come down on his bare head, and he said, "I got sacks. A man down at the feed store said the river's going to take the low farms, and I thought of your place, being lowest."

June came to the door. The two of them looked at each other across the yard, the woman whose windbreak he had cut, the man who had cut it, and the rain came down between them, and June said, "You cut my trees."

"Yes," Abel said. "I did. And my wife's been sick over it, and I've been sick over it, and I can't give you the wood back, and I can't give you the years it would have taken to grow. But I can carry sand, and I can bring my sacks, and I can stand in the water on your land and help you hold your house, and it won't make the trees come back, but it's what I've got."

June stood a long moment in the doorway. The rain came down. The dogs had stopped barking and stood under the porch with their heads low, and Cora watched her mother's face, and she saw the war in it, the ledger and the hurt and the frost-nipped truce and the chicken broth and the seed swap and the lilac cutting, and then June stepped back from the door and said, "The seed's in the cellar. The cellar's the low place. We move the seed first, and then we sandbag."

They worked all night. The rain came down and the river rose and the men came, the section men, the same men who had stood at the feed store and passed the shelterbelt pamphlet hand to hand, and the women came, and the children came, and they filled the sacks with the sand the wind had drifted up against the fence lines, the dust of three years, the dust that had buried them, now stacked in sacks against the water, and there was something in it that Cora felt but could not say, the dust turned to a wall, the enemy turned to a tool.

The men laid the sacks along the east wall where the yard ran down to the pasture, and the women worked the line behind them, tying and stacking, and the lanterns hissed in the rain and the wind moved the light and the shadows of the men went long and short across the mud, and Cora worked with her hands the way the year had taught her, the way she had worked the cotton rows and the packing shed and the picket line, and she found that the work of the flood was the same work as the work of the strike: you put your body where the pressure is, and you hold the line, and the line holds if enough of you stand on it.

Cora filled sacks with Mrs. Halloran. The two of them worked side by side in the rain with the lanterns hissing, and neither of them spoke of the windbreak, and then Mrs. Halloran said, "He wanted to tell you himself, and I wouldn't let him, and I'm sorry for that too. He was ashamed. A man ashamed does stupid things, and a man who's done stupid things gets more ashamed, and it's a wheel that turns on itself."

"Your children were cold," Cora said. "That's what I keep coming back to. You took my mother's trees because your children were cold."

"They were cold," Mrs. Halloran said. "They're always cold now. And I still took what wasn't mine, and I still lied about it, and I'll stand in the water here until it's paid back or it isn't, whichever comes first."

Cora filled a sack and tied it and set it on the pile. The rain drummed on her back. She looked out at the pasture where the water was walking up through the grass, the brown line of it moving steady and slow like something patient, and she said, "It's coming."

The water came up the pasture in the dark. Cora watched it from the porch, the line of it crossing the yard like a slow army, and the men worked at the east wall where the sandbags were going up, and the women carried the lamps and the children carried the dry things to the high shelf, and June moved through it all with the ledger under her arm, wrapped in oilcloth the way she had wrapped it for a journey, and she did not let it go, not to fill a sack, not to light a lamp, not for anything, and Cora understood that her mother would go down with that book before she would let it go, and the understanding put a coldness in her that was not the rain.

She found June in the root cellar, moving the jars. The cellar was the lowest place on the place, the place the water would find first, and June was handing up the seed corn jars, the jar of saved corn, the jars of beans and wheat and the seed packets from the swap, and Cora took them and set them on the high shelf in the kitchen where the flood could not reach, the jars that held next year's planting, the seed saved through the drought and the storms, the seed that was the family's whole bet on a spring.

Cora worked the jars up out of the dark, and her mother worked the shelves below her, and the rain came in through the cellar door and stood on the dirt floor and crept up the stairs behind them, and they raced it, and the race was the same race the family had been running for three years, the race against the land taking what they had saved, and this time the water was not the brown wind, it was the brown river, and it came up the cellar steps with a sound like a tongue.

"Don't lose the beans," June said. "The beans are the ones that will feed us if everything else fails. Corn is money, but beans are food."

Cora took the bean jar and set it on the high shelf. "How do you know which is which?"

"I've been keeping this book since 1919," June said. "I know every seed on this farm the way I know your face. Corn for selling, beans for eating, wheat for flour, and the seed corn in the big jars is the seed for next year's planting, and you don't eat the seed. You never eat the seed. That's the first law."

The last jar came up out of the dark. June came up behind it, the ledger still under her arm in the oilcloth, and she stood in the kitchen with the water creeping across the cellar floor below her and the jars on the high shelf above her and the rain coming down beyond the door, and she looked at the seed and then at the river, and she said, "That's all we've got. That's the whole farm, right there on that shelf. Three years of saving, and it fits on one shelf."

"We save the seed," June said, "and we lose the seed, and we save it again. That's the whole story of the Vogel family. You could write it on the back of a seed sack."

"Ma," Cora said. "You're going to come up. When the water comes, you're going to come up and stand on the porch with the rest of us."

June looked at her. The lantern light moved on her face. "I'll come up," she said. "I'll come up when the seed's up. And the ledger comes with me, and I come with the ledger, and that's the order of it. You learn that, and you'll be able to hold this farm when I'm gone."

The water reached the east wall in the small hours. Cora stood in the rain with the sacks stacked and the lantern hissing and watched it come, and she thought about the valley, about the flood of the strike, about everything the year had taught her, and she took the next sack from Abel Halloran and set it on the wall, and she stood with her mother's neighbors on her mother's land in the rain, and she did not know if the house would hold, and she worked anyway, because that was the arithmetic, the arithmetic her mother had always known: you work, and then you see.

Chapter 36: The Flood of the Cimarron

JUNE — the farm — April 1936

The water arrived at dawn. It did not arrive like a wall, the way the stories told it, the way the county men had described the bridges going out. It arrived like a tide, like something that had always been coming and had only been waiting for the right season, and June stood at the window in the grey light and watched it walk up the yard, and she felt the house take its first small settling, the timbers shifting on the ground that had held them for thirty years, and she knew the river had found them.

The sandbags held the east wall. The men had worked until the light failed and then worked on in the rain, and the wall of sacks stood three high along the low side, and the water came up to the sacks and stopped, and then it came over them, slow, the brown finding the seam where one sack met the next, and the water stood in the yard to the depth of a man's boot, and then to a man's knee, and the men moved the sacks and the women moved the last of the dry things higher, and the water kept coming.

The stock went first. June had sent the cow and the horses up to the high ground with the section men the night before, and the chickens were in the barn loft, and she heard the barn door go in the dark, the water taking it off its hinges, and she heard the chickens go, a panicked clatter of wings against the inside of the loft, and then the loft was not high enough, and the chickens came out into the rain in a white scatter and the water took them, and June stood at the window and watched the chickens float past the porch, white and still and gone, and she did not count them, because counting the dead is a different arithmetic from counting the living.

Cora was at the door. She had her coat on and her boots and she was looking out at the yard where the water was climbing the steps, and she said, "The porch steps are under. It's at the door."

"We stay," June said. "The house has stood thirty years. It'll stand one more night."

"Ma—"

"Bring the oilcloth off the shelf. And the matches, and the lamp, and anything else that can't float. We're going to sit it out in the kitchen, on the high side, and we're going to keep the seed dry, and we're going to be here in the morning."

Cora looked at her, and June saw the argument in her face, the daughter who had been west, who had seen men put their weight where the pressure was and get broken for it, and she waited for the argument, and then Cora went and did what she said, and June stood at the window and watched the water climb the porch and cover the steps and she thought about all the years, the dry years, the years of watching the wind strip the topsoil and carry it east, the years of watching the family go west in pieces, and she thought about how the water was doing what the wind had done, taking the land, and how different it felt, the taking by water, slower, surer, like a hand closing.

The water came in under the door. It came the way the dust had come, finding the same cracks, the same low places, the dust that had sifted in for three years now running in as water, and June took the wet cloths that had hung at the door against the dust for three years and she stuffed them into the crack under the door, the same cloths, the same door, the enemy changed, the defense the same, and the water found the next crack and she stuffed that one too, and she worked the house the way she had worked it against the dust, closing it, battening it, and it did not close, and the water came in anyway, and it stood an inch deep on the kitchen floor and then two, and June moved the jars to the highest shelf and stood on a chair and set them on the very top, above the reach of the water she could see and the water she could not see, and she did not know how high the water would go, and she put the seed as high as her hands could reach and then higher, and the water kept coming.

The ledger never left her. It went with her in the oilcloth, from the window to the door to the shelf to the high shelf, and when the water stood at her knees she tied the oilcloth bundle to her with a length of twine, the way you carry a child, the way you carry a thing you will not put down, and she moved through the house with it against her chest, and Cora watched her and did not say anything, because there was nothing to say.

Sammy was in the loft, on the stairs. He had the .22, the one she had put on the pantry shelf, and he had it over his shoulder and he was watching the water come up the hall, and she looked at him and she did not tell him to put it down. The water was the only thing the gun was for now, and it would not help, but a boy with a gun is a boy who still thinks there is something to be done, and she did not take that from him.

"Sammy," she said. "The high shelf. The seed jars. If the water reaches them, you tell me."

He nodded and went up the stairs. The water followed him up the hall, slow, brown, patient.

The barn went in the afternoon. June heard the beams give, a long groan that went on and on and then a crack like a shot, and the whole of it settled sideways into the flood, and the roof went under, and the water jumped the yard and came at the house from the side now, from the north, where the windbreak stumps stood under the water, and the sandbags at the east wall were under, and the water came in from every direction at once, and it stood ankle deep through the whole downstairs, and the kitchen table floated, a slow turn in the middle of the room like a boat at anchor, and June watched the table turn and the water carry the chairs one by one out the door, and she stood in the middle of the room with the ledger at her breast and the water at her knees and she did not move.

Cora came to her. The girl was soaked to the shoulders and her face was white with the cold and the effort, and she said, "We have to go to the roof. The water's still rising. Ma, we have to go to the roof."

"The seed—"

"Is on the highest shelf. I checked. Sammy's on the stairs with it. He's not leaving it, Ma. And you're not either, and that means we're staying together, and if we're staying together we're staying alive."

June looked at her daughter. The water moved around her knees. The house groaned and settled. The rain came down beyond the door, and the river ran past the porch where the fence had been, and the world beyond the window was brown water with the tops of trees showing, and she stood in the middle of it with the ledger against her chest and she heard the word that had been coming to her all day, the word she had heard in the storm, the word the land had been saying to her for three years, the word she had written in the ledger after Black Sunday and after the flood of her father's barn and after Ezra's grave and after every dry spring, the word she had never said aloud to anyone:

reckoning.

It came up out of the water. It was the river's word and the land's word and it was the sum of the years, the dry years and the wet years, the buried fences and the cut windbreak and the boy in the ground and the family scattered, and the flood was the earth's own reckoning, the earth settling its account with the years of dust, and she stood in the water with the ledger at her breast and she was not afraid, and that was the strange thing, that the terror of the day had gone out of her and what was left was something like quiet, the quiet of a column added and re-added and finally made to balance.

"All right," she said. "We go to the roof. But the ledger comes, and I come with the ledger."

Cora took her arm. They went up the stairs through the rising water, June first with the ledger, Cora behind her, and the water followed them up, and Sammy was at the top with the seed jar under his arm and the .22 over his shoulder, and the three of them stood at the top of the stairs and looked down at the water climbing the last treads, and then they went through the window onto the roof.

The roof was a raft in a brown world. The rain had thinned to a drizzle and the light was grey and wide, and the water stretched away to the trees and beyond the trees to where the sky and the water met, and the house was an island, and the three of them were on it, and June sat with her back to the chimney with the ledger at her breast and the oilcloth over it, and Cora sat beside her with her arm through her mother's arm, and Sammy sat on the ridge with the seed jar between his knees, and the water came up over the eaves and stopped, and the house held.

They did not talk. There was nothing to talk about that the water was not already saying. The light went grey and darker, and the evening came, and the water stayed, and the house stayed, and the three of them stayed, and June watched the water move past the roof, carrying the evidence of the county with it — a chicken coop, a wagon wheel, the top of a fence post, a child's ball, floating slow and indifferent — and she watched them go, the wreckage of the neighbors' lives, and she did not look away, because looking away was the first sin, the one she had committed in the dry years when she had not wanted to see what the dust was doing.

In the dark, with the water murmuring around the house and the drizzle coming down, Cora spoke.

"Ma. In the valley, when the strike broke, the camp went in one morning. The tents came down and the people went up the road and there was nothing left but the mud and the trucks going away. I stood in it and I thought, this is what it is to lose everything. I thought I'd seen it."

June said nothing.

"This is worse," Cora said. "This is the whole county, all at once. And you're sitting on the roof of your house in the dark and you're not crying and you're not shaking. How?"

June was quiet a long moment. The water moved around the house. The drizzle came down.

"Because I've been here before," she said. "Not on this roof. But in this water. When I was a girl, the river took my father's barn, and I stood on the porch with my mother and watched it go, and I thought the world was ending, and it wasn't. And I've been watching the world end, a piece at a time, for fifteen years, and every time it's ended I've been here the next morning, and the next spring, and the next storm, and I'll be here the morning after this one too, because that's what the seed is for. You don't plant it in the good years and eat it in the bad. You plant it in the good years and save it in the bad, and you plant it again."

The water had stopped rising. June felt it before she saw it, the way the pressure changed, the way the murmur of the river shifted and settled, and she sat up and looked out at the water and she could not read the level in the dark, but she knew, the way you know the rain has stopped by the silence, that the river had turned.

"I think it's over," she said. "I think the water's going back."

"How do you know?"

"The sound," June said. "It's a different sound. It's been coming at the house all day, and now it's just going by. Listen."

They listened. The three of them on the roof in the dark, and the water was still moving past the house, but the sound had changed, the pressure gone out of it, and Sammy said, "It is different," and Cora said, "It is," and they sat in the dark and listened to the river turning, and the rain stopped, and the stars came out over the flood, one by one, the first stars over the drowned county, and June sat with the ledger at her breast and the oilcloth over it and her children on either side of her and the house under them holding, and she thought about the word, the reckoning, and she thought about what came after, the water going down, the mud, the green, the seed in the jar, the family whole on the roof of the house that had held, and she let herself believe the way she had let herself believe at the storm's end, on faith, against the evidence of the years.

In the morning the water was down to the porch. The three of them came in through the window, the seed jar first, the ledger second, the people after, and the house was a wreck of mud and silt and floating chairs, and the yard was brown water going down, and the barn was a heap of lumber in the mud, and the fence was gone, and the pasture was painted with silt, and the sun was coming up over the flood, clean and gold, the way it had not come up in three years, and June stood on the porch with the ledger at her breast and the mud at her boots and the sun on her face, and she thought the word again, the reckoning, and she thought, this is what it cost, and this is what it bought, and she looked at the mud and the wreckage and her son and her daughter and the seed jar between them, and she was still here, and the house had held, and the sun was up.

The family stood on the porch in the mud and watched the water go, and no one said anything, and the sun climbed, and the drowned county began to dry.

Chapter 37: The House Held

CORA — the farm — April 1936

The water went down the way it had come up, slow, taking its time, and Cora stood on the porch in the mud-caked light of the first morning and watched it fall. It was down to the porch steps by breakfast, down to the yard by noon, and by evening the yard was a brown sea of standing puddles with the tops of the weeds showing through, and the smell was the smell of a drowned country, wet and green and rank, the smell of the river giving back what it had taken.

The house had held.

She had been awake before the light, lying in the loft in the wet clothes she had dried over the stove, listening to the water slide off the roof and the house settle and the world beyond the window go quiet, and she had been afraid to get up, afraid that getting up would break the spell, that the daylight would show the house cracked open or the porch gone or the whole of it a wreck like the barn. She had lain still and listened to her mother's breathing in the room below and her brother's in the loft with her, and she had counted the sounds the house made, the creak and the settle and the drip, and she had told herself, the way she had told herself on the roof in the dark, that the house had held, and then she had got up and gone down the stairs and the house had held, and she had stood on the porch in the mud-light and watched the water fall, and she had believed it.

She walked the count of it that first day, the way you walk a field you are thinking of planting. The barn was gone, a heap of grey lumber in the mud with the roof beams snapped and the loft floor hanging, and she stood in front of it and remembered the chickens coming out into the rain, the white scatter, and she looked at the wreck and she did not cry, because crying was not the work. The chicken coop was gone, washed off its blocks and lying on its side in the yard. The fence along the east was gone, the posts washed out and the wire lying in loops in the silt. The cistern was silted and would have to be cleaned or dug. The root cellar was full of water and the water would have to be pumped before anything could go back in it, and the pump handle, she saw, was gone.

But the house had held. And the seed had held. And the ledger had held. And the family had held, and Cora stood in the middle of the wreckage and put her hands on her hips and looked at it, and she heard her mother's voice in her head, the voice from the kitchen the night before the water came: corn is money, beans are food, and you never eat the seed. And she said it out loud, standing in the mud, to the wreckage: "We don't eat the seed."

Sammy was out in the yard with his boots on and his pants rolled and a stick in his hand, poking the mud, and he found things the water had brought and things the water had taken. He found the cistern lid, washed up against the barn wreckage. He found a neighbor's gate, a mile of water having carried it down to the Vogel yard. He found the weathervane off the barn roof, lying in the mud like a dead bird. And Cora watched him work through the wreckage the way a boy works, sorting what was lost from what was found, and she saw the seed jar under his arm, the one he had carried to the roof and down again, and he had not put it down since, and she did not ask him to.

The dead calf was in the pasture. Cora found it when she walked the fence line, the little body lying in the silt with the water still standing in rings around it, and she stood over it a long moment and then she went and found Abel Halloran, who was digging out his own place across the section, and she told him, and he came with his shovel and they buried it together in the high ground beyond the flood line, and Abel stood over the grave with his hat in his hand and said, "The river don't ask which farm it takes," and Cora said, "No," and they walked back to the house in the mud.

The neighbors came back through the week, the ones who had moved their families to the high ground, and they stood in the mud of their own yards and took the count, and they helped each other, the way the section had always worked, the way it had worked in the dry years when the windbreak pamphlet had gone hand to hand. Abel came back with his wagon and helped dig the cellar, and his wife came with the children and helped scrub the mud off the walls, and Mrs. Halloran stood in the kitchen with a rag and a bucket and she scrubbed the mud line below the window and she did not say much, and Cora watched her and she thought about the sandbag line, about the two of them filling sacks in the rain, and she said, "Thank you," and Mrs. Halloran said, "Don't thank me. It's the least of it," and she scrubbed.

June counted the livestock. The cow was on the high ground with the section herd, safe, and the horses with her, and the chickens were gone, every one, and June stood at the empty coop and wrote nothing down and said nothing, and Cora knew the count was in her head, going into the ledger's invisible column, the column of the dead and the gone, the column that never balanced but that had to be counted anyway.

The Red Cross truck came the second day. It came up the county road, which was a brown scar of mud and standing water, with the flag on the side and a woman and a man in the cab, and they pulled up at the gate and the woman got out with a clipboard and stood in the mud and asked the questions that everyone in the county was being asked: how many in the family, how much was lost, was anyone hurt, was the house livable, and Cora answered for the family because June was in the house with the ledger, and the woman wrote it all down and then she went to the truck and came back with blankets and cans of beans and a sack of flour and a box of salt, and she said, "The county's been through it, but the county's still here, and the government says to tell you that the relief will come, and you're to tell us what you need and we'll do what we can."

Cora stood in the mud with the blankets in her arms and the woman's clipboard and the flag on the truck, and she thought about the valley, about the relief hospital tent, about the nurse who had sewn her ribs, about how the same flag had carried the same kind of help to the strike camps, and she said, "We need word of my father. His name is Walter Vogel. He's out west, in the Imperial Valley, and we don't know if he knows the river took the county."

The woman wrote it down. "Imperial Valley. We've got a way to get word to the relief people out there. It'll take time, but it'll go."

Cora stood with the blankets and the beans and the flour and the salt and watched the truck go back down the mud road, and she thought about the letter, the one that would go west looking for her father, the root going out, and she did not know yet that the root would come back, or when, or what it would carry.

June found her in the evening, standing in the kitchen with the mud line on the walls and the water gone. June had the ledger in her hands, the oilcloth unwrapped at last, and she was turning the pages, checking, and Cora watched her mother's hands move over the book the way you touch a face to see if it's still there, and she said, "Is it dry?"

"It's dry," June said. "The oilcloth held. Every page. The whole of it, from 1919 to now."

Cora came to look. The pages lay open on the table in the lamplight, the small even handwriting running down the columns, the weather column and the seed column and the years, and she ran her finger down the page for 1934, the year Ezra died, and she stopped at the entry, the small dark handwriting, and she read it, the words her mother had written and never said:

"Spring 1934 — Ezra took the fever. The doctor came too late. We buried him on a Sunday in the rain. I have planted the extra tree. I will not leave this land while I live."

She looked up at her mother. June was standing at the stove, setting the beans to soak, her back to the room, and Cora said, "You never told me you wrote that."

"I didn't write it to tell you," June said. "I wrote it so it would be true. The ledger keeps what I can't keep in my head. It keeps the weather and the seed and the dead, and it's the only book that ever told the truth about this family, because it was written one day at a time, and a day at a time, you can stand it."

Cora closed the ledger carefully, the way you close a book that is sleeping, and she set it on the high shelf, and she stood in the kitchen with the mud line on the walls and the water gone and the seed jars on the shelf and the ledger between them, and she looked around the room, at the wreckage and the salvage, at what the river had taken and what it had left, and she felt the strangest thing she had felt in a year.

She laughed. It came up out of her before she could stop it, a small cracked sound, and June turned from the stove, and Cora said, "I'm sorry. I don't know why I did that."

"Don't be sorry," June said. "It's the first laugh that's been in this house since the water came, and I'll take it."

"It's just — we lost the barn, and the chicken coop, and the fence, and the calf, and half the county, and there's a mud line on the walls that'll be there till the walls fall, and we've got a sack of flour and a box of salt and a jar of seed corn and a book full of weather, and it's the most we've had in three years."

June stood at the stove and looked at her daughter across the mud-light, and a thing moved in her face, and she said, "That's the seed talking. You can't plant a laugh, but you can stand in the middle of a wreck and find one, and that's the whole of the family's fortune, right there."

Cora laughed again, a real one this time, and June laughed, a small sound, and they stood in the ruined kitchen and laughed, the two of them, at nothing, at the mud and the water and the years, and Sammy came in from the yard with his boots full of mud and his stick in his hand and the seed jar under his arm, and he looked at them, at his mother and his sister laughing in the middle of the wreck, and he said, "What's so funny?"

"Nothing," June said. "Everything. Come wash your boots, there's beans."

He came in and set the seed jar on the high shelf with the others, and the three of them sat at the table with the lamp lit and the mud line on the walls and the water gone, and they ate the beans, the first meal in the house that the water had not touched, and the light was warm and small and the house creaked and settled around them like a body getting comfortable, and Cora looked at her mother and her brother across the table and she thought about the count of what was lost and what was saved, and she did the arithmetic the way she had learned it, the way Miguel had taught her, the way her mother had always known it, and the sum came out on the side of the saved: the seed, the ledger, the house, the family, the laugh.

The family's first true laugh in a year, in the ruined kitchen, with the mud drying on the walls and the beans going down and the lamp burning, and outside, beyond the mud, the water going back to the river, and the river going back to its bed, and the land, for the first time in three years, wet and dark and ready.

Chapter 38: June's Confession

JUNE — the farm — April 1936

The mud dried in the yard and cracked into plates, and the river went back to its bed, and the county began to breathe. June worked through the days the way she had worked through everything, one thing at a time, the way the ledger taught you: the cellar pumped, the walls scrubbed, the seed aired on the porch in the sun and counted back into the jars, and the jars back onto the high shelf, and the ledger back between them, and the count came out right, every seed accounted for, and she sat in the lamplight one evening with the book open and the columns running down the page and she thought about the account.

Cora came in from the yard. She had been digging with Abel at the east fence, and she came in with the mud on her boots and the color in her face, and she set the shovel by the door and washed at the basin and sat at the table across from June, and the lamp burned between them, and the quiet went on a long time, the good quiet, the quiet of work done, and then Cora said, "Tell me about Dad."

June's hands went still on the page.

"You never told me the whole of it," Cora said. "You told me he went west for the harvests, and the letters thinned, and that's all you ever said. And I've been home a month now, and you've told me about the boy and the doctor and the flood and the seed, and you've never told me about the spring, about how he left, about why he left, about why you didn't go with him. I want the whole of it. I've earned it."

June looked at her daughter across the lamplight. The mud-light of the flood was still in the room, the line on the walls, the smell of the river in the wood, and she thought about the ledger, about how it told the weather and the seed and the dead, and how it had never told the whole of the living, and she thought, it's time to tell it to somebody, or it will never be told.

"When your father came home from the harvest run in '34," she said, "he came home a different man. The wheat had failed him out there, the same as here, and he'd worked for men who paid him in what they owed him and then didn't pay, and he came home broke and ashamed, and he stood in this room and he said, 'We sell out. We take the boys and the girl and we go west together, the whole family, and we start over where the ground's not trying to kill us.'"

Cora said nothing.

"And I told him no."

June let the words sit. She watched her daughter's face and she saw the question in it, the question that had been in Walter's face that night, the question the family had never asked out loud, and she went on.

"I told him I would not sell. He asked me why, and I could not tell him the truth, because the truth was too small and too large at once. I told him the land would come back, and he said it wouldn't, and he was right and I was right, and we stood in this room and we tore each other's hearts out with the quietest words in the house. There's a way to fight that's loud and there's a way to fight that's quiet, and we did the quiet kind, the kind that does the damage you can't see, and when he walked out the door that morning he was a man leaving a wife, not a man leaving a farm, and I stood at the window and watched him go and I told myself the land would hold me the way he couldn't."

"Why?" Cora said. "Why wouldn't you go? He was offering to take you out of it. He was offering the thing you wanted, the thing that would have saved the family, and you said no."

June was quiet. She had thought about the answer to that question every day for two years, in the dust and the ledger and the windbreak rows, and she had never said it aloud, and she looked at the lamp and the dark beyond the window and she said it now, plain.

"Because I was afraid," she said. "Not of the west. Of the leaving. I was born on this land, Cora. My mother planted the lilac bush that's in Mrs. Halloran's yard now, and my father raised the barn that the river just took, and my grandmother was buried in the ground beyond the school, and this house has held my family for fifty years, and I was afraid that if I left it, I would be nobody, that all the years of saving and planting and keeping the ledger would turn out to have been for nothing, and that I would get out there and the west would not want me, and I would have given up my people's land for a stranger's country, and I would have nothing left to show for any of it. So I stayed, and I called it faith, and it was fear dressed up in seed and soil."

Cora looked at her a long time. "And Dad went out there anyway. He gave up the land and the grave and the family to try, and you stayed and called it faith."

"He went because I told him to go," June said. "That's the part I've never been able to live down. He wanted to stay and fight it together, and I told him there was no money to stay, and it was true, and it was also the thing I said to make him go, because I could not watch him stay and starve, and I could not go with him, and so I made him choose between the two things I couldn't give him, and he chose the road, and I let him, and the road has had him ever since."

Cora stood up. She came around the table and she stood over her mother, and June looked up at her, and the lamplight moved on the girl's face, and Cora said, "Did you love him?"

"Yes. I love him now. I've never stopped. That's the stupidest and truest thing about me, that I can hold a grudge against a man and love him at the same time, and the grudge is the part I've been keeping the ledger with, and the love is the part I couldn't write down."

"Did you love the land more?"

June looked at the ledger on the table. The open page, the columns, the small even hand, the weather and the seed and the dead, the whole account of her life written in ink, and she said, "I don't know. I thought I loved the land more. I kept the ledger, and I kept the seed, and I kept the grave, and I let the man go, and I told myself the land was the family, and the land was going to be the thing I held against everything, and then the windbreak came down, and the flood came up, and the land took the barn and the calf and the chickens and nearly took the boy, and it was the ledger and the seed that held, not the land. The land doesn't love you back, Cora. It doesn't hold you. It holds what you put in it, and it gives it back or it doesn't, and it doesn't care which."

Cora was quiet. She looked out the window at the dark, at the drowned county, at the stars coming up over the flood, and she said, "You're telling me you chose wrong."

"I'm telling you I chose," June said. "And a choice is a ledger entry, and you can't take it back, and you can spend the rest of your life adding to it and it still won't balance, and you still have to keep it, because it's the only book you've got. And the only way to make it balance is not to cross it out, Cora. It's to write the other entry, the one that comes after, the one that says the account isn't closed. That's what the blank page is for."

They were quiet a long time. The lamp burned. The house creaked and settled. The river went on going down, past the porch, past the stumps, past the drowned pasture, and June sat with the ledger open and her daughter standing over her and the confession made, the whole of it at last, the spring of 1934 and the quarrel and the letting go, and she felt lighter than she had felt in two years, the way you feel after you have carried something a long way and set it down.

"You could write to him," Cora said. "He's out there, in the pea fields. You could tell him. The letters stopped, but they stopped on both sides. Somebody has to start again."

"I wrote him letters," June said. "Through the winter. I wrote them and I didn't send them. They're in the bottom drawer of the bureau, a whole stack of them, telling him about the boy and the seed and the weather and the windbreak and every day I wanted him back. I didn't send them because I was ashamed, and because I didn't know how to say the part that mattered, and because I was afraid he'd say no."

"He won't say no," Cora said. "I know Dad. He's stubborn and he's proud and he's ashamed, and he's not a man who says no to the one thing he's been waiting for. You send those letters, and you tell him the truth, and you bring him home."

June looked at her daughter. The girl who had been west, who had been marked, who had come home and stood in the flood and worked the sandbag line and laughed in the wreck, stood in the lamplight and looked back at her, and June thought about the seed packet, the zinnias, the one she had folded into Cora's coat a year ago, planted nowhere, carried home unplanted, and she said, "You sound like you've learned something out there, beyond the arithmetic."

"I learned that a woman can be marked and still get up," Cora said. "I learned that the growers' arithmetic is a lie, and that the ledger arithmetic is the truth, and that the truth is that family is the seed, and you don't sell the seed, and you don't let it go west alone out of pride."

The lamp guttered and June reached to trim it, and her hand stopped, and she looked at the flame, and she said, "Your father's letters. The ones he sent from the road, the first year. I kept them all. They're in the same drawer. I used to read them the way I read the almanac, the way you read scripture, the way you read anything you're afraid of losing. And then the letters stopped, and I kept reading the old ones, and the old ones ran out of comfort, and I stopped opening the drawer."

"Until now?"

June was quiet. She looked at the ledger, at the columns, at the small even hand that had kept the family's account for seventeen years, and she said, "Maybe now."

The wind came up the yard, the last of the night wind, and went over the roof, and the house settled, and the two women sat at the table with the lamp between them and the confession made and the letters waiting in the drawer and the family's account, for the first time in two years, opened to the page where it could be balanced.

Cora reached across the table and put her hand on her mother's hand, the hand that held the ledger, and she said, "We'll write him together. Tomorrow. You tell me what to say, and I'll write it so it comes out true."

June looked at her daughter's hand on hers. The mud-light was on the walls, the river smell in the wood, the stars beyond the window, and she turned her hand over and held her daughter's, and she said, "All right."

The lamp burned. The ledger lay open on the table between them, and the page was blank below the last entry, the page for the spring, the page that was waiting to be written, and June looked at the blank page and she thought about the word, the reckoning, and she thought about how the reckoning had not been the storm and had not been the flood and had not been the grave, and she thought about how the reckoning was this, the account finally opened, the words finally spoken, the family finally added up.

And she did not write anything down. She sat with her daughter in the lamplight with the blank page open before them and the whole account of the family lying in the dark of the bottom drawer, waiting to be sent, waiting to be spoken, waiting to be brought home.

Chapter 39: Walter's Letter

CORA — the farm — late April 1936

The letter came in the flood mail. That was what the county called it, the wad of delayed envelopes that arrived at Boise City in a canvas sack two weeks after the water went down, the letters that had been held in rail yards and post offices while the bridges were out, the letters the flood had kept from their people, and Cora was at the feed store when the sack was emptied onto the counter, and the postmaster called the names and the men and women came up one at a time for what the water had held back from them.

It was a slow ceremony, the giving back of the mail. The postmaster turned the envelopes over one by one, reading the names in his dry voice, and the people came up the way you come up to a grave, slow, and the envelopes were water-stained and the ink was run in places, and the women held theirs for a moment before opening them, and the men put theirs in their pockets to read later, and the room was full of the smell of wet paper and the hush of people hearing from the dead and the missing.

Cora had come in for the relief seed. The county agent had sent word that the seed wheat had arrived, the federal grain for the spring planting, and she had walked the four miles in the morning to help carry the sacks, and the sacks were on the wagon outside, and she had stepped into the store to sign for them, and the mail sack had come in behind her on the same wagon, and she had stood at the counter and watched the envelopes go out one by one, and she had thought about the mailbox at the farm, the one that had stood empty through the winter, the one she had watched her mother walk to at dusk in the six weeks without letters.

"Vogel," the postmaster said.

Cora's heart did a thing it had not done in a year. She went up to the counter and took the envelope, and the paper was thin and water-stained at the edge and the handwriting was the handwriting she knew, the blocky upright hand of her father, and she held it and she did not open it in the store, because a letter from a father who had not written in months is not a letter you open in front of the feed store crowd, and because her hands were shaking and she did not want the crowd to see.

She carried it home the four miles, walking, the wagon following slow behind her with the seed wheat, and she did not open it on the road either. She held it in her coat pocket and she could feel it there, the thin fold, the weight of it, the way you can feel a thing that matters without touching it, and the late light came down gold over the mud and the new green trying to come up through the silt, and she passed the schoolhouse and the Carter place and the rise where she had stood and seen the farm, and she came up the lane in the late light and Sammy was at the gate and he saw her face and he said, "What's the matter?"

"Nothing's the matter," she said. "Go get Ma. Tell her to come in."

June was in the kitchen, and Cora set the envelope on the table, on the oilcloth, and the three of them stood around it, and the lamp was not lit yet and the light was going grey, and they looked at the envelope the way you look at a thing you have been afraid of, and then June picked it up and held it a moment and said, "It's his hand."

"Open it," Sammy said.

June did not open it. She handed it to Cora. "You read it. I want you to read it, so I can watch your face while you do."

Cora took the envelope and opened it. The paper was creased from the months of travel, from the storms and the water and the waiting, and she unfolded it, and the handwriting was her father's, and the date on it was February, and she read it aloud, and the room went still.

"Dear June," she read. "This letter will find you, or it won't. I've written it three times and torn it up twice, and this is the third, and I'm sending this one because a man can't keep tearing up his life forever, and I've been living in a pea field in the Imperial Valley since the cotton played out, and I've been thinking about the farm every day, and I've been ashamed, and a man who's ashamed enough stops writing, and that's the truth of why the letters stopped."

She stopped. The light was going. June stood with her hands at her sides, and Cora looked at the page and went on.

"I wrote to you from the road for the first year," she read. "I sent them to the farm, the way I always did, and the letters came back to me, or they never got there, I don't know which, and by the time I figured out the mail was failing in the storms, I'd lost the courage to start again, and the weeks turned to months, and the months to longer, and I told myself you were better off without me, and I was wrong, and I've known I was wrong the whole time, and knowing it didn't make me write."

Cora looked up. June's face was very still, and the grey light was on it, and Cora went on.

"The river's taken the county out there, they say," she read. "The relief man out here told us, and I thought of the farm, and of you standing in the flood water, and of the boy, and of Cora, and I thought, the river can't have my family, it can't, and I've been standing in the fields out here ever since trying to figure out how to get home, and the pea harvest ends next month, and I'll have a little money, and I'm writing to ask, if the house still stands, if you'll have me back. I know I don't deserve it. I know I left. But I've been out here for a year and a half, and I've learned what a man learns out here, which is that a field is a field, but a family is the only crop that doesn't need rain to grow, and I've been a poor farmer of that crop, and I want to be better."

The room was still. The light had gone grey and the lamp was unlit and the three of them stood in the dark around the table with the letter open on the oilcloth between them, and Cora read the last lines, her voice going low.

"I've got my shame," she read. "I'll carry it. But I'll carry it home, if you'll let me. The truck's been out to the relief office with the pea money, and I'll be on the road by the middle of May, and I'll walk the last miles if I have to. Tell the boy I'm coming. Tell Cora I'm sorry I wasn't there to carry her west, the way a father should. Your husband, if you'll still have me. Walter."

The letter lay on the oilcloth. The dark had come into the room and no one had moved to light the lamp, and the three of them stood around the table and no one spoke, and the wind came up the yard and went over the roof, and the house settled, and the family stood in the dark with the letter between them like a live thing, like a root coming out of the ground after a long winter.

Sammy spoke first. His voice was cracked and young and it went high, and he said, "He's coming home. He's coming home. Ma, he's coming home."

June did not answer. She stood very still, and Cora watched her face in the dark and she could not read it, and she said, "Ma."

June reached for the letter and read the closing lines over, her eyes moving down the page in the last of the grey light, and then she said, "He wrote it in February. He's been sitting on this letter since February, not knowing if it would find us, not knowing if we were alive, and the relief man told him about the river, and he thought we might have gone under, and he wrote anyway, on the chance." She said it wonderingly, the way you say a thing you are only just understanding, and her voice was not angry and not sad, it was something else, something opened.

Cora looked at the date on the letter, the blocky February hand, and she thought about her own February, about the hospital tent and the stopped letters and the silence, and she thought about how the two of them had been writing into the dark at the same time, her father in his pea field and her mother at the mailbox, and how neither had known the other was writing, and she said, "He never stopped. He was writing the whole time. The letters just never made it through the storms, and he thought you'd stopped writing back."

"That's the arithmetic," June said. "Two people, both writing, both thinking the other has gone quiet, and the weather in between eating the mail. The whole family's been writing letters for a year and a half, Cora. You, me, him, the boy — and the letters have been going under in the storms, and we've all been standing at our own mailboxes thinking we were the only one still holding a pen."

The words went into the room and the dark held them, and Cora stood by the table and she thought about her own letters, the ones she had sent from the valley, the lies about the food, the letters that had stopped during the strike, the letters she had not written from the hospital tent because she had not wanted her mother to see the bruises, and she thought about how the family's letters were the roots, and how the roots had been going out in every direction, and how the storms had been burying them all, and how they had all been waiting on the other to write first.

She lit the lamp. The light came up over the table, over the letter, over her mother's face, and June's face was wet, and she was not trying to hide it, and she stood with her hand on the letter and the tears going down her face and she said, "Light the fire. We're going to write him back tonight, and we're going to send it in the morning, and we're going to tell him the house held, and the seed held, and the boy lived, and Cora's home, and the windbreak stumps are still there and we're going to plant them again, and he's got a family waiting, and the family's the only crop that doesn't need rain."

They wrote it together at the kitchen table, the four of them, and it was the first letter the family had written as a family, the paper going from hand to hand, each one adding a line, Sammy's in the laboring hand of a boy, June's in the small even hand of the ledger, Cora's in the fast sharp hand of the road, and the letter said the house held, and the seed held, and the boy lived, and Cora was home, and the windbreak was going to be planted again, and the relief seed had come, and the green was coming up through the mud, and the family was whole and waiting, and Walter was to come home to it, and the last line was Cora's, and she wrote it slow, the way you write a thing you have been afraid to say: "The flood took the barn and the fence and the calf, and it didn't take us. The house held and the seed held and we are holding. Come home to it."

The lamp burned. The fire took in the stove. The wind went over the roof, and the family sat down at the table with the letter open between them and the blank paper waiting, and outside, beyond the lamplight, the flood water was going down to the river, and the river was going back to its bed, and the land was wet and dark and ready, and somewhere out on the road west, a man in a pea field was loading his truck, and the roots were going home.

Chapter 40: The Family Reconciled

JUNE — the farm — May 1936

The first of May came in green. That was the thing that took June by surprise, after all the years of brown, the green. It came up out of the mud of the flood the way it had been waiting under the water all along, the pasture greening, the weeds running up the fence lines, the garden rows she had put in with the relief seed standing in their straight dark lines, and the wheat the county agent had sent showing green on the north field, the first green she had seen on that field in three years.

She walked the fields at evening the way she had walked them all her life, the ledger under her arm, and she counted. The garden, the wheat, the pasture, the new windbreak rows where she had set the survivors from the cellar cans, the cottonwood and the willow and the Russian olive, small and brave in the wet ground. She counted the way she counted everything, one at a time, and the count came out on the side of the living, and she stood at the fence in the green light and let herself feel it, the way you let yourself feel the sun after a long illness.

The house was whole. The mud line was still on the walls, washed pale now, a watermark the river had left like a signature, and June had decided to leave it, to let it fade on its own, the way a scar fades, because to scrub it away would be to pretend the water had not come, and the water had come, and the house had held, and the line was the evidence.

Cora came out of the house with the plate of bread and the butter, and Sammy came out behind her with the milk, and they set the table on the porch, the three of them, and they ate the supper with the green coming up around them and the light going long and gold, and they did not talk about the letter, about the reply that had gone out on the morning mail to the Imperial Valley, about the waiting that had begun. The letter was on the road. That was all. The waiting was the work now, the way the sandbagging had been the work, and they did it together, the way they had done everything since the water came.

June did not tell them, at supper, what she had decided. She carried it the way she had carried the ledger through the flood, close, and she waited until the dishes were done and the lamp was lit and the three of them sat at the table, and then she went to the shelf and she took down the ledger, the old repurposed composition book, the one begun in 1919, and she brought it to the table and set it down in the lamplight, and she said, "I want to read you something."

Cora and Sammy looked at her. She opened the book. The pages were the color of old paper, the small even hand running down them, the weather column and the seed column and the years, and she found the first page, and she read it aloud.

"1919," she read. "We planted the north quarter for the first time. The wheat came up the second week of May. I saved the seed heads off the best row and wrapped them in a flour sack. Walter says a man who saves seed is a man who trusts the spring."

She turned the pages. She read the years, one at a time, her voice low and even, the way she had read the almanac to a sick boy in the dark, the way you read scripture in a plain church, without music.

"1921. The well came in at a hundred and twenty feet. The water's good. We'll never be rich, but we'll never be dry." "1923. Ezra was born today. The corn's in and the sky's clear and I've never been so tired." "1926. The cistern cracked in the freeze. Walter fixed it in a day." "1929. The bank took the east forty from the Mitchells. It's a hard year to be a farmer and a worse year to be a neighbor." "1931. The wind's been up a week. We held the topsoil on the west field with the disked furrows. It's not enough. It's never enough."

She read the entries the way she had lived them, one at a time, and the years came up out of the pages the way the green was coming up out of the mud, and Cora heard her own childhood in them, the years before the dust, the years of the good rains and the full cellar, and she saw her mother's hand moving down the columns and she understood, for the first time, what the book was. It was not a record. It was a holding. Her mother had held the family up with this book the way the sandbags had held the house, one entry at a time, one season at a time, one terrible year at a time, and she had never let go, and the book was the reason they were still here.

There were entries she read that Cora had never seen. "1925. Cora was born today. The wheat's in the shock and the sky's clear and she's got her father's eyes." "1928. Cora took her first steps on the porch. She'll be a runner." "1930. Sammy was born today. The wind came up the same hour, and I held him against it, and I told him, you're a Vogel, you're built for weather." June read them and her voice did not change, but Cora felt the years close around her, the way the flood water had closed around the house, and she sat at the table with the ledger open between them and she was a girl again, held in her mother's account.

She came to the dry years. The pages went on, the small even hand, and the entries changed, the weather column filling with the same word, dry, dry, dry, and she read them, the years of watching the land fail, and she did not hurry them, and Cora sat with her hands folded on the table and Sammy sat with his chin on his hands and they listened, the way you listen to a thing that has been kept from you and given back.

"1933. The first storm buried the fence line. We dug it out twice and gave up." "1934. Ezra took the fever. The doctor came too late. We buried him on a Sunday in the rain. I have planted the extra tree. I will not leave this land while I live." "April 14, 1935. The third burial. We are still here."

The room was still. The lamp burned. The green dark pressed at the windows and the wind went over the roof and the house settled, and June turned the pages to the end, to the entries for the year, and she read them, the last ones: "Feb 1936 — Sammy lived." "March 1936 — it's over." "April 1936 — the river took the barn and the calf and the chickens, and the house held, and the seed held, and the family held."

She closed the ledger. She set her hand on it, the way she always did, the way you touch a thing that has carried you, and she looked at her children across the lamplight, at the girl who had come home and the boy who had lived, and she said, "I've kept this book for seventeen years. I've kept it the way other women keep their Bibles. I've kept it because I thought it was the truth of this family, the account of everything we planted and lost and saved, and I've been wrong about what it is."

Cora said, "Wrong how?"

"It's not the truth," June said. "It's the weather and the seed and the dead. It's the account of the land, and the land is not the whole of the family. The family is the table, and the table isn't in the ledger. Your father isn't in the ledger. The letters that went under in the storms aren't in it. The year and a half he spent in a pea field writing to us while we didn't know, that's not in it. I've been keeping the account of the farm and calling it the account of the family, and the flood taught me the difference. The land doesn't hold you, Cora. It holds what you put in it. The ledger held the seed and the weather and the dead, and it never once held the man I sent west."

She was quiet a moment. The lamp flame stood still.

"The reckoning," she said, and the word went into the room, and Cora felt it, the word that had come to her in the storm and in the water and in the long winter, the word that had been the preacher's and the grower's and the land's, and here it was in her mother's plain voice, in the family's own voice, at the family's own table, and she understood that the word had been waiting for this room all along.

"The reckoning isn't the storm," June said. "It isn't the flood and it isn't the dry years and it isn't the grave. The preachers said it was punishment, and the growers said it was the price of standing up, and the land said it was the sum of the weather. It's none of those. It's the adding up, that's all it is. It's the column that has to be balanced, and a column can't be balanced by pretending the other entries don't exist. It has to be added honestly, the whole of it, the seed and the weather and the dead and the living, the letters that went under and the ones that got through, the man I sent west and the girl who came home and the boy who lived, and when you add it all honestly, it comes out to this: we are still here. The house held. The seed held. And the family is the family, and it was never the land."

She looked at Cora. "You went west with a seed packet and you brought it home unplanted, and that was the truth of you, that you were keeping the seed for home. And I kept the ledger and I let the ledger be my grave and my church and my husband's replacement, and I was wrong, and I'm telling you I was wrong, because you're old enough to hear it and because you're the one who'll have to keep it from here."

Cora was quiet a long moment. The lamp burned. The green dark pressed at the windows, and she heard in her head the other voice, the one from the west, the one that had taught her the grower's arithmetic, the voice that had said, tenderness, precision, unillusioned, the voice of a man who had also been marked, and she said, "I learned something out there, Ma, about a different kind of ledger. A man taught me to read a pay stub, and the first thing he taught me was that the growers' arithmetic was rigged, that they weighed the cotton wet and counted the tare twice, and that the books never told the truth because the books were kept by the men who held the scales. And he taught me that the only way to keep a book honest is to keep it yourself, with your own hand, and to never let the men with the scales keep it for you. And I thought of you, keeping this book for seventeen years with your own hand, and I understood that you were doing the same thing he was, keeping the honest book against the rigged one, and that the ledger is not my grave and not my church, it's my hand on the scale."

June looked at her daughter, and the lamplight moved on the girl's face, and she said, "Then you know why I kept it. And you know why I have to hand it to you."

"No," Cora said. "Not yet. You keep it, Ma. You're not done keeping it. And I'll learn it, and I'll keep it with you, and then I'll keep it after. But the hand on the scale has to be both of us now, because the spring is coming and the planting is coming and Dad is coming, and the ledger's got room for all of it."

She looked at Cora. "You went west with a seed packet and you brought it home unplanted, and that was the truth of you, that you were keeping the seed for home. And I kept the ledger and I let the ledger be my grave and my church and my husband's replacement, and I was wrong, and I'm telling you I was wrong, because you're old enough to hear it and because you're the one who'll have to keep it from here."

June looked at her a long time. Then she turned the ledger to the back page, the blank page, the page that was waiting, and she dipped the pen and she held it over the paper, and she said, "This is the first entry in the other book. The one that holds the table."

And she wrote, in her small even hand, the hand that had kept the account for seventeen years, at the top of the blank page, not a date and not a weather reading and not a seed count, but the thing the family had been afraid to say and had finally learned to say:

"May 1936. The river took the barn and gave us the spring. The family is whole. Walter is coming home. The reckoning is kept."

She set the pen down. The word lay on the page, in her hand, in the family's own hand now, not the preacher's, not the grower's, not the land's — the family's, spoken at the family's table in the family's own voice, and the word meant what they meant by it now, the sum of the choices, the account honestly added, the family's account settled and opened at the same time, settled because it had been added honestly and opened because the spring was waiting.

She passed the ledger to Cora. Cora looked at the page, at her mother's hand, at the word, and she took the pen and she wrote her own line under it, her fast sharp hand, the hand of the road: "I carried the seed packet west and back. We plant it together this spring. The reckoning kept us, and we are going to keep each other."

She passed the ledger to Sammy. He looked at it a long moment, the word and the lines, and he took the pen, the way a boy takes a heavy tool, and he wrote, in the laboring hand of a boy who had learned to breathe again: "I kept the seed jar through the flood. Dad is coming home."

The three of them looked at the page. The lamp burned. The green dark pressed at the windows, and beyond the windows the green was coming up through the mud, the wheat and the garden and the pasture and the new windbreak rows, and June took the ledger and closed it and set it on the shelf between the canning jars, where it had always sat, and it was the same book and it was a different book, and she stood with her hand on it a moment and then she turned to the table, and the forgiveness went around the table the way the bread had gone, passed by hand, taken by hand, and the family sat down to the rest of the evening whole, the account opened, the spring waiting, the letter on the road, and the green coming up in the dark outside the lamp.

Chapter 41: The Seed Ledger Opened

JUNE — the farm — May 1936

The ledger lay open on the kitchen table and the May air came through the screen behind it, clean and wet-smelling, the way it had not come through that screen in three years. June Vogel set the iron on the cold stove and wiped her hands down her apron, and she stood a long moment looking at the book before she touched it, the way she had learned to look at a thing before she put her hand to it, in case it was the last time it would be whole.

The flood had kissed it. A pale line ran across the back cover where the water had reached through the oilcloth, and the pages had dried stiff and crimped at the edges, but the ink had held. Every column, every year. 1919 to 1936, a girl's round hand running into a woman's plain one. She turned the pages with two fingers, careful as a nurse turning a patient, and the smell of the wet pages came up to her, and under it the smell of the dry years, the grit that had been ground into the paper and would never come out.

The last entries read like a chart at a sickbed. April 14, 1935 — the third burial. We are still here. Then the weather column through the summer and the winter. February — Sammy lived. And after that the storm month and the flood month, and nothing. The spring had moved faster than her hand.

"Are you going to look at it all day," Cora said from the door, "or are you going to open it?"

June closed the book and laid her palm flat on the cover, on the water line that ran under her hand like a horizon. "I'm counting," she said. "Counting's not looking."

Outside, the seed corn stood in jars on the porch where she had set them in the sun to dry — the jars saved through the flood, the flood line still faint on the glass — and the relief seed from the county agent leaned in a tow sack against the cistern, and the swap seed from the women's meeting waited in the wagon box in twists of newspaper. She counted what she had and wrote it in her head before she wrote it in the book. Arithmetic. It was the only prayer that had ever answered her.

The county agent had come out the week before with the shelterbelt box and the relief grain, and he had stood at the fence looking at the river pasture and said the water table was coming up, that the wells were hanging their water higher than they had in four years. June had nodded and said nothing. The agent did not know the land the way the ledger did. The ledger had kept its own counsel since 1919, and it said nothing about water tables. It said: plant the corn, save the beans, trust the ground or not — the ground is all there is.

They planted the last week of May.
