Cora took the far rows and Sammy the middle, and June walked behind them with the bag of corn at her hip, dropping the kernels two and three to a hill the way her mother had taught her and her mother's mother before that. The soil was black where it had been gray. It had taken the rain and kept it, and it turned under the plow in long dark ribbons that smelled like the bottom of a well.

Sammy had buried his .22 in the shed loft after the flood — no one had asked him to; he had just done it one night and told no one, but the shed loft held things the way the ledger held them, and she had found the butt of it wrapped in a flour sack under the eaves when she went up for the seed jars. She left it where it lay. A boy who could bury his gun when the water rose was a boy who could be trusted with his hands. He worked the rows barefoot and bent, and every few rods he straightened and looked at the sky as if he expected it to take everything back.

"Eyes on the row," June said.

"Yes'm."

"The sky's not going anywhere."

"It moved last time."

She had no answer to that, so she dropped another hill of corn and moved on.

Cora worked the way she did everything — fast, and a little angry, and thorough. Her hands had the scar of the hospital tent on one knuckle and the muscle the cotton had put on her shoulders, and she set the seed in the ground as if she were daring it not to come up. June watched her from two rows over and said nothing, because there were no words yet for the thing she felt, watching her daughter kneel in the black soil in the new morning.

At noon they ate at the edge of the field, bread and cold potatoes and water from the cistern, and the wind came up mild and carried the smell of the wet furrows, and Cora said, "The ledger. You haven't made the entry."

"It's not the entry's time. The entry comes after the planting, when the rows are in and the rain's had its say."

"You and that book."

"The book keeps what the dust forgets," June said. "That's all it's for."

Cora looked at her mother across the bread and the potatoes, and for a moment her face did the thing it did now sometimes, the west coming up in it like a watermark — the camps, the scale, the boot, the tent. Then she passed the water jar and said, "Then the dust has a lot to be told."

The rows went in by supper. The evenings were long now, the light going gold and staying, and the swallows had come back to the barn loft where the flood had not reached, and the cottonwoods that had lived through the flood stood at the north fence putting on leaf in a way that made June stop in the middle of the yard and just look at them — the double row, and over it the one tree she had planted for Ezra, the one she never explained, the one that had come through the axe year and the flood year and was leafing now like a hand opening.

That evening, when the light had gone long and yellow and the birds were loud, June carried the ledger to the table and opened it to the new page. She tested the pen. The good ink had gone out of the house in the flood and the new was water-cut, thin and pale, but it would hold. She wrote in her plain hand:

May 1936 — the land is willing again.

Then the weather column — the rains of April, the warm May, the wind that had come up mild instead of mean. The seed column — the corn from the jars, the relief wheat, the swap beans, the twist of morning-glory seed that had ridden home in Cora's coat and would be planted by the door. She wrote the hills to a row and the rows to a field, as if they were kin, because in her keeping they were.

Cora came in from the porch with the last of the jar water and set it on the stove, and she stood behind her mother's chair and read the page over her shoulder. She was quiet a long time.

"That's the first page since the flood," she said.

"It's the first page since the spring," June said.

"No. It's the first page that's about something coming instead of something gone."

June looked at her daughter. Cora had a smudge of black soil across her forehead and her sleeves rolled past her elbows, and she smelled of sweat and wet earth, and the look she gave the ledger was the look of a woman reading her own future and finding it plain enough to live in. June thought of the day the book would pass to another hand — Cora's, maybe, or Sammy's, or a hand not yet born — and of the heirloom the dust had made of it, and she did not say it aloud, because some things are only true if you keep them in the columns.

"I'm going to bring the chickens down to the new ground," Cora said, and went out.

June sat a while in the quiet kitchen. The window was up and the air came in with the birdsong and the smell of turned earth, and the ledger lay open on the table like a door left ajar. Outside, the last light lay flat across the field where the rows ran straight and black and full of seed. She went to the porch and stood at the rail. The wind came up from the river, and it was wet, and it carried nothing at all — no grit, no sting, no weight of the dry years riding on its back. It was just wind, and under the wind the field lay quiet and dark and patient, and somewhere along the far row the last bird called once and stopped, and June stood at the rail in the dark listening to the wind that had nothing in it to carry.

Chapter 42: The First Rain

CORA — the farm — May 1936

Cora heard it first. She was at the well with the bucket rope burning her palms, the way it always did, and the sky to the west was doing something she had learned to read in the camps and had not forgotten: the color was wrong, a deep bruised gray the color of the inside of the storm years, and her hands went cold on the rope before her mind had caught up. Then the smell came, and she understood.

It was the smell of rain on dust. Wet earth, the ground giving up its one perfume. She had smelled it in the valley after the first sprinkle, had watched the old men of the camp stand still and breathe it in like a man at the rail of a boat coming in to shore. But this was her own dirt. This was the Vogel ground, her mother's rows, her mother's ledger's country, and the rain was coming down on it.

She dropped the bucket and ran. Not for the house — the thunder was still far, the drops still a rumor in the air — she ran for the field, past the porch where June stood at the rail with her hand shading her eyes, past Sammy whooping at the barn door, out onto the black rows she had helped set the week before, and she stood in the middle of the turned earth and turned her face up.

The first drops were huge and separate and warm, and they struck the dust in front of her like coins and made small craters, and the dust jumped. Then the rain came down in a sheet, the whole sky letting go at once, and it was not like the hammering rains of April that had turned the river and frightened the house. This rain was different. It had no anger in it. It stood in the air like a curtain and came down steady and stayed.

Cora stood in it until she was soaked through. She had not been rained on — truly rained on, the way she had been as a child, before the dust came — since she could remember. The water ran off her hair and down her collar and filled her shoes, and she opened her mouth and drank a little of it, and she laughed, and then she stood still and let the storm years have their way with her memory.

She remembered the day they had buried the third planting, the day the sky had gone brown at noon and her mother had hung wet cloths at the doors and windows and the house had breathed grit anyway, and Sammy had cried into the flour sack her mother tied over his face, and she had watched the gray light come under the door like a tide. She remembered the weigh scale in the valley and the weighmaster's thumb and the water they had to pay for that came from a barrel that had scum on it. She remembered the boot, and the dark, and Miguel's face above her in the lantern light, and the tent, and Nurse O'Dell's hands on her ribs. She remembered the smell of the dust in every one of those places, the way it got into everything, the way it lay on a person like a second skin.

And now the rain was washing it. She could smell the ground give up the dust to the water, the way a wound gives up a splinter. The field drank, and the furrows filled, and the little green spears of the corn that had just broken the surface stood up in rows of bright green in the gray rain, and she thought: my mother's rows. My mother's corn. I helped put it in the ground.

June had come down off the porch and stood at the edge of the field with her arms crossed, and she was not getting wet the way Cora was — she stood under the rain as if she had expected it, as if the ledger had promised it. Cora walked to her, dripping, and they stood together at the edge of the field and watched the rain walk across the pasture.

"Wash day," June said.

"Wash day," Cora said.

"You're soaked to the bone."

"I wanted to be."

June looked at her a long moment, the way she looked at the columns of the ledger when they added up right. "Well," she said. "You're the prodigal." It was the first time she had ever said a thing like that out loud, and Cora understood it was a blessing, of a kind, from a woman who blessed in arithmetic.

The rain went on into the afternoon and the evening and turned the yard to mud and the pasture to a dark mirror, and the cistern filled and ran over, and June stood at the kitchen window watching the water pour off the eaves, and she said, more to the ledger than to Cora, "It's the one that stays. I can feel it in the ground." The rain drummed on the tin roof all night, a sound Cora had almost forgotten how to hear without fear, and she lay in her old bed listening to it and remembering, and the memory of the storm years came up out of her like grit out of a well.

In the night she woke once and stood at her window. The rain had eased to a fine steady fall, and the yard lay dark and glistening, and the new corn stood in rows she could just see by the light of the kitchen lamp her mother had left burning at the window — burning, she understood, so that someone standing at a window in the dark could see the rows were still there. The rain was falling straight and slow now, the way rain falls when the sky has finished arguing with itself, and under it the field drank, and the dust went down into the ground where it could not hurt anything anymore, and Cora stood at the glass with her hand flat on the cold pane and let herself be rained on through the window, and said the words she had not known she had, a kind of prayer with no church in it, made of wet earth and new corn and a light left burning.

In the morning the rain was still falling, gentle and even, and the yard was a lake and the gutters ran and the whole farm smelled of green, and Cora came out onto the porch barefoot and stood on the wet boards and looked at the sky, which was low and soft and gray and full of the good thing, and she thought of the camps, of Rosa, of Lula, of the grower's scale and the boot and the tent, and she let them go, one by one, the way the water was letting go of the eaves — and the last of the storm years went down into the ground with the rain, and the first true rain of the good year fell on the Vogel farm, and it stayed.

Chapter 43: Green on the Field

JUNE — the farm — June 1936

The green came up like a slow tide. June walked the fields each evening after the dishes were done, and every evening there was more of it — the wheat standing in rows so straight the eye could run a furrow to the fence, the garden making its own little map at the corner of the house, the pasture putting on a fuzz of new grass that she could not help touching with the flat of her hand, the way she had once touched the children's heads to see if they were feverish. She walked and she counted. The count was not arithmetic. It was the same prayer, with the numbers left out.

She measured the windbreak's survivors against the ledger's back page, where she had recorded their heights the spring she planted them, and then again the fall the axe came. The old cottonwoods that had stood had put on rings of new leaf, and the willows had gone up overnight it seemed, and the Russian olives stood silver in the evening light like a row of old women in shawls. The row was broken where Abel Halloran's axe had cut it, but it was not dead. Nothing that had lived through the flood was going to die of the sun.

The first letter had come from Walter in April, out of the wad of flood-delayed mail, and Cora had read it aloud at the table, and June had listened with her hands folded and had not cried, though the words were plain enough. He was in the Imperial Valley pea fields. His letters had gone astray in the storms. He was ashamed. He would come home if they would have him. June had said, into the quiet of the kitchen, "The mail is a slow river," and had gone on with the dishes, and had not written back, and had not not written back. The letter lay in the ledger now, pressed between the pages, a root going home that had not yet arrived.

Mrs. Halloran came to the fence on a Wednesday evening in June. June was at the far end of the garden row, and she saw the woman's figure come up the lane and stand at the north fence where the windbreak's stumps were, and she straightened and wiped her hands and walked down to meet her. It had been four months since the seed swap, since the frost-nipped truce, since the coffee can of apology the Halloran woman had set on June's doorstep in the winter without a word. Four months of the fence between them, and the fence had held, and now here was Mrs. Halloran on the Vogel side of it, holding a coffee can against her apron with both hands.

"June," she said.

"Edna."

The wind came up mild between them. Mrs. Halloran was thinner than she had been, and there was a gray in her hair that had not been there in the fall, and she looked at the stumps of the windbreak as if she could not look at June.

"I brought you something," she said. She held out the coffee can, and in it, standing in a little mud of black dirt, was a cutting of the lilac — rooted, leafed, a small green thing with a purpose. The lilac from her grandmother's yard. The one she had given June at the seed swap, traded for the last of June's saved corn. The one she had gone on watering all winter, and that had struck root.

"It's the same one," June said.

"It's the same one. I kept it back. I watered it all winter, I don't know why — I kept thinking of your corn, how you gave me your last of it. And I thought, if it roots, I'll bring it. And it rooted."

June looked at the small green thing in the coffee can, and she looked at the stumps of the windbreak, and she looked at Edna Halloran's hands, which had loaded the last greenwood into the wagon in the dark — the hands that had chosen her children's warmth over June's trees, and lied about it, and apologized in a coffee can in the winter, and were holding out now, in the good light of June, the rooted cutting of the lilac.

"It was not the wood they took," June said. It was the first time she had said it aloud, the words she had carried all winter like a seed in a pocket. "It was the rows. It was standing together."

Mrs. Halloran's face did something then — the thing a face does when a weight it has been carrying is finally named. "I know," she said. "Abel knows. He cut every one of them in his sleep. He dreams about it. He says he'd go and plant them back if he knew where the wood went, but the wood went into our stove, June. Into our children. I don't know how to un-burn it."

June took the coffee can. She held it up into the light and turned it, and the small green leaves shone, and she said, "You plant a seed and the land will answer." She said it quietly, the way she said things she had learned to be true. "You can't un-burn the wood. But the lilac roots. And the windbreak goes back in the ground this month. The county agent brought the shelterbelt cottonwoods. Cora's already picked her row."

Mrs. Halloran looked at her, and the fence between them seemed to give a little, the way a fence gives when the earth settles under it. "Can I help?" she said. "With the planting. Abel would come. He'd be the one to carry the water."

"The planting's Cora's," June said. "But there'll be water to carry before it's done. The water's always the last thing to be carried."

They stood a while at the fence with the coffee can between them, the rooted lilac in it, and the evening came on mild and green-smelling, and June said, "Edna. The seed swap. You never told me how the morning-glories did."

Mrs. Halloran laughed, a small dry sound like a bird clearing its throat. "They came up like a miracle. The whole side of the porch. I told Abel they were your doing, and he said, 'She'd plant a fence post and it would come up bearing wire.'"

"Fence posts don't come up."

"They will now, the way the ground's acting."

They laughed, both of them, and it was the first time the two women had laughed together since before the axe, and June held the coffee can up and said, "I'll set this by the door where I can see it. It'll do better than the morning-glories, I think. A lilac that's been through the winter knows what it's for."

She carried the coffee can up to the house and set it on the porch rail in the evening light, and she stood and looked at the green coming up all over her fields, the wheat and the corn and the grass and the windbreak's new leaf, and she thought of the ledger's columns filling themselves in, one green row at a time, and she said to the green: there it is. That's the answer. The land answering the ledger. The green on the field.

Chapter 44: The Windbreak Replanted

CORA — the farm — June 1936

The county agent's box of shelterbelt cottonwoods came up the lane in the back of a WPA truck on a Saturday, and the whole family came out to meet it, because there had not been a delivery of growing things to the Vogel place since before the flood, and a truck full of trees was the next best thing to a wagon full of rain. The agent himself had driven it, a thin young man with a notebook, and he stood at the tailgate and read off the federal forms the way a man reads scripture he has not quite memorized, and Cora stood with her arms crossed and let him talk.

The Prairie States Forestry Project. The windbreak program. One hundred and sixty million trees up and down the plains, the agent said, from the Canadian line to the Panhandle, the whole country planting itself against the wind. He said it with the pride of a man reading his own name in the paper. Cora looked at the box of seedlings — a hundred or more, bundled, roots wrapped in wet burlap, leaves the pale green of things just born — and she thought of the word that had followed her west and come home with her, the word that sat in her chest like a splinter under a bandage, and she thought: the whole country's reckoning. The whole country planting itself against the wind.

The agent stayed for dinner. June set an extra plate without being asked, and they ate at the table with the box of trees on the porch and the window up, and the agent told them about the trees — how the cottonwood would grow a foot a year, how the willow would take hold in the wet ground by the river, how the Russian olive would stand the dust when the other trees would not, how the rows were meant to break the wind before it reached the house and the field. Cora listened, and she remembered the old windbreak, the double row her mother had planted with Ezra's tree in it, and the night the axe had come, and she did not say anything.

The planting was set for Monday. Cora took the north quarter, where the stumps were, where the old rows had run. Sammy took the east line. June and the agent marked the rows with string, and Abel Halloran came up the lane mid-morning with his own shovel over his shoulder, and he stood at the edge of the field and said, "I carried the water for the old ones. I'll carry it for the new." He did not look at Cora. He did not look at the stumps. He walked to the water barrel and filled the first pail and carried it to the row Cora was opening, and he set it down and went back for another, and no one said anything, and the work began.

Cora planted her row with her hands, the way her mother had taught her — a hole deep enough for the roots to spread, the tree set straight, the dirt packed firm around the collar, a little basin of earth around the base to hold the water. She had done it for the old windbreak when she was a girl, following her mother down the row with the pail, and the muscle memory came back like a song she had not known she remembered. She planted the cottonwoods first, then the willows, then the Russian olives, one and then another down the string line, and the box of trees went down row by row, and the windbreak grew out of the ground again out of the back of a WPA truck, and the federal program, so far away in Washington, had put its hand on the north quarter of the Vogel farm.

At noon they stopped and ate on the porch, and Abel Halloran sat at the end of the table farthest from June and did not speak except to say the beans were good, and Sammy talked to the agent about the trees — how fast they would grow, whether a man could climb them, whether they would hold a rope swing, whether the wind would ever knock them down. And then, in the afternoon, Cora planted the last one.

The last one was the extra tree. The one for Ezra. The old one had been a cottonwood, the one her mother had planted alone, the one she had never explained, and it had come through the axe year and the flood year and stood now at the north end of the row leafing like a hand opening. Cora had watched it all her life without knowing what it was, and then she had learned, the way children learn such things, by being old enough to understand what was not said. She set the new cottonwood in the ground a little apart from the row, where the old one stood, so that there would be two trees for Ezra now, the old and the new, the tree that had lived and the tree that would live.

She packed the dirt around its collar and made the little basin and fetched the water herself, and she poured it slow around the base and watched it take, and she sat back on her heels in the dust of the new row and looked at the whole north quarter — the line of new trees running straight and green, the old survivors standing at intervals, the extra tree for Ezra at the end, the stumps of the fallen ones going gray under the new grass — and she said, to the tree, "You're the one I'll water. You're my row."

Her mother came up behind her and stood a moment looking at the new cottonwood. "The extra tree," June said.

"I know what it is," Cora said.

June was quiet a long time. "You never asked," she said. "All those years. You never asked."

"I didn't have to. It was the tree you planted alone, and you never explained it, and that was the explanation."

June knelt in the dirt beside her daughter, and she put her hand on the little tree, on the pale new bark, and she said, "He would have been thirteen this year. I plant them so there's something to outlive me. Something that stands when I'm not standing."

"I know," Cora said. "You planted the row so there'd be something standing together." She looked at her mother, and the west was in her voice for a moment, the camps, the scale, the boot, the tent — and then it was not. "I'm watering it every day. It's the one I'll carry water to."

June looked at her daughter, and her face did the thing it did now when the girls' mother and the ledger woman met in the same expression, and she said, "That's a good row, Cora. You set them straight. The windbreak's a family that stands together, and this one's got your hand on it."

The two of them knelt in the dust of the new row, mother and daughter, the new cottonwood between them, and behind them the north quarter ran full of the new green trees, and the old survivors stood over them, and the WPA truck had gone down the lane and taken the road back to Boise City, and the sun went down behind the row and laid the shadows long and straight across the new grass, and the wind came up from the river and took the tops of the new trees and moved them, and they did not break, and Cora watched the windbreak hold the wind the way it was meant to hold it, and she thought: it stands. The whole green row of them, standing.

Chapter 45: Cora's Choice

CORA — the farm — July 1936

Cora made the choice the way she made most of her choices — suddenly, and with her hands, and then she had to spend the rest of the day being sure of it, the way a woman spends a day being sure of a coin she has flipped. It was a Sunday in July, the hottest Sunday of the year, and the family had eaten dinner early because the heat lay on the farm like a hand, and June had gone out to the fields to walk the rows as she walked them every evening now, and Sammy had gone to the river, and Cora had sat on the porch in the shade with her chin on her knees and watched the heat shiver over the pasture, and she had thought about the road.

The road was still in her. It would always be in her. The jukebox song played in her head at odd hours — the song the world plays, the nickel song, the one she had heard in the café on Highway 66 with Lula and Rosa and the whole moving river of people — and she could still smell the gas stations and the tourist cabins and the roadside camps, could still feel the flatbed truck's racket in her teeth, could still see the land so flat the horizon was a rumor. The west had put its hand on her and it had not let go. She knew that. She could hear the highway the way some people hear music they cannot name.

And yet. She looked down at her own hands, at the scar on her knuckle from the hospital tent, at the calluses the cotton had put on her palms and the rows had kept there, and she thought about what the road had cost her and what it had given her, and she thought about her mother walking the rows in the heat, counting, and she thought about the ledger open on the table, and she made the choice.

She found June at the far end of the corn field, standing at the edge of the rows with her hand shading her eyes, doing what she did every evening — reading the field the way she read the ledger, one green column at a time. Cora walked down the furrow to her, and June heard her coming and did not turn.

"The corn's up to my knee," June said.

"It's up to your knee," Cora said. "It was up to Sammy's boot yesterday. It grows at night, I think. When no one's looking."

"Everything good grows at night. It's the bad that grows in the day, in front of everybody."

Cora stood beside her mother at the edge of the field, and the heat lay on them, and she said, "I'm staying."

June turned then, and looked at her. The look was long, and it was the look of a woman reading a column she had been afraid to read, and her hands, which had been shading her eyes, came down to her sides, and she said, "Staying how?"

"Staying. Here. The farm. The ledger, when it's time. The school when the term begins — Mr. Putnam's asked me, and I told him I'd think on it, and I've thought on it." She said it fast, the way she did the things she was afraid of, and then she said it again, slower, the way she did the things she meant. "I'm staying, Mama. The road still calls — I hear it. I'll always hear it. But I'm not going."

June was quiet a long time. The wind came up the field, and the corn rustled, and a bird called once and stopped, and June said, "You've been west. You know what the road is. If you stay because you think it's your duty, that's a grave, and I won't have you in a grave of your own digging." She said it hard, the way she said the hard things, and then her voice came down. "If you stay because you want to, that's a different thing."

"I want to," Cora said. "I've wanted to since the flood, I think. Since I came over the rise and saw the house smaller than memory and the windbreak stumps and you at the well. I thought I'd come home to be sure, and then I'd leave again, and the sureness came and it wasn't the road." She looked at her mother. "Home's a verb now. I know how to do it. That's not duty, Mama. That's the choice."

June nodded. She stood very still at the edge of the corn field, and then she did a thing she almost never did: she put her arms around her daughter, in the open field, in the heat, in the sight of the green corn and the sky, and she held her, and the two of them stood together at the edge of the rows, mother and daughter, and neither of them spoke for a long while.

Then June stepped back and wiped her face with the back of her hand and said, "Well. Then there's something I've been keeping for you."

They walked back to the house together, and June went to her room and came back with a folded seed packet, and she held it out to Cora, and Cora looked at it, and her breath went out of her. It was the seed packet. The one her mother had given her the morning she left for the west, the folded paper with the seeds in it — "plant them where you land, so there's a piece of home" — and she had hidden it in her coat, and carried it across three states and a hundred camps, and never planted it, and brought it home again, unplanted, and had not known what to do with it, and had not known how to give it back.

"You kept it," Cora said.

"I kept it," June said. "It rode to California and it rode home. It's been west and it's been east, and it's never been in the ground. I thought maybe that's what it was waiting for — to be planted by the girl who carried it, in the ground she came home to."

Cora took the packet and turned it over in her hands. The paper was soft from the years of her coat, the fold creased deep and pale. She opened it, and the seeds were still there — small and brown and patient, the way seeds are, the way faith is. She looked at her mother, and the jukebox song was playing in her head, faint, the song the world plays, and she let it play, and she said, "Where do we plant them?"

They planted them that evening, in the garden by the door, in the bed where the morning-glories from the women's meeting had been set, in the good black ground that had taken the rain and kept it. Cora knelt and made the row with her fingers, and June stood over her with the water jar, and Cora set the seeds in the ground, one and another and another, small brown seeds that had ridden west and east and never been in the ground, and she packed the earth over them and made the little basin, and June poured the water, slow, and the water took, and the two of them knelt in the garden in the long light and watched the ground where the seeds were.

"There," June said. "The faith that traveled. It's home, and it's in the ground."

Cora looked at the turned earth, at the little basin holding the water, and she thought of the road, and of Lula and Rosa and Miguel, and of the weigh scale and the boot and the tent, and of the song the world plays — and she thought of this, the ground, the seed, her mother's hands with the water jar — and she said, "It's in the ground." And the seed packet lay empty on the porch rail, and the last of the light went down across the garden, and under the dark turned earth the seeds that had traveled nine hundred miles and come home lay patient in the ground, and the first of them had already begun, somewhere down in the dark, to think about coming up.

Chapter 46: Miguel's Farewell

CORA — the farm — August 1936

The letter came in August, in the wad of mail the county mailman brought up the lane on Thursdays, and Cora knew the hand on the envelope before she opened it, the careful square letters of a man who had taught school and had taught himself to write so the children of the camps could read him. She carried it to the porch and sat on the steps with her back against the post and opened it, and she read it twice, and then she sat a long time with it in her hands, the way you sit with a thing you know you will keep.

He wrote from the Imperial Valley. The pea harvest had been good, and then the strike talk had come up again, and he had gone on from there — the way he always went on, the way the road went on, the way the organizers went on from one harvest to the next, one camp to the next, one fight to the next. He did not say the fight was going well, and he did not say it was going badly. He said the work was there and the work went on, and the growers' blacklist still carried his photograph, and he was still carrying it, like a man carrying his own wanted poster because it was the truest thing about him.

She read the letter aloud to herself, low, the way she read letters she meant to keep. He wrote about the camp where he was — the cotton had been short, the water had been dear, the arithmetic had been the same arithmetic he had taught her at the plank table, the grower's arithmetic that never came out in the worker's favor. He wrote that he had seen Rosa at a camp in the next county, and that Rosa was well, and that Rosa had asked after her. He wrote that Lula had made the crossing one more time, the way she had made it since 1929, and that Lula sent her love and said the roads were the same roads and the camps were the same camps and the people were the same people.

And then he wrote about her. It was the part she read three times, and then four. He wrote that he had known, when she left, that she was going home to stay — that a girl who could come home over the rise and see the house smaller than memory and stay anyway was a girl who had found her ground, and that he was glad of it, and that he would not have asked her to the valley with him because the valley was not where she belonged, and he had known it even when he said she could come or stay.

He wrote: I think of you in the ground there, in the good ground, and I think of the seed you carried. Plant it, Cora. It's been west and it's been east and it's time it was in the ground. I have the growers' file with my photograph in it, and I have the road, and the road is where I'm bound. That is my arithmetic. Yours is different, and it is not less. The arithmetic that is yours is the arithmetic of putting things in the ground and watching them come up, and the world needs that arithmetic as much as it needs mine, and I have never known which of us was braver, only that we are both doing what we are made to do.

She sat on the porch steps in the August heat with the letter in her hands, and the jukebox song played in her head, faint, the song the world plays, and she thought of the plank table at the camp and the pay stub and the way he had shown her on paper why they were always short, and she thought of the scar on his jaw, his mark, his history, the school he had taught in and been fired from, the sister who stayed, the hospital tent and the pencil. And she understood, sitting there, that he had released her — that the letter was his way of setting her down gently, the way you set a thing down that you have carried a long way because it is time, and that she had released him too, without knowing she had, when she had come over the rise and stayed.

She went inside and found the pencil. It was the pencil from the hospital tent, the one he had given her to keep her hand busy, the one she had carried home and kept, and she had kept it in the drawer by the ledger shelf, and now she took it out and held it in her hand, the worn wood, the blunt point, the tooth marks he had left in it. And she went to the ledger shelf and she set the pencil on it, beside the ledger itself, so that the pencil and the book sat together on the shelf between the canning jars, the book that kept the arithmetic of the farm and the pencil that had come from the arithmetic of the road, and she stood and looked at them.

Then she went back to the porch and sat down and read the letter once more, and she folded it carefully, the way you fold a thing you will keep, and she put it in the drawer with the pencil's place empty beside it, and she thought about what the letter had cost him to write, and what it had cost her to read, and she thought about the word that had followed her west and come home with her, the reckoning, and she understood that this was part of it too — the part where you let a thing go, not because it has stopped mattering, but because letting it go is what the arithmetic requires.

In the evening she walked out to the north quarter and stood at her row, the row of new trees she had planted, the cottonwoods and the willows and the Russian olives, and the extra tree for Ezra at the end, and she put her hand on the little cottonwood she had sworn to water, and she said, "He's on the road, and I'm in the ground, and the road and the ground are both where they belong." And the pencil sat on the ledger shelf in the dark house between the canning jars, and the letter lay folded in the drawer, and the last of the light went down across the row of new trees, and the wind came up and took the tops of them and moved them, and they stood, and the tree she had sworn to water stood with them.

Chapter 47: The Harvest of 1936

JUNE — the farm — August–September 1936

The wheat ripened in August, and June stood at the edge of the field on a Sunday morning and looked at it, and she could not find the words, and so she said nothing, which was the way she said the biggest things. The field stood full and golden to the fence, the heads heavy, the stalks tall, the whole north field one solid sheet of grain moving in the wind like water. She had planted it in May with the relief seed, and she had watched it come up green and thin, and she had watched it fill, and now it stood ready, and she walked into it and put her hand out and let the grain run through her fingers, and the tally in her head — the arithmetic, the prayer — came out in the black at last.

The harvest took the neighbors. It was the first year since 1931 that the harvest had been a shared thing, and they came up the lane in the last week of August — the men who had gone west and come home, and the men who had stayed, and the boys who had grown — with the binder and the threshing machine on wagons, and they set up in the field as if they had never been scattered, and the work began. Abel Halloran was among them. He drove the binder, and he worked the field from the first light to the last, and he did not look at June, and he did not avoid her, and when the threshing crew sat down to dinner at the long table under the cottonwoods, he sat at the far end and ate what was set before him and said the bread was good.

June cooked for them all. It was what the harvest demanded, and she did it the way she did everything, quiet and thorough and in the black — the bread, the beans, the meat the county agent's wife had sent over, the pies, the coffee that went down the line of men at noon. The house filled with the men's voices and the smell of cut wheat and the sound of the thresher, and Sammy ran errands between the field and the kitchen and was given a place at the table as if he were a man, and he sat very straight and did not say much, and June watched him and said nothing, and the harvest went on.

Cora worked the field with the men. She had learned the west's arithmetic, and she had learned the farm's, and she worked the shocks with a speed that made the neighbor men look twice, and Abel Halloran, forking the bundles into the thresher, watched her and said to no one in particular, "She's got her mother's hands." June heard it from the kitchen window, and she stood still a moment with the bread knife in her hand, and then she went on with the cutting.

The year's arithmetic came in. The grain went through the thresher and filled the sacks, and the sacks went up in the wagon, and the tally stood: more wheat than the farm had raised since 1931, more than the ledger had any right to hope for in May. June counted the sacks as they went into the barn, and she went into the house in the evening and opened the ledger and wrote the column in the black, and the column added, and she sat a long time looking at the page, at the numbers that had turned from red to black, from the years of dust to this, and she thought of the word that had followed her family west and come home, and she thought: the land keeps a reckoning, and this is its answer.

The last day of the threshing, the crew sat down to the long table under the cottonwoods in the long light, and the food went around, and the men talked about the wheat — how the head was full, how the straw stood, how the year had turned — and Abel Halloran sat at the end of the table, and June came out with the last pie and set it down, and there was a moment when the table went quiet, the way a table goes quiet when a thing that has been waiting is about to be said.

Abel Halloran stood up. He was not a tall man, and the years had not been kind to him, and he stood at the end of the long table and looked at June, and he said, "I cut your trees, June. I cut your windbreak down in the dark and I burned it to keep my children warm, and I have carried it every day since." He said it to the table, and then he said it to her. "I can't plant them back. The wood's gone. But I want to say it in front of the neighbors, in the light, where it's said true. I cut your trees, and I'm sorry, and the sorry doesn't bring them back, but it's true, and it's said."

June stood at the end of the table with the pie in her hands. The whole table watched her — the neighbor men, their wives, Sammy, Cora, Abel Halloran standing in the long light. And June said, "The windbreak's replanted. It's in the ground and it's growing. The sorry's a seed too, Abel. It roots." She set the pie down. "Sit and eat. The threshing's done and the wheat's in the barn, and that's the year's answer."

Abel Halloran sat down. The table went back to its eating and its talk, and June went around the table with the coffee pot, and when she came to Abel's place she poured his coffee herself, and he looked up at her, and neither of them said anything, and the coffee went down the line.

In the evening, when the crew had gone and the field lay stubble and the barn stood full of sacks, June walked out into the field alone. The light had gone long and gold, and the stubble ran to the fence, and the birds were loud in the windbreak, and she stood in the middle of the cut field, in the stubble, in the gold light, with the tally in her head, and the year's arithmetic came out in the black, and she knelt and put her hand flat on the stubble, on the dry gold stalks that had held the grain, and she said, quietly, to the field, "There it is. The answer." And the wheat was in the barn in the seed sacks, and the ledger was open on the table with the column in the black, and the field lay stubble in the long light, and the wind came up and moved through the stubble with a sound like grain in a hopper, and the harvest of 1936 was in.

Chapter 48: The Reckoning Named

JUNE — the farm — September 1936

It was a September evening, the first cool evening of the fall, and the three of them sat on the porch after supper — June in her chair, Cora on the steps, Sammy on the rail with his legs hanging — and the light went down across the fields, the stubble gold, the new windbreak green at the north, the corn in the garden by the door. They had been quiet a long time, the way they were quiet now, in the good year, as if they were still learning to believe it would hold.

"Sammy," June said, "the preacher's revival. You went with the Halloran boys."

"Yes'm."

"What did he preach on?"

Sammy was quiet a moment. "The reckoning," he said. "He said the dust was the Lord's reckoning, that we sowed and the land answered, and the land answered in dust because we had been dust in our hearts, and it was a punishment, and we'd been brought low for our sins, and now that we were brought low we could be lifted up."

June nodded slowly. The light had gone from gold to gray, and the first star had come out over the river, and she looked at the word that had followed her family for a year and a half — the preacher's word, the grower's word, the word twisted into a threat in the dark, the word her daughter had heard under a boot — and she said, "The preacher's wrong."

Sammy looked at her. Cora looked at her. June sat in her chair with her hands folded in her lap, the way she sat when she had come to the end of a column and was ready to draw the line under it.

"The preacher's wrong," she said again. "The dust wasn't a punishment. The grower said we needed to be taught to reckon — that the reckoning was the strong man's arithmetic, the arithmetic that comes out in the strong man's favor. The preacher says it's the Lord's arithmetic, the arithmetic that comes out in the Lord's favor and never mind the rest. They're the same arithmetic. Somebody above you, holding the scale, weighing you out." She looked at her daughter. "You know the scale, Cora. You stood in front of it in the valley."

Cora nodded. "The weighmaster's thumb," she said. "The tare. The wet weight."

"That's the scale they mean when they say reckoning. A scale that weighs somebody else's thumb. And the ledger taught me different." She looked at the house, where the ledger lay open on the table in the lamplight, and she said, "The reckoning isn't punishment, and it isn't a scale held over your head. The reckoning is the sum. It's what the years add up to when you write them down — every seed bought and saved and planted and lost, every weather column, every life. It's not the Lord weighing you. It's not the grower weighing you. It's the sum of what you chose, and it doesn't have to come out in the black to be true, and it doesn't have to be a punishment to be an answer."

She was quiet a moment, and then she said the thing she had been holding for a year and a half, the word she had heard the preacher preach and the grower threaten and her own heart whisper, the word she had been afraid to speak in her own voice. "That's what I think the reckoning is. The sum of the choices. And the sum of ours was the dust and the flood and the green coming back, and it was hard, and it was true, and it's ours."

The three of them sat on the porch in the gray light, and the first star held over the river, and Cora said, "Ezra."

June was quiet. The name went out of her into the dark like a stone into water, and the ripples went out and came back. "Ezra," she said. "He's in the ledger. He's the extra tree. He's the reason I wouldn't sell, wouldn't leave, wouldn't let your father sell — and I drove him west over a grave, and I wrote it in the columns for years without writing his name, and I thought if I kept the land I kept him, and I was half right, and half wrong." She looked at her hands. "The ledger was my faith, and it was my fault. I loved the land more than I loved the people on it, and I set that down in the columns where I could see it, and I have been a long time understanding what the columns were telling me."

Cora was quiet. Then she said, "I carried a seed packet nine hundred miles because you gave it to me. You gave it to me and I never planted it, and I kept it through the camps and the strike and the beating and the hospital, and I brought it home, and we planted it in July, and it's up." She looked at her mother. "The seed was the faith. The faith that traveled. You planted that in me, Mama, and it came up."

Sammy slid off the rail and came and stood by June's chair, and he said, "Dad's coming home."

June looked at him. "He said he would."

"He said he would in the letter. He'll come. He's got the address now. We wrote it right this time, on the envelope, the whole thing, Boise City, Cimarron County." He said it carefully, the way a boy says an address he has learned because it matters. "The mail can't lose it. I put the stamp on square."

June put her hand on his head, the way she had once done to see if he was feverish. "The stamp's square," she said. "He'll come."

The light had gone almost out of the sky, and the stars had come up in numbers, and the three of them sat on the porch in the dark, and the word had been spoken in their own voice at last — not the preacher's, not the grower's — and it had not killed them, and it had not weighed them out, and it had set them down the way a sum sets you down, plain and true and yours. And June thought of the ledger open on the table, and of all the columns, and of the sum of them, and she thought: the account is settled. Not forgiven, not paid — settled. And it comes out in the black because it's true, not because it's owed.

In the morning she went out to the north quarter before the light was full and stood at the new windbreak, at the extra tree for Ezra, and she put her hand on the new bark, and she said, "I think the reckoning is this, son: we're still here, and we're together, and the land is willing." And the tree stood in the gray morning, and the wind came up and moved its top, and it did not break, and June stood with her hand on the tree that had been planted for her lost boy, and the sum of the years stood around her in the green rows, holding the wind.

Chapter 49: The Letter to Walter

CORA — the farm — October 1936

They wrote the letter on a Sunday evening in October, the whole family at the kitchen table, the way they did everything now, with the window up and the lamp lit and the ledger open on the shelf behind them like a third presence in the room. Cora had the paper and the pen, and June had her hands folded in her lap, and Sammy had the stamp he had bought with his own nickel and was already licking the corner of the envelope, and Cora looked at the blank page and the blank page looked back at her, and she understood, sitting there, that the letter had to be true, and that the truth had to be written down in the way the family had learned to write it — in objects, in columns, in weather.

"Start with the harvest," June said. "He'll want to know the harvest."

Cora wrote: Walter — the harvest is in. The wheat made more than we've raised since '31. The threshing crew was the neighbors come home, Abel Halloran among them, and Abel said his piece in the light, and I want you to know that. The grain's in the barn in the seed sacks, and we've kept back the seed for spring, and there's a column in the ledger in the black for the first year since you left.

She read it back, and June nodded, and Cora wrote on.

The windbreak's replanted. The county agent brought the shelterbelt cottonwoods in June, the whole program's planting itself against the wind, and we put in the new rows on the north quarter where the old ones were cut. The old trees that lived through the axe and the flood are still standing, and the new ones are up to your hip, and there's a new tree for Ezra planted beside the old one, and I water it every day. It's the one I carry water to. The lilac's rooted by the door, and the morning-glories from the women's meeting came up like a miracle, and the garden's in, and the corn's in, and the land is willing, Walter. The land is willing again.

She wrote the flood. It was the part she did not want to write, and the part she had to write, because the truth of the year had the flood in it, and a letter that left out the flood would be a letter that left out the reckoning. She wrote: In April the river came up and covered the pasture and took the barn, and the chickens floated past the porch, and the house stood — the house held, Walter. Mama stayed with the ledger in the oilcloth at her breast and the seed jars on the high shelf, and I stayed with her, and the water climbed the porch and then it turned, and the house held. The seed corn came through in the jars, and the ledger came through dry in the oilcloth, and the flood washed the dust away, and it was the earth's own reckoning with the years, and we came through it, all of us, and the house held.

She read that back, and June was quiet a long time, and then June said, "Tell him about Sammy."

Cora wrote: Sammy's grown. He buried his gun in the shed loft after the flood, of his own accord, and he works the rows and he went to the river and he's old for his years in the way we all are. He caught the fever in February and he lived, Walter. He took what Ezra took and he lived. Mama carried him through the night and the doctor came through the sand, and the fever turned at dawn, and Sammy lived, and there's a column in the ledger that says so.

She wrote about June. It was the hardest part, and she wrote it plain. Mama's well. The dust pneumonia took her in December and she nearly went the way Ezra went, and I was in the west and I didn't know, and the letters were stopped, and it was the worst of the year, and she lived. She taught the district school in the winter when the teacher quit for California, eight children in dust masks at the stove, and she kept the ledger through all of it, and the ledger is the story, Walter. The whole story is in the ledger — every seed, every weather column, every life — and there's a page that says the land is willing again, and there's a page that says Sammy lived, and there's a page for the flood, and there's a page for the harvest, and the pages add up, and the sum of them is this.

She wrote the last line last. She had known it was coming, the whole letter, the whole year, the whole year and a half since her mother had stood on the porch in the blowing dust and said the words, and she wrote it in her own hand, plain, in the lamplight, the words that had followed her west and come home with her and been planted in the garden by the door:

Come home, Walter. The land keeps a reckoning, and it kept us. Come home.

She set the pen down. The kitchen was quiet, and the lamp was lit, and the page was full, and Sammy had the stamp licked and square on the corner of the envelope, and June took the letter and read it through, and her eyes went over the words slowly, the way she read the columns of the ledger, and she held it a long moment, and then she folded it — the way you fold a thing you will send — and she put it in the envelope, and she sealed it, and she handed it to Cora.

"Post it Monday," she said. "The mail's a slow river. It'll get there."

Cora took the envelope and turned it over in her hands, the address written square and whole on the front — Walter Vogel, General Delivery, Imperial Valley, California — and she thought of the letters that had gone astray in the storms, and the letters that had stopped, and the vigil at the mailbox in the winter, and she thought of the letter lying in the ledger, his, the one he had sent out of the flood mail, the one that had said he was ashamed and would come home if they would have him, and she thought: this is the answer. This is the root going out, and this time it will reach him.

Monday morning she rode to Boise City with the mailman and walked into the post office, and she stood at the window where she had once stood as a girl, and she gave the letter to Mr. Ketchum, who had been the postmaster of Cimarron County since before the dust, and he took it and weighed it and stamped it and dropped it in the sack, and Cora watched it go down into the sack with the other letters, the whole county's words going out along the roads, and she stood at the window a moment and thought of the word that had followed her west and come home, and of the letter's last line riding in the sack, and she said, to the empty window, "It's in the mail." And the letter lay in the sack with the other letters, and the mailman took the sack and went out to the truck, and the truck took the road out of Boise City, and the letter rode in the sack toward the west, toward the river of the road, toward a man in the Imperial Valley pea fields who carried his shame like a root not yet planted, and the waiting began.

Chapter 50: Home

JUNE — the farm — November 1936

The truck came up the lane on a grey day in November, the first day of the year when the air had that cold clean bite to it that says winter is coming on, and June saw it from the kitchen window where she was setting the bread to rise, and her hands went still in the flour. She stood a long moment looking at it, the dark truck moving slow up the lane between the cottonwoods, the new windbreak running green at the north, and she did not call out, and she did not move, because there are some things a woman does not trust until she has seen them all the way to the end.

The truck stopped at the yard gate. The door opened, and a man got out, and he stood a moment by the truck looking at the house — a man in a worn coat, thinner than she remembered, older, with the west in his face the way the west had been in her daughter's face when she came over the rise — and he took off his hat, and he stood there in the grey light, and he said, "June."

She went out onto the porch. The bread flour was still on her hands, and she wiped them down her apron, and she stood at the top of the steps looking at him, and the years went between them — the dust, the grave, the leaving, the letters that thinned to nothing, the shame, the flood, the harvest — and she said, "Walter."

He came up the lane to the porch steps, and he stopped at the bottom of them, and he looked at her, and he looked past her at the house, and he said, "I got the letter."

"The letter got there."

"It got there. The mailman in the valley had it for me. He said it had come through the flood mail and the storm mail, and it got there, and I read it, and I read the last line." He was quiet a moment. "The land keeps a reckoning, and it kept you. That's what it said. I read it in the bunkhouse and I read it again, and I packed my bag, and I come home."

June stood at the top of the steps. The wind came up across the yard, and it was cold, and it carried nothing — no grit, no sting — and she said, "The bread's rising. There'll be room at the table."

Walter Vogel came up the steps, and he stopped on the porch, and he and June stood facing each other in the grey light, a foot of porch boards between them, and he said, "I was ashamed, June. The letters went astray and I let them, and I let the shame grow where the letters should have been, and I was a long way off, and I was wrong, and I'm here to say it plain."

"Say it then," June said.

He said it. He stood on the porch of the house on the Cimarron flood plain, in the grey November light, with his hat in his hands, and he said the words plain, and June listened, and when he was done she said, "There's water on the stove. Wash up. The boy's down at the river."

Sammy came up the lane at noon with a string of fish, and he saw the truck, and he stopped, and the string of fish went slack in his hand, and Walter came off the porch, and the boy and the man stood looking at each other across the yard, and Sammy said, "Dad?" and Walter said, "Sammy," and the two of them stood in the yard and then they were not standing, and June watched from the porch, and Cora came out of the house and stood beside her, and they watched the man and the boy in the yard.

At the table that evening the four of them sat down — June at her place, Walter at his, Cora and Sammy where they had always sat — and the place that had been empty since May of 1935 was full, and the table was the table, and the bread was the bread, and Walter looked at the empty chair at the end of the table, the chair that had been Ezra's, and he was quiet a long time, and no one hurried him, and then he said, "I'm sorry about the grave, June. I'm sorry I couldn't stay by it. I'm sorry I wanted to sell out and go together and I'm sorry I went alone." And June said, "You're home. The rest is columns." And they ate.

After supper they walked the land, the four of them, in the grey evening, the way June had walked it every evening through the long years, and they went down to the north quarter and stood at the new windbreak, and Walter put his hand on the new cottonwoods, on the row of them running green in the grey light, and he said, "The old ones are still standing," and June said, "The old ones stood, and the new ones are up, and there's a new tree for Ezra beside the old one, planted in June, and Cora carries the water to it." And Walter looked at his daughter, and Cora looked at him, and neither of them said anything, and the wind came up and took the tops of the trees and moved them, and the row stood.

They walked the field, the stubble running to the fence in the grey light, and Walter knelt and put his hand flat on the stubble the way June had done after the harvest, and he said, "The wheat made," and June said, "The wheat made. It's in the barn in the seed sacks, and we've kept back the seed for spring." And they walked the pasture where the flood had stood, and Walter said, "The river took the barn," and June said, "The river took the barn and the chickens floated past the porch, and the house held, Walter. The house held."

They walked to the grave. It was the last place, and they walked to it slow, the four of them, down the fence line where the grass had come back, and they stood at the marker — the marker the dust had buried and the flood had uncovered, the one that stood now in the green grass — and no one spoke, and the wind moved over them, and June put her hand on the marker, and Walter put his hand on hers, and they stood that way a long time, the man and the woman and the two children, at the grave of the boy who had died in the dust, in the green grass the rain had brought.

Back at the house they sat in the kitchen with the lamp lit, and the ledger lay on the shelf between the canning jars, where it had lain through the dust and the flood and the harvest, and June took it down and opened it, and she turned to the back page, the page with the flood line on it, the page that held the columns of the good year, and she took up the pen, and the family watched her, and she wrote, in her plain hand, the year's last entry:

1936 — we are here.

She set the pen down, and she closed the ledger, and she put it back on the shelf between the canning jars, the jars of the year's canning standing in a row in the lamplight, and she said, "It's on the shelf between the canning jars, and it will be there in the morning, and it will be there for the years, and the sum of it is we are here." And the family sat in the lamplight, the four of them, the table the table, the house the house that had held, and outside the grey evening had gone to dark, and the wind came up over the new grass, and the windbreak held the wind the way it was meant to hold it, and under the rain that had come and the rain that was to come the dust lay under the ground where it could not hurt anything, and the house stood on the flood plain with its windows lit, and in the morning light of the first day of the rest of the year the family's shadows would lie long and dark on the new grass, and the windbreak would hold the wind, and the ledger would lie on the shelf between the canning jars, and they would be here, and the year's last entry would be true.
